# Sources: Personal Safety

<!-- unread: Ferraro, Hale, Stanko, Pain (1995, Farrall and Gadd, Hindelang, Karmen, Doerner, Crime Prevention: Approaches, Felson and Eckert, Walklate, Daigle, RoSPA, NISVS 2023/2024 Data Brief, Collins (2016, Pew, Attracting Assault full text, Changing kinematics, Sakaguchi, Endsley, Guerette and Bowers, Nee, Meenaghan, Burglars on the Job, Wright, Understanding Decisions to Burglarize full report, Lee dissertation, Welsh Farrington lighting full review, Gift of Fear, de Becker, Cooper, Torgerson, Vera-Gray full text, Right Amount of Panic, StreetSafe, safety-plan.thehotline.org, Danger Assessment, ncpc Street Smarts, psychosis-induced aggression, Zimring, Zuehl, Robbery Violence, noncommercial robbery, DeLone, Armed Robbers in Action, A comprehensive approach to sexual violence prevention, Evaluation outcomes of self-defense training, Empowerment Self-Defense Prevents Rape, Is Not About Empowerment, On Combat, Siddle, Germain Impact Solutions, Does Danger Level, Facing Violence, Meditations on Violence, SARE trial protocol, Walking the Tightrope, Resources for Action, Palmer v R judgment, McInnes, R v Ray judgment, Collins judgment, Cheeseman, Doherty judgment, Lieser judgment, Owens, Burns v HM Advocate, Evans v Hughes, Flack v Baldry, Firearms (Northern Ireland) Order 2004, www.police.uk, FCC text-to-911, Cook and Ludwig, McDowall, Kleck 2021 CDC surveys, Kellermann, Humphreys, Yakubovich, Giffords and SPLC report, Everytown shoot first report, Heller opinion, armes-ufa, NACVCB, Stand Your Ground Kills -->

*Stage 1 research, 2026-09-25. Written before any lesson is drafted, by four researchers working
in parallel, each in a fresh context, each on one part of the subject (Parts A to D below), and
brought together here. Part A: risk, and reading the crime data. Part B: awareness and avoidance.
Part C: de-escalation, confrontation and resistance. Part D: the law, weapons, and afterwards. For
each source the parts say what it is good for, what it establishes, and exactly how much of it was
read. All reading was done on 2026-09-25 unless a capture date is given.*

**This is a sensitive domain several times over** (standards 3.4: crime, gender and family, race,
law, and a live US political argument about guns). Every lesson gets the neutrality audit and the
five-reviewer Stage 4. Every legal statement is general information, not legal advice, and the
lesson must say so. Readers will include people who have been robbed, assaulted, raped or abused,
and people who are in danger now; the handling notes in Parts B and C (numbers first, content notes
before the section, never "you should have") are not optional.

**How to read the quotation marks.** All four parts use the same core convention, with a few
local variants:

- **[V]**: copied from text the researcher extracted directly: curl with an HTML stripper,
  pdftotext on the publisher's PDF, the GOV.UK content API, the PubMed E-utilities API, the
  Crossref API (abstracts as the publisher deposited them), or an Internet Archive capture (ID
  given). Part D also counts the in-app browser's page text for four pages that block curl
  (nysenate.gov twice, statutes.capitol.texas.gov, 911.gov).
- **[V, two-column]** (Part B): read through pdftotext from a two-column PDF where the column
  interleaving was undone by eye. Re-read against the PDF before a lesson quotes it.
- **[V, via B7]**, **[V, via B8]**, **[V, secondary]** (Part B): a verbatim quotation of one work
  as it appears inside another that was read. Cite the work that was read, not the one it quotes.
- **[V, table]**, **[V, from the table]**, **[V, headings]**: copied from a table or a list of
  headings rather than running text.
- **[T]** (Part A only, two lines): returned by the WebFetch summarising tool. Both are publisher
  tables of contents, and chapter titles are not quoted in lessons.
- **Snippet only**: seen only in a search result. Nothing so marked is in quotation marks, and
  nothing so marked may be stated in a lesson as a finding.
- **Abstract only** (Parts B, C, D): the lesson may report what the abstract says and nothing
  more, and may not describe methods or caveats the abstract does not state.

**Access and provenance problems a drafter inherits.** Each is described in its part; they are
collected here because together they decide which sources a lesson can lean on.

1. **CPS self-defence guidance is gone from the live site** (Part D §1). "Self-Defence and the
   Prevention of Crime" returns 404 at both the old and new CPS paths and the site search finds no
   replacement. It was read from **Internet Archive capture 20240723174716** (marked updated 30
   September 2019 and 9 September 2022). Cite it as "CPS legal guidance, as archived July 2024", or
   look once more in a browser. Its "Retreating" paragraph cites s.76(6) where the statute says
   **s.76(6A)**; cite the statute for that point. The CPS/NPCC householder leaflet is live as a PDF
   though its HTML landing page also 404s.
2. **The CPS rape myths annex has moved** (Part C §1). Its URL returns 404; it was read from
   **capture 20260421173121**. Find the current CPS location before citing it. (Closed at lesson
   11, 2026-09-25: it is Annex A of the live full-page guidance; see "Gate closures, lesson 11".)
3. **The Model Penal Code was read from a teaching copy** (Part D §1) hosted by Markus Dubber at
   the University of Toronto, because the American Law Institute does not post the text. Check any
   quoted clause against a second copy.
4. **Third-party copies of published papers.** None was a workaround of a paywall or a block, but
   the provenance is not the publisher's, and a lesson should check a quoted line against the
   publisher's page or say where it was read: **Engel, McManus and Herold 2020** (Part C) and
   **Piza and colleagues 2019** (Part B), both from prohic.nl; **English's 2021 National Firearms
   Survey** from armes-ufa.com (Part D; the host is on the unread line, so re-read the abstract on
   SSRN, ID 3887145, before quoting); **Gunns and colleagues 2002** from a University of Rijeka
   course page; **Hyman and colleagues 2010** from spryfeet.com; **Cohen and Felson 1979** from a
   JSTOR-watermarked copy on a University of Washington course page (all Part B).
5. **Accepted manuscripts, not versions of record** (Parts A and B): Gray, Jackson and Farrall 2008;
   Lee, Jackson and Ellis 2020; Jackson and Gray 2010; Jackson and Stafford 2009, all from LSE
   Research Online. Check wording against the journal before quoting.
6. **The CDC's STOP SV technical package has been withdrawn from cdc.gov** (Part C §1). The 2016
   package was read from a 2021 Internet Archive capture; the current CDC page points to "Resources
   for Action" (unread). A lesson must call it "the CDC's 2016 technical package", not current CDC
   guidance.
7. **cdc.gov and nhtsa.gov returned "Access Denied"** (Part A). The CDC FastStats pages and the
   NHTSA 2025 release were read from Wayback captures; the CDC pages label their data years
   inconsistently (2024 on one, 2023 on another). Check the year on the live page.
8. **The NCVS changed instruments in 2024** (Part A). *Criminal Victimization, 2024* reports only
   the legacy half of the sample, so 2024 estimates are less precise than usual. (Stage 4
   correction, lesson 02: BJS says the split sample keeps year-to-year comparisons possible; it
   does not say the new figures will not be comparable.) A jump in a 2025 or later figure may still
   be the instrument, not the world: check which instrument, and whether BJS calls it significant.
9. **CSEW comparability** (Part A). The regulator suspended the survey's accreditation in July 2022
   and reinstated it in 2024; the years ending March 2023 and March 2024 are "not designated as
   accredited official statistics". The sexual victimisation questions are new and **not
   comparable** with earlier years; anti-social behaviour was inflated by a question change from
   April 2025. Police recorded crime lost its accreditation in January 2014 and has not regained
   it. The ONS March 2026 bulletin gives the Report Fraud launch as 4 December 2025 in one place and
   "4 December 2026" in another; the second is a typo.
10. **England and Wales is not the UK** (Parts A and D). ONS crime and homicide figures cover
    England and Wales only; road deaths are Great Britain; the 2022 ONS perceptions survey (Part B)
    is Great Britain. In law, s.76 extends to Northern Ireland but its householder and retreat
    subsections are England and Wales only; Scotland has its own common law with different rules
    (Part D §3); Northern Ireland's firearms and knife law was not read. Victim Support covers
    England and Wales; Criminal Injuries Compensation covers England, Wales and Scotland. **The
    course description says "the UK"; the lessons must say "England and Wales" and "Scotland"
    precisely**, and mention Northern Ireland only as "different again" unless someone reads its
    sources.
11. **Sites that block automated readers**, read from Internet Archive captures with IDs in the
    parts: the Metropolitan Police and Right To Be (Part C); the State Department and Take Five
    (Part B); the CDC and NHTSA (Part A).
    **Not read at all**: police.uk and fcc.gov (Cloudflare), Victim Support Scotland (connection
    failed), police.uk StreetSafe (script-rendered), the Hotline's interactive safety planner (its
    old URL now 404s). GOV.UK "Contact the police" covers 999 and 101 instead of police.uk.
12. **Foundational studies that could not be read.** Grayson and Stein 1981 (the gait study
    everybody cites) was read only as the ERIC abstract and through two later works that quote it;
    its sample sizes circulate in unreliable forms and must not be given. Zimring and Zuehl 1986 is
    an image-only scan; Kleck and DeLone 1993 and Cook's robbery papers are walled. The compliance
    side of the robbery question therefore rests on Block and Skogan's NCJRS abstract, Faulkner
    2001, Smith 2003 and police advice (Part C §3).
13. **Case law was read only through official summaries** (Part D §1): the English cases through
    the CPS guidance, the Scottish ones through the Judicial Institute's Jury Manual and Leverick's
    note on *Lieser*. Quote a judge only "as quoted by the CPS" or "as quoted in the Jury Manual".
14. **Two very recent items** (Part C): Liebst and colleagues' "would I be hurt" study was read as
    a 2020 PsyArXiv preprint abstract; Lindegaard and colleagues' commercial robbery study was
    published online on 2026-09-15. Check both for a published version at drafting.
15. **Textbooks were read as tables of contents only** (Part A §8), and are on the unread line.
16. **Everything with a date on it moves.** Every phone number and opening time was read on
    2026-09-25 and must be rechecked at drafting. The UK fraud reporting service formerly called
    Action Fraud now appears as Report Fraud at reportfraud.police.uk. legislation.gov.uk shows
    amendments up to 2026; the CPS knife guidance was updated in May 2026; Florida's statutes page
    is the 2026 edition; New York's is the 2024-09-06 revision. Quote statutes from the live page
    at drafting, not from this file.

**The `unread:` line is the build's enforcement.** It matches each entry as a whole word or
phrase, case-sensitively, anywhere in a lesson file, and a published course fails the build on a
match. It combines the four parts' lines, deduplicated, with these changes so that no work that
**was** read is blocked by a bare surname another part used for an unread one:

- **Senn** (Part A) is **off the line**. Parts B and C read Senn and colleagues' NEJM 2015 abstract,
  and Part C read the 2017 two-year outcomes paper in full. Part A never discusses Senn.
- **Clarke** and **Cohen and Felson** (Part A) are **off the line**. Part B read the Introduction to
  Clarke's *Situational Crime Prevention* (1997) and the POP Center's 25 techniques grid, and read
  Cohen and Felson 1979 in full. The unread Felson book stays as **Felson and Eckert**.
- **Guerette** (Part B) became **Guerette and Bowers**, because Part C read Guerette and Santana's
  abstract.
- **Decker** (Part B) is **off the line**, because Part C read Decker and colleagues' Malawi trial
  abstract (a different Decker). Wright and Decker's *Burglars on the Job* stays blocked by
  **Wright** and **Burglars on the Job**.
- **Collins** (Part A, the 2016 meta-analysis) became **Collins (2016**, so that it does not collide
  with Part D's **Collins judgment** (the householder case, which a lesson may mention as the CPS
  summarises it).
- **Pain** (Part A) became **Pain (1995**, and **Lab** became **Crime Prevention: Approaches** (the
  title of Lab's unread textbook): as bare capitalised words they would fire on any sentence that
  began "Pain ..." or "Lab studies ...".
- **police.uk** (Part D) became **www.police.uk**, because the bare string also matches
  reportfraud.police.uk, which Part B read from the Take Five page and which a lesson will print. A
  lesson that names the police.uk crime maps or StreetSafe without "www." slips through; the
  fact-check must catch it.
- **Tark and Kleck** (Part D) is **off the line**. Part C read both Tark and Kleck abstracts (2004
  robbery and assault, 2014 rape) through the Crossref API; Part D meant that nobody read the full
  papers. **A lesson may quote those abstracts and nothing more.** That limit, like every
  "abstract only" limit in this file, is enforced by the fact-check, not the build. Kleck and Gertz
  1995 and its reply were read in full (Part D), so **Kleck** is never on the line bare; the unread
  Kleck work is **Kleck 2021 CDC surveys**, and Kleck and DeLone is blocked by **DeLone**.

Some entries block a phrase that a lesson would rarely write (**Attracting Assault full text**,
**Welsh Farrington lighting full review**, **Lee dissertation**, **Palmer v R judgment**, **R v Ray
judgment**). The researchers chose them deliberately, because the abstract, the summary or the CPS
quotation *was* read and a lesson may cite that; the entry records that the full work was not. A
drafter who cites Grayson and Stein, Welsh and Farrington, Lee, Palmer or Ray must cite what was
read and no more.

Two entries block a name a lesson will want. **Stanko** stays on the line because Part A read her
argument only through Scott (2003); the "undercount" position in the fear paradox (contested
question 1) is attributed to Scott's summary until someone reads Stanko. **Wright** blocks Wright
and Decker's *Burglars on the Job*, which Part B saw only as quoted in a Rutgers press release; the
pro-alarm side of contested question 5 is cited to the UNC Charlotte release and the Lee release.

**Boundaries with other Core courses, settled here so no lesson re-opens them.**

- **First Aid and CPR** (T3, published, just before this course). **Lesson 1** ("You are the first
  responder") already teaches the bystander effect, the Kitty Genovese correction, Fischer 2011,
  Philpot 2020 (at least one bystander intervened in 90.9% of filmed public conflicts) and Good
  Samaritan law. This course builds on it and does not reteach it. **Lesson 16** ("After the
  emergency, and staying ready") already teaches support lines, 988, Samaritans 116 123, what to do
  for someone in crisis, and carries the suicide-crisis footer. This course links to it and does
  not repeat it. Anything medical (an injured person after an assault) is First Aid's.
- **Mental Fitness** (T2, published). **Lesson 7** ("Resilience, a ratio and a mindset") teaches
  what the evidence says about reactions after a potentially traumatic event (the four
  trajectories). The aftermath lesson points there.
- **Digital Literacy** (T1, published). **Lesson 10** ("Passwords, and the ladder above them") and
  **lesson 11** ("Recognising a con, and the ten minutes after you clicked") own passwords,
  multi-factor authentication and phishing. This course keeps only where fraud meets the person
  (pressure scams by phone and at the door, meeting people from online, trackers and stalkerware,
  reporting) and links there. **Cybersecurity for Everyone** (T6, planned) goes further.
- **Personal Finance Fundamentals** (T2, published). **Lesson 10** ("Defending it",
  `10-scams-and-fraud`) already teaches the three things nearly every scam has to do, pressure and
  manufactured deadlines, checking by a route you chose, and the payment rail as the gate. None of
  the four parts found it; Stage 2 should read it before planning the fraud section here, which
  must link it rather than reteach it and keep only the doorstep and the bogus caller, which are
  this course's.
- **Home Repair and Maintenance** (T3, planned, just after this course). This course owns the
  evidence on what deters burglary (WIDE, alarms); fitting a lock is Home Repair's.
- **Conflict Resolution** (T4) and **Negotiation** (T17), planned. De-escalation here is about a
  threat of violence. Ordinary disagreement is theirs.
- **Relationships and Family** (T4, planned) owns relationships. This course owns safety planning
  when a relationship is abusive.
- **Introduction to Law** (T12, planned) owns criminal law in general. This course teaches only
  the law of self-defence, weapons carried for protection, and a victim's rights.
- **Travel Skills** and **Emergency Preparedness** (T24, planned) own travel and disasters in
  general. This course keeps the personal-safety part of travel.
- **Children**: a short household section on talking to children (NCMEC's scripts, Part B B23).
  Child protection is not this course.

## The first things Stage 1 found, and they shape the course

1. **The harm people fear most is not the harm they are most likely to meet.** Fraud is nearly
   half of all crime the England and Wales survey counts (4.5 million of 9.6 million incidents, YE
   March 2026), violence about a tenth, and most violence causes minor or no injury (42% of violent
   incidents caused any injury; broken bones 2%). Violence in England and Wales is 78% below its
   1995 peak and homicide is at its lowest recorded rate. In the US, falls and roads kill more
   people than homicide does. Stereotypical child abduction is about a hundred cases a year in the
   US. Yet majorities in both countries believe crime is rising in most years. (Part A §1 to §4,
   Part B B22 and B23.)
2. **"Who harms whom" has no single answer; it depends on the crime and on the victim's sex.**
   Women who are killed are most often killed by a partner or ex-partner (40% of adult female
   homicide victims in England and Wales, YE March 2025); men by a friend, acquaintance or stranger.
   The largest share of rape of women is by a partner or ex-partner (44.5% of female victims since
   16), and the commonest places are the victim's own home (38.2% of all victims) and the
   perpetrator's (28.5%); 8.0% happened in a street, car park, park or other open
   public space. Stranger violence falls mostly on young men. Robbery is the stranger crime. A
   lesson that tells everyone "most violence is by people you know" is wrong for men; one that
   tells women "stranger attacks are rare" is right on the numbers and must still not dismiss
   street harassment, which is common. (Part A §1, §7.2, §12.4; Part B B20, B21.)
3. **Much popular safety advice is sensible and untested.** "Plan your route, tell someone, stay
   where there are people and light" is reasonable inference from routine activity theory, with
   area-level evidence for lighting (21% fewer crimes in 13 studies) and modest evidence for CCTV,
   but no trial of the advice itself. "Walk confidently and you will not be picked" overstates a
   literature that measures how raters judge walkers, not who is later attacked. The course should
   say which advice is which (Part B §2, §4).
4. **The strongest evidence in the course is about the home.** Window locks, indoor lights on a
   timer, double or deadlocks, and external lights on a sensor together (WIDE) are associated with
   almost fifty times lower risk of burglary with entry than no security, in England and Wales
   survey data (a comparison between households, not a trial). Burglar alarms are genuinely
   contested. Unlocked doors and windows remain a large share of entries. (Part B §3.)
5. **Where the risk is greatest the research on "avoidance" says least.** Domestic abuse and
   stalking are where most serious harm to women comes from, and they are underestimated by the
   surveys (ONS says the interviewer questions undercount domestic abuse; the National Research
   Council says the NCVS cannot measure rape precisely). Separation from a controlling partner is
   the most dangerous time (Campbell 2003: adjusted OR 8.98 for a highly controlling abuser combined with separation after
   living together). A
   course that spends three lessons on streets and a paragraph on the home repeats the misweighting
   the evidence corrects. (Parts A, B.)
6. **De-escalation is recommended by every official body and has trial evidence only in policing.**
   ICAT reduced police use of force in a stepped-wedge randomised trial in Louisville (use of force
   down 28.1%) and a replication in Indianapolis, with attitude gains that faded. In health
   settings a Cochrane review found one eligible study. The specific techniques (space, angle,
   hands visible, one voice, short sentences, a face-saving exit) are expert consensus. Whether a
   member of the public who reads a page de-escalates better is the course's inference and must be
   labelled so. (Part C §2.)
7. **The comply-or-resist question has a working consensus only on the narrow case.** When a
   weapon is shown and all that is wanted is property, every official body read says hand it over,
   and the pro-resistance data do not contradict that. When the attacker wants the person, the
   evidence (Tark and Kleck's abstracts, Ullman's review) points toward resistance, and forceful
   and non-forceful resistance to rape reduces completion without significantly raising other
   injury. Freezing is involuntary and common (70% significant tonic immobility in one Stockholm
   sample), and the law in England and Wales requires no resistance. (Part C §3, §4.)
8. **One randomised trial shows a self-defence programme preventing rape**, and it is aimed at
   acquaintances, not strangers: Senn and colleagues' EAAA, 5.2% against 9.8% completed rape at one
   year, with a wide confidence interval and an effect that faded in the second year. One
   independent trial in Nairobi found no effect and is itself disputed. There is **no controlled
   evidence on self-defence classes for men, or for general assault or robbery**. (Part C §4, §7.)
9. **Escape comes first in both countries' law in practice, though not in the same way.** England
   and Wales treat a chance to retreat as a factor; Scotland requires escape where it is safe and
   reasonable; New York requires retreat before deadly force outside the home; Florida and Texas
   remove the duty, and even NRA Women's page recommends leaving if you can. Carrying anything "for
   self-defence" is itself an offence in England and Wales, and pepper spray is a prohibited
   firearm in Great Britain; in the US a spray is lawful to carry and its use is judged like any
   other force. (Part D §2 to §5, §6.)
10. **After force, there is an investigation; after a crime, there is support whether or not you
    report**, and compensation in Great Britain depends on reporting. (Part D §8.)

## Where the parts overlap or disagree, and how this file reconciles them

1. **What share of violence is by strangers.** The parts give different numbers because the
   sources measure different things, not because any part is wrong:

   | Source | Measure | Stranger share |
   |---|---|---|
   | NCVS 2024 (A US-1, B B21) | Nonfatal violent victimizations, survey | about 46% (3,091,500 of 6,671,640; the researchers' arithmetic) |
   | BJS stranger special report, 2005 to 2010 (A US-7) | Same survey, older years | about 39%; 62% of violence in public places; 9% in the home |
   | NIBRS 2024 (A US-2) | Police-recorded | assaults 13.5%, homicides 16.5%, sex offences 9.1%, robbery 42.5% |
   | CSEW YE March 2026 (A UK-1) | Prevalence, not share | 0.7% of adults hit by a stranger in a year, 0.4% by an acquaintance |
   | ONS homicide YE March 2025 (A UK-3) | Adult victims | men 18% by a stranger; women mostly by partners (40%) and family (16%) |
   | ONS rape YE March 2025 (B B20) | Victims since 16 | 16.3% by a stranger (15.0% of women, 37.5% of men) |

   **Reconciled as Part A §7.2 proposes: teach the method, not a number.** Every figure a lesson
   gives names its source, year, measure and whose violence it is. The CSEW numbers are
   percentages of adults, not shares of violence, and must not sit in the same column as the
   others in a lesson.
2. **Whether burglars meet people at home.** Part A lists "Burglars attack people at home" as a
   misconception, citing Warr's line that "burglaries normally occur when no one is home". Part B
   lists "Burglars strike when nobody is home" as a misconception, citing ONS 2017 (someone was at
   home in over half of burglaries with entry in England and Wales) and BJS 2010 (a household member
   was present in about 28% of US burglaries). **Both slogans fail, and they are different
   claims.** Someone being home is common; violence is not (a household member suffered violence
   in 7% of US household burglaries, 2003 to 2007). Warr's line is a 2000 paper summarising older US
   research; it should not be used as reassurance without that date and the BJS and ONS figures
   beside it. The US and UK "someone at home" figures differ in definition and period and are not
   to be reconciled into one number (Part B B17).
3. **Senn's trial appears in Parts B and C.** Part B's contested-question summary says the trial
   "halves rape". **Use Part C's wording**: 5.2% against 9.8% one-year completed rape, relative risk
   reduction 46.3% with a 95% interval of 6.8% to 69.1%, give both absolute risks, and say the effect
   diminished in year two. The trial is taught once, in the self-defence class lesson (sequence
   below); the avoidance lessons mention its "assess" and "acknowledge" steps only as awareness
   applied to people you know.
4. **Tark and Kleck** (see the unread-line note above). Read at abstract level in Part C, unread as
   full papers in Part D. Off the line; abstracts only. Part C leaves their gun-resistance result to
   Part D, and Part D does not discuss it; a lesson that mentions it may report only that the
   abstract lists resistance with a gun among the most protective actions and says some findings
   "were unstable due to the small numbers of sample cases".
5. **Philpot's CCTV study** is dated 2019 in Part B and 2020 in Part C. First Aid and CPR cites it
   as 2020 (its journal year). **Use 2020.**
6. **The victim's own gun in domestic abuse** (Part B B27 to B28, Part D §5). Part B asked Part D for
   the strongest statement of the armed-self-defence position; Part D supplied it for guns in
   general (NRA-ILA: "Self-defense is a fundamental right"; NRA Women on the duty to retreat; Kleck
   and Gertz on defensive gun use; the National Research Council's finding of lower injury rates
   among gun-using victims) but **found no statement specific to domestic abuse victims.** The
   lesson that raises the question must either find one (a drafter's task, noted in the open
   concerns below) or keep the question at the general level Part D reached, with Campbell's
   exact finding (clear danger from the abuser's access, no clear evidence of protection from the
   victim's) and the Hotline's position stated as the Hotline's.
7. **Several near-identical numbers are different measures.** Fraud victimisation and domestic
   abuse in England and Wales are both "7.8% of adults" in the parts (fraud YE March 2026; domestic
   abuse YE March 2025). Domestic abuse by sex (women 6.6%, men 3.0%) is from the YE March 2024
   article. UK homicides appear as 499 offences (police recorded, YE March 2026, Parts A and B) and
   522 victims (Homicide Index, YE March 2025, Part A). Male homicide victims appear as 72% (YE
   March 2024, Part B) and as rates per million (YE March 2025, Part A). A lesson uses one source per
   claim and gives its year.
8. **Emergency numbers and help lines are split between Parts B and D.** Part B has the domestic
   abuse and stalking lines and the Silent Solution; Part D has 999, 101, 911, text-to-911,
   VictimConnect, Victim Support and compensation. **VictimConnect's number, which Part B said was
   still to be read, was read in Part D (855-484-2846). RAINN's was not read by anyone.** Part B
   says the Silent Solution's "press 55" applies to calls from a caller who cannot speak; Part D
   notes that whether the 55 step applies to landlines was not checked. Do not state the landline
   case until someone checks the full IOPC guide.
9. **Reporting to police.** Part A's figures (about half of US violence and under a third of US
   property crime is reported; 36% of England and Wales survey crime) sit beside Part C's and D's
   guidance on when and how to report. The aftermath lesson should use Part A's figures to say
   plainly that most crime is never reported, and Part D's to say what reporting gets you
   (compensation in Great Britain depends on it; Victim Support does not).
10. **Where de-escalation stops.** Part C teaches de-escalation for strangers and workplaces and
    warns that de-escalation advice for an abusive partner "can look like managing the abuser".
    Part B's domestic abuse section says the reader decides and gives planning resources. **The
    de-escalation lesson must say plainly that it is not advice for an abusive relationship** and
    point to the domestic abuse lesson.

---

## What the field considers essential

In the order the ideas build. This becomes the outline's backbone.

1. **Two ways to count crime.** Police records and victim surveys answer different questions. Not
   every crime is reported, not every report is recorded (HMIC: 19% of crimes reported to police
   went unrecorded, 33% for violence), recording rules change, surveys miss homicide and
   institutions. Rates, counts and prevalence; incidents against victims; per how many, of whom,
   over what period. (Part A §1, §5.)
2. **Fear is not the same as risk, and neither is irrational.** Fear is shaped by seriousness
   times perceived likelihood (Warr), by media, and by everyday harassment; standard survey
   questions inflate it (Gray, Jackson and Farrall). The goal is functional fear: worry that turns
   into a few cheap precautions and then stops (Jackson and Gray). Worry that shrinks a life harms
   health (Jackson and Stafford). (Part A §4, Part B B12.)
3. **Who is at risk, from whom, where and when**, split by crime and by sex: the young, men for
   stranger and public violence, women for partner violence, sexual violence and stalking; the
   separated, the urban, disabled people, repeat victims. Violence clusters in evenings and around
   alcohol; half of homicides in England and Wales start as quarrels. **Responsibility for a crime
   lies with the offender; precautions change odds, not blame.** (Parts A and B.)
4. **Repeat victimisation.** Past victimisation is the best single predictor of the next, and the
   risk is highest soon after. That is the moment to change the lock, the route or the account.
   (Part A §2, Part B B14.)
5. **Opportunity is the part of crime you can change.** Routine activity theory (a likely offender,
   a suitable target, no capable guardian) and situational prevention (effort, risk, reward,
   provocation, excuse), taught as tools for your own risk, not a theory of offenders. (Part B §1.)
6. **Awareness is attention, and it is limited.** Endsley's three levels as a frame (once read);
   phone conversations cut noticing (Hyman: 25% against 71% for pairs); what the gait research does
   and does not show. (Part B §2.)
7. **The home: locks and lights in combination**, don't go in if you think someone is inside, the
   door and the phone as one con. (Part B §3, B24.)
8. **Out and away: plan the way home, tell someone, stay where there are people and light**, with
   the evidence behind each labelled. Travel and meeting people from online are guardianship
   applied. (Part B §4, B25.)
9. **Domestic abuse and stalking: plan, don't prescribe.** Numbers first; the reader decides;
   leaving is the most dangerous time; technology can be used against you; the Silent Solution.
   (Part B §7.)
10. **The ladder**: avoid, leave, talk, comply or resist, get away, with leaving available at every
    rung. (Part C §8.)
11. **De-escalation as a named skill**, with its honest evidence status: regulate yourself first,
    one voice, space and angle, hands visible, short sentences, listen, agree where you honestly
    can, limits without threats, a face-saving exit, and know when to stop talking and leave.
    (Part C §2.)
12. **Bystanders**: safe indirect options first, what cameras show bystanders actually do, the risk
    to interveners, and the commercial-robbery exception. (Part C §6, building on First Aid 1.)
13. **What acute fear does**: freezing is common and involuntary, perception and memory distort,
    fine skill degrades. (Part C §5, §4.4.)
14. **The robbery decision**: a demand for property against an attack on the person. (Part C §3.)
15. **Sexual assault**: responsibility lies with the perpetrator; freeze is common; resistance
    often works and rarely adds injury; resistance education has trial evidence. (Part C §4.)
16. **What a class can teach, what a page cannot, and how to choose one.** (Part C §7.)
17. **The law of force**: necessity, proportionality, imminence, belief; escape first in practice;
    the home; property; the differences between England and Wales, Scotland, and US states. (Part
    D §2 to §4, §6.)
18. **Carrying something for protection**: the law in each country, and the contested evidence on
    guns stated with each side's own words. (Part D §2.5, §2.6, §5.)
19. **Afterwards**: an investigation after force; how to report; rights if questioned; support
    whether or not you report; compensation. (Part D §8.)

---

## Common misconceptions

Merged from the four parts; each is corrected by a named source there.

**About risk and the numbers** (Part A §6, Part B)
- "Crime is rising." Long-run data in both countries show large falls since the mid-1990s; ONS
  says national perceptions "do not typically match well to reality".
- "Most violence is committed by strangers", and its mirror, "most violence is by people you know."
  Each is true for some crimes and victims and false for others. Say which.
- "Police figures are the real numbers" and "survey figures are just opinion" (OSR's own list of
  misunderstandings).
- "Recorded crime went up, so crime went up." Recording improved after 2014 (HMIC).
- "A single year's figure is the trend." NCVS rape reporting went from 46% to 24% in a year;
  anti-social behaviour rose with a question change.
- "A lifetime figure and an annual figure are the same kind of number" (NISVS against NCVS).
- "Women and older people are irrationally afraid." Warr rejects rationality as the frame; see the
  contested questions.
- "The person most at risk on the street at night is a woman alone." Measured by stranger violence,
  it is young men (Part B B21).

**About avoidance** (Part B)
- "Stranger danger" as the core of child safety: only about one percent of missing child cases
  reported to NCMEC are stereotypical kidnappings.
- "Don't walk alone at night" as the core of women's safety: the street is a small share of where
  rape happens. The advice is not wrong, only misweighted.
- "Walk confidently and you won't be picked": overstates rating studies.
- "An alarm is the best protection": not supported by the England and Wales data; contested.
- "Burglars strike when nobody is home" and "burglars attack people at home": both fail (see the
  reconciliation above).
- "Earphones make you a target": music players did not reduce noticing in Hyman's study; phone
  conversations did.
- "Change your passwords regularly": withdrawn advice (NCSC), still on some safety pages.
- "Just leave": separation from a controlling partner is the highest-risk period.
- "If I call 999 and can't speak, they'll come": not unless you press 55 when prompted.

**About confrontation** (Part C §9)
- "A real victim fights back." False (CPS; Möller: 70% significant tonic immobility).
- "Resisting a robber always gets you hurt", and "resisting is always safer". Both overclaim.
- "De-escalation is proven." Recommended everywhere; the trial evidence is thin outside policing.
- "Training fixes it once." Effects and attitudes fade.
- "Crowds mean no one helps." Corrected in First Aid 1; add the commercial robbery exception.
- "Filming is always helping." Right To Be: check someone is already helping, and never post
  without the person's consent.
- "Self-defence means stranger attacks." The best programmes centre acquaintances.

**About the law** (Part D §7)
- "In the UK, householders can use any disproportionate force." After *R v Ray*, force short of
  grossly disproportionate is not automatically reasonable.
- "The householder rule covers my garden, or my property." Building only; self-defence only.
- "You must wait to be hit first." No, in England, Scotland or US statutes.
- "Stand your ground means you can use deadly force whenever you feel threatened." It removes only
  the duty to retreat.
- "The UK has a duty to retreat." England and Wales: a factor only. Scotland: in effect yes, where a
  safe escape exists. The UK is not one jurisdiction on this.
- "Carrying something for self-defence is a reasonable excuse." Not in England and Wales for a
  constant or general fear; intention can make an ordinary object an offensive weapon.
- "Pepper spray is legal in the UK if it's for self-defence." Prohibited.
- "You can shoot someone for stealing." Not under the MPC, in England or in Scotland; Texas §9.42 is
  the narrow exception.
- "A drunk mistake still counts." Not in England (s.76(5)).
- "Using a household object makes it worse." No (CPS/NPCC).

---

## Contested questions and the positions on each

Each is set out in full, with sources and each side's best case, in the part named. The
classification is standards 3.1: kind 1 established, kind 2 contested empirical, kind 3 value.

| # | Question | Classification | Where |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Is the fear-victimisation paradox real, an artefact of measurement, an undercount of women's victimisation, or the product of precaution? | Kind 2, genuinely open; present all four positions, pick none | A §7.1 |
| 2 | What share of violence is by strangers? | Kind 1 within each source; the apparent dispute is about measures. Teach the method | A §7.2, reconciliation 1 above |
| 3 | Is US crime rising or falling since 2020? | Kind 2, politically charged. Teach how to read NCVS and UCR/NIBRS; never state a partisan conclusion; both agree on long-term declines | A §7.3 |
| 4 | Should safety teaching reduce fear or tolerate it; is personal safety advice for women protective or harmful? | Partly kind 2, partly kind 3. Warr's "dial"; functional fear; Vera-Gray and Kelly's "safety work" critique in its own terms; the evidence separates restriction-based advice from skill-based training; who should carry the burden of prevention is described, not resolved. The course's one value commitment, which every position shares: responsibility lies with the offender | A §7.4, B contested 3 |
| 5 | Do burglar alarms protect a home? | Kind 2, no consensus. England and Wales survey data (Tilley 2015, Tseloni 2017) against US offender interviews and a Newark area study (largely alarm-industry funded). Say who funded what without implying funding decides the result. Consensus is strong on locks and lights | B B14 to B16, contested 1 |
| 6 | Does situational awareness, or a change of gait, prevent victimisation? | Kind 2, not shown. Raters agree on cues; some cues track past victimisation; nobody has measured later victimisation after training | B §2, contested 2 |
| 7 | Does displacement undo situational prevention? | Kind 2 with a strong lean: displacement is usually small and diffusion of benefits about as common (Clarke; Guerette and Bowers snippet only) | B B2, B4, contested 5 |
| 8 | Does de-escalation work, and does training members of the public reduce violence? | Official recommendation everywhere (kind 1 that bodies recommend it); ICAT's effect on police force is trial evidence; the specific techniques are expert consensus; public training is untested (kind 2) | C §2 |
| 9 | Comply with a robber or resist? | Kind 2, with a working consensus on the narrow case (weapon shown, property wanted: hand it over). Official advice, Faulkner, Smith and Block and Skogan against Tark and Kleck, Guerette and Santana and the POP guide. Both sides share one flaw: victims choose to resist where it looks survivable, and surveys cannot count the dead | C §3 |
| 10 | Does resistance education prevent sexual assault, and should prevention effort go there? | Effectiveness kind 2 (EAAA RCT and follow-ups; Nairobi trials, one independent null and disputed; Kettrey's meta-analysis and the ESD authors' reply). Where the burden should fall is kind 3: describe Senn, Hollander and the CDC 2016 package alongside the critics, reach no verdict | C §4.2, §4.3 |
| 11 | What is the human acute stress response sequence, and do the sexes differ? | Freezing, perceptual distortion and loss of fine skill are kind 1. The sequence and names (Bracha's five Fs, tend-and-befriend, "fawn") are proposals. Heart-rate thresholds are practitioner lore with no validation found; do not state them | C §5 |
| 12 | Should a bystander call the police? | Kind 3 as Right To Be frames it. Attribute its position (check with the person first) and set it beside the police position and the plain point that a violent assault in progress is an emergency; adopt neither | C §6 |
| 13 | Stand your ground or a duty to retreat? | Whether the law should require retreat is kind 3 (NRA Women and NRA-ILA against Giffords, in their own words; "stand-your-ground" as proponents say, "shoot-first" as opponents say). Whether SYG laws raise homicides is kind 2 where RAND grades the evidence "supportive" of an increase: attribute that to RAND, with the deterrence rationale and RAND's own caveat about case-law states | D §5.1 |
| 14 | How often do Americans use guns in self-defence? | Kind 2. Estimates differ more than twentyfold (NCVS about 60,000 to 120,000; Kleck and Gertz 2.2 to 2.5 million; English 1.67 million). Frame on the National Research Council 2013 statement, which states both halves; reach no verdict | D §5.2 |
| 15 | Does a gun in the home make a household safer? | Kind 2. Anglemyer's meta-analysis (suicide OR 3.24, homicide victimisation OR 2.00, observational) against the NRC's finding of lower injury among gun-using victims and the causation critique (Kleck, unread; read one before writing that side). Crisis footer required | D §5.3 |
| 16 | Should a domestic abuse victim keep a gun? | Kind 3 with an empirical core. Campbell: clear danger from the abuser's access, no clear evidence of protection from the victim's. The Hotline advises against; the armed-self-defence position has not yet been found in a domestic-abuse-specific statement (reconciliation 6) | B B27, D §5 |

Not contested, and not to be both-sided: that pepper spray is a prohibited weapon in Great
Britain (D §5.4); what the statutes say (D §2 to §4); that most violence causes minor or no injury;
that women are most often killed by partners; that responsibility lies with the offender.

---

## Safety-critical guidance

The rules every lesson must keep, merged from the four parts.

**Handling readers**
- **Never tell a reader their fear is irrational**, and never compare a victim's risk to falling
  downstairs as a way of dismissing it; say why the comparison is incomplete (Warr's advice on
  risk comparison) (Part A §11).
- **Say plainly, once in every lesson built on who is at risk or what raises risk, that
  responsibility lies with the offender** and that precautions change odds, not blame (Parts A, B,
  C).
- **Never tell a reader what they should have done.** Scenarios must not imply a victim chose
  wrongly. Nobody who complied or resisted chose wrongly (Part C §3, §11).
- **Content notes** go before, not after, any section on sexual violence or domestic abuse, with a
  link to support at that point (Part C §4, §11). In the sexual assault material, the freeze comes
  before the resistance evidence.
- **Domestic abuse and stalking lessons**: helpline numbers and a "browsing can be monitored" line
  in the first screen; never say "just leave"; the reader is the expert and decides (Part B §7).
- **De-escalation lessons**: leaving is always allowed; de-escalation is for when leaving is not yet
  possible or someone depends on you staying; this is not advice for an abusive relationship (Part
  C §11).
- **The aftermath lesson and any passage on guns in the home carry First Aid lesson 16's
  suicide-crisis footer word for word** (Part D §8.4). Where guns and suicide meet, the footer is
  mandatory.

**What not to reproduce**
- **The National Domestic Violence Hotline firearms page's tip** to report an abusive partner's car
  to 911 "for suspected drunk driving" (it amounts to advising a false report), and its tactics for
  when a gun is pointed at you. Its "500 percent" figure is broader than Campbell's finding; hold
  lessons to Campbell's wording (Part B B28).
- **The Suzy Lamplugh Trust's unsourced figure** that "half of all burglaries happen on the spur of
  the moment through unlocked doors or windows"; use BJS (40% of entries to unoccupied homes through
  an unlocked door or window) and the UNCC figure (41% spur of the moment) as the different measures
  they are (Part B B17). Nor its "change passwords regularly" advice (Part B B25), nor its personal
  alarm technique beyond "set it off and leave" (Part B B18).
- **Offender race tables** (NCVS Tables 11 and 13; ONS ethnicity data). The course does not need
  them, and a bare number is misleading given ONS's own caveat about confounding. If a reviewer
  insists, include ONS's caveat verbatim (Part A §1, §11).
- **Grayson and Stein's sample sizes** until someone reads the paper (Part B B6).
- **Siddle's or Grossman's heart-rate thresholds** (Part C §5).
- **Effectiveness figures for pepper spray**, which nobody researched (Part D §5.4).
- **Product recommendations** of any kind: sprays, "legal alternatives", alarms by brand, apps,
  firearms (Part D §5.4, §10.3).

**Practical rules the evidence supports**
- If you think someone is inside your home, do not go in or investigate; call the police (Part B
  B17; CPS/NPCC "Wherever possible you should call the police").
- A personal alarm is for making noise and leaving (Part B B18).
- Urgency is the tell in every con: stop, hang up, call back on a number you already have (Part B
  B24).
- In England, Wales and Scotland: 999, and if you cannot speak, listen, cough or tap, and press 55
  when prompted (mobile case; landline not checked). In the US: 911, text if you cannot call where
  text-to-911 is available (more than half of call centres in 2021) (Part B B26, Part D §8.1).

**The legal line**
- Every legal statement is general information, with "the law differs by state, and between
  England and Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland". The course never tells a reader what they may
  lawfully do in a specific situation (Part D §10).
- **On talking to police after using force, state the rights and say "get a lawyer's advice",
  nothing more.** What to say is legal advice, varies by jurisdiction, and is a commercial genre
  (Part D §8.2). The US rights (Miranda) were not read; cite the Constitution Annotated if a lesson
  needs them.
- **No technique.** A page cannot teach a strike or an escape, and the course never asks a reader to
  practise one on a person (Part C §7). Nothing on choosing or using a firearm (Part D §10.3).

## Best existing free resources (to link, not duplicate)

**Reading the numbers** (Part A §10)
- BJS NCVS Dashboard (N-DASH), https://ncvs.bjs.ojp.gov
- FBI Crime Data Explorer, https://cde.ucr.cjis.gov
- BJS, *The Nation's Two Crime Measures, 2015–2024*, a short explainer
- Council on Criminal Justice, *When Crime Statistics Diverge*
- ONS, *Crime in England and Wales* (quarterly) and *Homicide in England and Wales* (annual)
- Warr, "Fear of Crime in the United States" (free on ojp.gov)

**Avoidance** (Part B)
- Suzy Lamplugh Trust personal safety pages (with the cautions above)
- FTC "How To Avoid a Scam" and ReportFraud.ftc.gov (US); Take Five and Report Fraud (UK)
- FCDO "Solo and independent travel" (UK); State Department Travel Advisories, STEP and the
  traveller's checklist (US)
- NCMEC KidSmartz, for the household section on children
- Tseloni and colleagues 2017 (open access), the POP Center's 25 techniques grid

**Domestic abuse and stalking** (Part B §7)
- National Domestic Violence Hotline, 1.800.799.SAFE (7233), text START to 88788 (US)
- National Domestic Abuse Helpline (Refuge), 0808 2000 247; Refuge Tech Safety (UK)
- Men's Advice Line (Respect), 0808 8010327 (UK)
- National Stalking Helpline (Suzy Lamplugh Trust), 0808 802 0300 (UK)
- IOPC Silent Solution page (UK)

**Confrontation** (Part C §10)
- Right To Be, the 5Ds and free virtual trainings
- Richmond and colleagues 2012, open access on PMC
- HSE "Violence and aggression at work"; OSHA workplace violence page
- Metropolitan Police street robbery page (UK); DC MPD robbery brochure (US)
- CPS rape myths annex, once its current URL is confirmed
- CrimeSolutions ICAT profile
- National Women's Martial Arts Federation, "How to choose a self-defense class"

**Law and afterwards** (Part D §9)
- CPS/NPCC, *Householders and the use of force against intruders*; GOV.UK "Using reasonable force
  against intruders"; legislation.gov.uk CJIA 2008 s.76; CPS knife guidance
- Judicial Institute for Scotland, Jury Manual (self-defence chapter)
- NCSL, "Self-Defense and 'Stand Your Ground'"; RAND's stand-your-ground and defensive gun use
  pages; National Academies 2013, *Priorities for Research to Reduce the Threat of Firearm-Related
  Violence*
- GOV.UK "Contact the police"; 911.gov FAQ
- OVC "Help for Victims"; VictimConnect 855-484-2846 (US); Victim Support 08 08 16 89 111 (England
  and Wales); GOV.UK criminal injuries compensation

**Internal**: First Aid and CPR lessons 1 and 16; Mental Fitness lesson 7; Digital Literacy lessons
10 and 11; Personal Finance Fundamentals lesson 10.

Every number above was read on 2026-09-25; recheck at drafting.

## Open concerns about scope

1. **A lesson of its own for domestic abuse and stalking** (Part B's recommendation, supported by
   Part A §11 and Part C §11). This is where most serious risk to women lies, and the surveys
   undercount it. The sequence below gives it one. It needs the domestic abuse services' own
   guidance, which Part B read (Refuge, the Hotline, Men's Advice Line, Refuge Tech Safety,
   Campbell), and it must include men as victims.
2. **Fraud stays small and linked** (Parts A §12.3, B §6). It is the commonest crime and the pull to
   reteach phishing will be strong. Keep it to the pressure pattern shared by the phone, the text
   and the doorstep, and link Digital Literacy lessons 10 and 11 for the technical side and
   Personal Finance lesson 10 for scams as a money problem. With three published lessons already
   on it, this course's fraud section may be a few paragraphs.
3. **Offender race tables are left out** (Part A). See safety-critical guidance.
4. **The Hotline's unsafe tip and Suzy Lamplugh's unsourced figure are not reproduced** (Part B).
5. **Content notes for sexual violence**, and for domestic abuse, before the section (Part C).
6. **A crisis footer where guns and suicide meet** (Part D §8.4): First Aid lesson 16's footer,
   word for word.
7. **No legal advice on talking to police beyond stating rights** (Part D §8.2).
8. **"The UK" in the course description** outruns the research. The lessons say England and Wales
   for crime data, Great Britain where the source does, Scotland separately for law, and Northern
   Ireland only as different again. Either that, or someone reads the Police Scotland, PSNI and
   Northern Ireland legal sources later (Parts A §12.2, D §10.2).
9. **The self-defence evidence is about women and girls and sexual assault.** Men's self-defence,
   general assault and robbery are almost unstudied. The class lesson must say this gap out loud
   and must not generalise the EAAA result (Part C §11).
10. **Weapons can swallow a lesson** (Part D §10.3). One section per country on what is legal to
    carry, then the defensive gun use and household-risk evidence as a short contested block framed
    by the National Research Council. The neutrality audit should read that block as a gun-rights
    reader and as a gun-control reader.
11. **The domestic-abuse-specific case for a victim's gun** was not found (reconciliation 6). Before
    the weapons lesson raises the question in the domestic abuse context, a drafter should look for
    the strongest statement from its proponents; if none is found, keep the question general.
12. **The race and stand-your-ground studies** (Roman 2012, cited by RAND; Giffords' 281% figure)
    were not read. Either read them or keep the point attributed and brief (Part D §10.5).
13. **The data change every year and some series are mid-transition** (NCVS redesign, new CSEW
    sexual victimisation questions, UK fraud reporting moved to Report Fraud). Lessons teach where to
    look and how to read, give dated examples, and put a year beside every figure (Part A §12.1).
14. **Children: one household section at most** (Part B).
15. **No university course teaches personal safety from the crime data** (Part A §9). The closest
    syllabi are victimology seminars (UT Dallas CRIM 6308 puts NCVS, NIBRS and the CSEW side by side
    before theory) and campus self-defence programmes (UConn: awareness to avoidance to basic
    self-defence, twelve hours minimum). This course's order, data first, has no syllabus to copy;
    that is a strength and a risk, and Stage 2 should check the first two lessons do not read as a
    statistics course that happens to be about crime.
16. **Status.** The course is at `status: research`; this file does not change it. The
    research-course command sets `drafting` once SOURCES.md is written, in the same commit as the
    TAXONOMY row.

---

## Suggested lesson sequence (for Stage 2 to confirm)

Twelve lessons, from the data, to avoidance, to de-escalation, to resistance, to the law, to
afterwards. Each lists the parts it draws on.

1. **What a crime number counts, and what fear measures.** Police records against victim surveys
   (NCVS, NIBRS, CSEW, police recorded crime); rates, counts and prevalence; the reporting gap;
   recording changes; the NCVS 2024 worked example (8.9, 5.3 and 3.7 per 1,000, all correct).
   Fear as seriousness times likelihood; the perception gap; functional fear; Warr's caution on
   comparing risks. Contested: the paradox, US trends since 2020. (Part A §1, §4, §7.)
2. **Who is harmed, by whom, where and when.** Age, sex, place and time; the stranger-share table
   taught as method; fraud as the commonest crime; most violence minor; ordinary risks (falls,
   roads) with Warr's caveat; repeat victimisation. Responsibility lies with the offender. Content
   note for the homicide and sexual violence figures. (Part A §1 to §3, §5; Part B §5.)
3. **Attention and opportunity.** Routine activity theory and situational prevention; what
   awareness is (Endsley once read); what degrades it (Hyman); the gait research and its limits;
   intuition and its prejudice problem, with cues framed as behaviour, not appearance. Contested:
   awareness and gait; displacement. (Part B §1, §2.)
4. **Home, the door and the phone.** WIDE and the burglary data; alarms as a contested question;
   how burglars get in; don't go in; the week after a burglary. The bogus caller, the phone scam
   and the text scam taught as one pressure pattern (FTC, Take Five), kept short and linked to
   Digital Literacy 10 and 11 and Personal Finance 10. (Part B §3, §6 B24; Part A R-5.)
5. **Out, away, and meeting people from online.** Route, company, light; what lighting and CCTV
   evidence does and does not show; the official street and travel advice and its honest
   evidence status; if you are followed; meeting someone from online as guardianship; a short
   household section on talking to children. (Part B §4, B23, B25.)
6. **Domestic abuse and stalking.** Numbers and the monitoring warning first; content note; what
   abuse and stalking look like; why leaving is the most dangerous time (Campbell); safety planning
   as the services teach it; trackers and stalkerware (Refuge Tech Safety, as links); the Silent
   Solution; lines for men as well as women. Not de-escalation. (Part B §7; Part A UK-2, UK-3.)
7. **Talking it down, and stepping in for someone else.** The ladder (avoid, leave, talk, comply or
   resist, get away); de-escalation techniques with each one's evidence status (NICE NG10,
   Richmond, ICAT, Quinn's rules as practitioner opinion; "social" and "asocial" as an untested
   model); when to stop and leave. Bystanders: the 5Ds, what CCTV shows bystanders do, the risk to
   interveners, the commercial-robbery exception, building on First Aid lesson 1. Contested: the
   police question (attributed). (Part C §2, §6.)
8. **When it turns physical: what fear does, and whether to hand it over.** Content note. The
   defence cascade, freezing as involuntary and common, perceptual and memory distortion (with
   Artwohl's caveats); then the robbery decision, both sides at full strength, and the distinction
   between a demand for property and an attack on the person; then resistance to sexual assault
   (Ullman, Tark and Kleck abstracts, Brecklin and Ullman), with the freeze taught first and the
   law's position that no resistance is required. (Part C §3, §4.1, §4.4, §5.)
9. **What a self-defence class can teach, and how to choose one.** The EAAA trial and follow-ups,
   the Nairobi and other trials, Kettrey's meta-analysis and the reply, the value question about
   where the burden falls; stress-exposed practice and fading skills; the gap for men and for
   robbery; the NWMAF checklist and the course's own labelled checklist. Content note. (Part C §4.2,
   §4.3, §5, §7.)
10. **The law on using force.** General information, not advice. The shared core (necessity,
    proportionality, imminence, belief); England and Wales (s.76, s.3, Palmer as quoted by the
    CPS, the householder rule and *Ray*); Scotland (reasonable belief, escape, cruel excess, no
    property defence); the US (MPC, Florida, New York, Texas, NCSL's categories rather than counts).
    Contested: stand your ground against a duty to retreat, with RAND's grading attributed. (Part
    D §2 to §4, §5.1, §6, §7.)
11. **Carrying something for protection.** What is legal to carry in each country (offensive
    weapons, blades, pepper spray as a prohibited firearm in Great Britain, US spray law and the
    TSA rule); then the contested evidence on defensive gun use and on a gun in the home, framed on
    the National Research Council, each side in its own words; the domestic abuse gun question if
    its sources are found. No product or technique. Crisis footer. (Part D §2.5, §2.6, §5.2 to §5.4;
    Part B B27.)
12. **Afterwards.** How to report (999, 101, 55; 911, text, the local non-emergency number); what
    reporting does and does not get you (most crime is never reported; compensation depends on it,
    support does not); the investigation after force; rights if questioned, and "get a lawyer's
    advice"; Victim Support, VictimConnect, the Victims' Code, compensation; then First Aid lesson
    16 for support lines and the crisis footer, and Mental Fitness lesson 7 for what reactions after
    a frightening event usually look like. Crisis footer. (Part D §8; Part A §1.)

If Stage 2 finds lesson 8 too long, the stress response can become its own lesson before the
robbery decision, making thirteen; the order stays the same.

---

## Part A: Risk, and reading the data

*Stage 1 research for Personal Safety, Part A of four, written 2026-09-25. Scope: what the crime and
injury data say about who is at risk, from whom, where and when, in the US and in England and
Wales; what each official source counts and misses; the fear-of-crime literature; comparisons
with ordinary risks; statistics literacy for crime numbers; and the textbooks and syllabi for the
whole course. All reading was done on 2026-09-25.*

**How the quotation marks work in this part.** Every quotation marked **[V]** was copied from text
I extracted myself: `pdftotext` on the publisher's own PDF, or `curl` plus an HTML stripper on the
publisher's page, or `curl` on an Internet Archive capture where the live site blocked automated
readers (the capture ID is given). **[T]** marks a line returned by the WebFetch summarising tool;
there are only two, both publisher tables of contents, and chapter titles are not quoted in a
lesson anyway. Nothing from a search-result snippet is in quotation marks. Where I write "snippet
only", nothing from that item may be stated in a lesson as a finding.

**Access notes a drafter inherits.**
- **cdc.gov and nhtsa.gov returned "Access Denied" to curl.** The CDC FastStats pages and the
  NHTSA 2025 press release were read from Wayback captures (IDs below). The CDC pages label their
  source years inconsistently (one page says "Mortality Data (2024)", the homicide page "(2023)").
  Check the year on the live page in a browser before a lesson states a year.
- **The NCVS changed instruments in 2024.** BJS ran the old and new questionnaires on half the
  sample each; *Criminal Victimization, 2024* reports the legacy half only, so its 2024 estimates are
  less precise than usual. (Stage 4 correction, lesson 02: BJS does not say the redesigned
  figures will not be comparable; it says the split sample "also allows for the continued
  production of year-to-year comparisons". See "Gate closures, lesson 02".) If a 2025 or later
  figure looks like a jump, check which instrument it came from.
- **The CSEW lost and regained its accreditation.** OSR suspended it in July 2022 over quality
  concerns including a falling response rate and reinstated it in 2024. ONS pages for the years
  ending March 2023 and March 2024 carry the note that those years "are not designated as accredited
  official statistics". Police recorded crime lost its accreditation in January 2014 and has not
  regained it.
- **Books were not read.** For every textbook below I read the publisher's table of contents only.
  They are on the unread line and a lesson may not attribute a claim to them.

---

### 1. The two measuring systems in each country

The single most important idea in this part, and probably the first lesson's spine: **every crime
number is either a count of what police recorded or an estimate from asking people**, and the two
answer different questions. Learners who grasp that can read any headline.

#### US-1. BJS, *Criminal Victimization, 2024* (Tapp and Coen, NCJ 310547, September 2025)
https://bjs.ojp.gov/document/cv24.pdf. **Read in full** (37 pp, pdftotext). The annual bulletin of
the National Crime Victimization Survey. What it establishes:

- **What it counts.** Nonfatal violent crime (rape or sexual assault, robbery, aggravated and simple
  assault) and household property crime, against people aged 12 or older in households, whether
  or not reported to police. It **cannot count homicide**: [V] "Excludes homicide because the
  National Crime Victimization Survey (NCVS) is based on interviews with victims." Sample: [V]
  "about 240,000 persons in approximately 150,000 households" in a typical year; the 2024 legacy
  half was 109,341 persons in 69,396 households.
- **Three kinds of number**, defined on p. 2 and worth teaching by name: victimizations ("the
  total number of times that persons or households were victims"), incidents ("specific criminal
  acts involving one or more victims") and prevalence ("the number or percentage of unique persons
  who were crime victims"). A learner who confuses incidents with victims will misread both
  countries' data.
- **Headline figures, 2024.** [V] "the rate of violent victimization in the United States was 23.3
  victimizations per 1,000 persons age 12 or older". Prevalence: [V] "In 2024, 1.45% of persons age
  12 or older experienced at least one violent victimization". Excluding simple assault, 0.60%.
  6.7 million violent victimizations; 13.1 million property victimizations.
- **Reporting to police.** [V] "Approximately 48% of violent victimizations were reported to police
  in 2024"; [V] "In 2024, 30% of property victimizations were reported". The rape or sexual assault
  reporting figure swung from 46% in 2023 to 24% in 2024, and robbery from 42% to 73%. **Do not
  teach either single-year figure as the reporting rate.** (Stage 4 correction, lesson 02: BJS
  table 4 marks **both changes significant at the 95% level**, so they are not to be taught as
  noise or "what small subsamples do". What stands is that each single-year figure is imprecise,
  with standard errors of about 7 to 8 points for rape and 5 to 6 for robbery, and that the
  National Research Council panel (US-5) warns that year comparisons of rape in this survey are
  "particularly problematic". See "Gate closures, lesson 02".) Teach "roughly half of violence and
  under a third of property crime reaches the police" as the dated rate for the 2023 and 2024
  surveys.
- **Relationship to offender (Table 1, 2024).** Stranger violence 3,091,500 victimizations (10.8
  per 1,000); domestic violence (intimate partners and family) 1,110,040 (3.9 per 1,000); intimate
  partner violence 782,900 (2.7 per 1,000). So **strangers account for about 46% of nonfatal
  violent victimizations** in the survey (my arithmetic: 3,091,500 / 6,671,640), which is a direct
  challenge to the slogan that violence is mostly by people you know. That slogan is true for
  homicide of women and for sexual violence; it is not true for nonfatal violence as a whole,
  which is dominated by simple assault among young men. A lesson has to make that distinction.
- **Who: age and sex (Table 3).** Rates per 1,000 in 2024: ages 12 to 17, 29.3; 18 to 24, 34.8;
  25 to 34, 31.7; 35 to 49, 27.1; 50 to 64, 20.4; **65 or older, 7.5**. Male 24.7, female 21.9 (Stage 4
  correction, lesson 03: the bulletin does **not** test the male-female difference; its only
  significance statement on sex is that the rate "did not change significantly from 2023 to 2024".
  Say the two are close; do not call the difference non-significant on BJS's authority). Marital
  status: married 12.5, never married 32.5, separated 74.7 (the least precise figure in the table:
  standard error 26.46, and 43.1 in 2023; see "Gate closures, lesson 03"). Risk falls steeply with age; the group most afraid in surveys (older people, section 4) is
  the least victimized.
- **Where people live (Table 8).** Violent victimization per 1,000: urban 34.0, suburban 23.3,
  rural 16.7.
- **Offenders (Table 12).** Of 6.1 million violent incidents, the offender was perceived as male in
  about 4.2 million; [V] "The percentage of violent incidents involving male offenders (76%) was 1.5
  times the percentage of males in the population". Offender characteristics are **victims'
  perceptions**, a caveat the bulletin states and a lesson should keep.
- **Weapons.** About 10% of violent victimizations involved a firearm; about 75% of those were
  reported to police.

The race tables (Tables 11 and 13) exist and a drafter will see them. This course has no reason to
teach offender race, and teaching it without the socioeconomic context the ONS homicide article
insists on (UK-3) would fail the neutrality audit. Leave them out.

#### US-2. FBI, *UCR Summary of Reported Crimes in the Nation, 2024* (released August 2025)
https://cde.ucr.cjis.gov/LATEST/resources/reports/UCR%20Summary%20of%20Reported%20Crimes%20in%20the%20Nation%202024.pdf.
**Read in full** (pdftotext). Also the companion *Reported Crimes in the Nation 2024 FAQs*
(same site, 7 pp, read in full).

- **What it counts.** Offences known to and reported by police agencies. 16,675 agencies covering
  95.6% of the population; NIBRS agencies cover 87.2%. Estimation fills the rest.
- **Headline.** [V] "An estimated 1,221,345 violent crime offenses were committed in 2024,
  indicating a rate of 359.1 violent crimes per 100,000 inhabitants". Murder fell an estimated
  14.9% from 2023; the 2024 murder rate was an estimated 5.0 per 100,000, against a 20-year low of
  4.4 (2014) and high of 6.7 (2020).
- **The hierarchy rule**, a classic reason two sources disagree. From the FAQ: [V] "The SRS
  Hierarchy Rule states only the one most serious offense within a criminal incident is counted.
  For example, if a robbery and a homicide occur in the same incident, SRS will only count the
  homicide." NIBRS records up to 10 offences per incident.
- **Victim-offender relationship, NIBRS 2024 (pp. 6 to 9).** Homicide: [V] "35.2% of homicide
  victims knew their offender(s) (or at least 1 offender when more than 1 was present) but were not
  related to them. NIBRS data also showed 16.5% of homicide victims were killed by a stranger,
  14.8% by a family member". And [V] "About one-third of homicide victims (32.7%) had relationships
  categorized as “all other,” meaning the victim(s) did not see the offender to determine whether
  they knew them, was a mutual combatant of the offender, or had a combination of other undefined
  relationships with multiple offenders." Assault: 13.5% by strangers, 21.9% by family, 49.2%
  "known to victim and other". Sex offences: 9.1% stranger, 26.0% family, 52.1% known. **Robbery:
  [V] "the most common relationship type reported by robbery victims in 2024 was stranger
  (42.5%)."**
- **Teaching point: compare 13.5% with the NCVS's 46%.** Police-recorded assaults show far fewer
  strangers than the victim survey does. Candidate reasons a lesson can offer as reasons (not as
  settled findings): different offence definitions (NIBRS assault includes intimidation), the large
  "all other" and "known and other" categories, and differences in which incidents reach police.
  The honest lesson is that "what share of violence is by strangers" has different answers in
  different sources, and you must say which.
- The FBI's caveat on **its own two estimate series** (the older SRS-based estimates and the
  NIBRS-only estimates; Stage 4 correction, lesson 02: it is not about FBI data against the NCVS):
  [V] "Both collections are estimates and should be viewed as analogous measures, rather than as
  one being more accurate than the other." On the NCVS, the FBI says each program "has unique
  strengths" (see "Gate closures, lesson 02").

#### US-3. BJS, *The Nation's Two Crime Measures, 2015–2024* (Remrey and Tinney, NCJ 310700, March 2026)
https://bjs.ojp.gov/document/ntcm1524.pdf. **Read in full** (short statistical brief). The official
side-by-side. Its opening is quotable and clean: [V] "The NCVS, sponsored by the Bureau of Justice
Statistics (BJS), measures nonfatal criminal victimization, both reported and not reported to
police. NIBRS, managed by the FBI, collects data on fatal and nonfatal crime incidents reported by
law enforcement agencies." The best single worked example for a lesson (Appendix Table 1): in 2024
the NCVS rate of violent crime excluding simple assault was **8.9 per 1,000** people 12 and over,
of which **5.3 per 1,000** was reported to police, while the NIBRS estimate of violent crime was
**3.7 per 1,000** residents. Same country, same year, three numbers, all correct. The notes explain
the denominators differ (persons 12+ against all residents) and that NIBRS includes crimes against
businesses, children under 12, and homeless and institutionalised people, which the NCVS cannot
reach. Property crime: NCVS 97.6 per 1,000 households, NIBRS 17.9 per 1,000 persons.

#### US-4. Council on Criminal Justice, *When Crime Statistics Diverge* (web report, 2025)
https://counciloncj.org/when-crime-statistics-diverge/. **Read in full** (curl). An independent,
non-partisan explainer, good to link. Key finding: [V] "The nation’s two major tools for measuring
crime, the UCR and the NCVS, show similar long-term trends for rates of serious violent crime, motor
vehicle theft, and burglary." It also records that the gap between the two sources' estimates of
the change from 2021 to 2022 (a 2% fall in the UCR, a 75% rise in the NCVS; Stage 4 correction,
lesson 02: not "2022 to 2024") [V] "became a heated point of political contention", and that the FBI revised figures in autumn
2024 [V] "without explanation". **Neutrality note:** US crime trends since 2020 are a live partisan
argument. The course should teach how to read the two sources and should not adjudicate whose
narrative was right.

#### US-5. National Research Council, *Estimating the Incidence of Rape and Sexual Assault* (2014)
https://nap.nationalacademies.org/read/18605/chapter/2. **Summary chapter read in full** (curl).
The authoritative account of why US rape estimates differ so much between surveys. Other surveys
(including the CDC's NISVS) measure higher levels; they [V] "differ in many ways, including the
definition used for rape; context in which data are collected; target population, sampling frame,
and sample size; and data collection mode, response rates". Conclusion 7-1: the NCVS [V] "does not
measure the low incidence events of rape and sexual assault with the precision needed for policy
and research purposes. Comparisons across subgroups and years are particularly problematic."
Conclusion 7-2 on "series victimizations" explains the year-to-year volatility. Use this to teach
why a lesson gives ranges and names the survey.

#### US-6. CDC, "About Intimate Partner Violence" (NISVS figures)
Wayback capture 20260921054056 of https://www.cdc.gov/intimate-partner-violence/about/index.html.
**Page read in full; the underlying NISVS 2023/2024 data brief (2026) was not read** (on the unread
line). Lifetime figures: [V] "More than 1 in 3 women (nearly 43.5 million) and more than 1 in 6 men
(20.7 million) experienced contact sexual violence, physical violence, and/or stalking by an
intimate partner during their lifetimes." And [V] "Data from from the National Violent Death
Reporting System suggests that about 1 in 5 homicide victims are killed by an intimate partner. The
reports also found that over half of female homicide victims are killed by a current or former male
intimate partner." (The doubled "from from" is in the source.) Stage 4 correction, lesson 03: the page's
reference 5 behind the homicide sentences is "Surveillance for Violent Deaths — National Violent
Death Reporting System, 27 States, 2015" [V], so those figures are **27 US states, 2015 deaths**,
not national and not current; label them so. These are **lifetime** prevalence and
broad definitions; the NCVS figures are **annual** and narrower. Do not set them side by side as if
they measured the same thing. Section 6 below covers this.

#### US-7. BJS, *Violent Victimization Committed by Strangers, 1993–2010* (Harrell, NCJ 239424, December 2012)
https://bjs.ojp.gov/content/pub/pdf/vvcs9310.pdf. **First four pages read** (highlights, Table 1,
trend text). Dated but the only BJS special report on strangers and still the best source for
*where* and *how*. Highlights, 2005 to 2010: strangers committed about 39% of nonfatal violence;
[V] "In 2005-10, strangers committed 62% of violent victimizations occurring in public places";
[V] "In 2005-10, about 9% of violent victimizations that occurred in victims’ homes were committed
by strangers"; strangers committed about a quarter of rapes and sexual assaults (Table 1: 23.7%,
26.1%, 24.1% across the three periods); and [V] "about 22% of victims of violence committed by
strangers were injured, compared to 31% of victims who knew the offender." Also: males were
victimized by strangers at about twice the female rate in 2010 (9.5 against 4.7 per 1,000). Say
the years when citing; the level has changed since, the pattern is the teachable part.

#### UK-1. ONS, *Crime in England and Wales: year ending March 2026* (bulletin, 23 July 2026)
https://www.ons.gov.uk/peoplepopulationandcommunity/crimeandjustice/bulletins/crimeinenglandandwales/yearendingmarch2026.
**Read in full** (curl). The current UK anchor.

- **What each source is for**, in ONS's own words, which a lesson can quote: [V] "Police recorded
  crime is not always a good indicator of general trends in crime, because of changes in how it is
  recorded over time. However, it can give more insight into lower-volume, higher-harm offences that
  are reported to the police". The CSEW is preferred for violence, theft, criminal damage, fraud and
  computer misuse; police data for homicide, weapons offences and robbery.
- **What the CSEW misses**: [V] "The survey excludes crimes against commercial or public sector
  bodies, tourists, or those living in communal establishments like care homes, student halls of
  residence, and prisons." It covers people 16 and over.
- **Reporting**: [V] "only an estimated 36% of CSEW headline crime incidents (excluding fraud and
  computer misuse) were reported to the police in the CSEW YE March 2026."
- **Headline crime**: 9.6 million CSEW incidents, of which **4.5 million were fraud**, 2.6 million
  theft, 798,000 computer misuse and **989,000 violence with or without injury**. So fraud is nearly
  half of all crime the survey measures, and violence about a tenth (my arithmetic). Fraud
  prevalence: 7.8% of adults. Violence is 78% below its 1995 peak.
- **Strangers, UK**: [V] "In YE March 2026, around 0.7% of people aged 16 years and over reported
  being a victim of violence, with or without injury, where the perpetrator was a stranger; 0.4%
  reported being a victim where the perpetrator was an acquaintance."
- **Domestic abuse** (latest, YE March 2025): 7.8% of adults 16+ in the last year, about 3.8 million.
  ONS says the interviewer-administered questions underestimate it; the self-completion module is
  the preferred measure.
- **Sexual victimisation**: 2.1% of adults (2.8% of women, 1.4% of men), on new questions **not
  comparable** with earlier years.
- **Homicide**: police recorded 499 homicides, 8.1 per million people, [V] "the lowest rate since
  current police recording practices began in YE March 2003."
- **Harassment**: 10.0% of adults; anti-social behaviour experienced or witnessed by 40.9%, a rise
  ONS says was [V] "inflated because of changes to how questions in the CSEW were asked from April
  2025." Another ready-made statistics lesson: a question change is not a crime wave.
- **Data quality**: [V] "Their accreditation was removed by the Office for Statistics Regulation
  (OSR) in January 2014 following an assessment that found evidence that the quality and consistency
  of the underlying data may not be reliable." (This refers to police recorded crime.) Note: the page
