  gives the Report Fraud launch as 4 December 2025 in one section and "4 December 2026" in another;
  the latter is an ONS typo.

#### UK-2. ONS, *The nature of violent crime in England and Wales: year ending March 2024* (26 September 2024)
https://www.ons.gov.uk/peoplepopulationandcommunity/crimeandjustice/articles/thenatureofviolentcrimeinenglandandwales/yearendingmarch2024.
**Read in full.** The best UK source on *who*:
- Men are more often victims of stranger violence: [V] "a higher proportion of men were a victim of
  violence with or without injury where the perpetrator was a stranger (1%) than women (0.4%)".
- Youth: highest victimisation at 16 to 24, stranger 2.2% and acquaintance 1.3%.
- Women are more often victims of domestic abuse (6.6% against 3.0%), stalking (4.0% against 2.3%),
  sexual assault (3.4% against 0.8%) and harassment (11.2% against 6.6%).
- Injury: [V] "The CSEW estimated that victims sustained a physical injury in 42% of violent
  incidents in YE March 2023. The most common type of injury experienced was minor bruising or black
  eye (25% of violent incidents). More serious injuries like cuts (7%) or broken bones (2%) were less
  common." Useful against catastrophising: most violence is minor.
- Police data from 18 forces: for female victims of violence 41% of suspects were intimate partners,
  against 22% for male victims.
- Stalking peaks at 16 to 19 (8.8%); harassment is higher for disabled people and for gay, lesbian
  and bisexual people. These are the groups a "who is at risk" lesson must not omit.

#### UK-3. ONS, *Homicide in England and Wales: year ending March 2025* (5 February 2026)
https://www.ons.gov.uk/peoplepopulationandcommunity/crimeandjustice/articles/homicideinenglandandwales/yearendingmarch2025.
**Sections 1 to 8 read in full.** From the Home Office Homicide Index (accredited). The clearest
statement anywhere of how the "known or stranger" answer depends on who the victim is:
- [V] "Adult female victims (defined as those aged 16 years and over) were most commonly killed by a
  partner or ex-partner (40%) or a family member (16%) in year ending (YE) March 2025. For adult
  males, the suspected killer was more commonly a friend or acquaintance (20%) or a stranger (18%)".
- [V] "Of all adult female victims, over half (56%) were a result of domestic homicides (75)."
- 522 victims, 8.6 per million, [V] "the lowest rate since 1977". Male rate 12.3 per million,
  female 5.0. Children under 1 have the highest single-year-of-age rate. Strangers killed eight
  children under 16 in the year.
- Circumstances: [V] "Around half (51%, 266 offences) of all homicide cases were thought to have
  resulted from a quarrel, a revenge attack or a loss of temper". Only 5% were in furtherance of
  theft or gain (Stage 4 correction, lesson 03: the source's words are [V] "Furtherance of theft or
  gain accounted for 5% of homicides (27 offences)"). The quarrel share was higher where the suspect
  was known to the victim (59%) than unknown (44%) [V, see "Gate closures, lesson 03"], so many of
  these quarrels are domestic: do not turn "half begin as quarrels" into de-escalation advice for an
  abusive relationship (reconciliation 10).
- Place: most in a residential setting. Stage 4 correction, lesson 03: Figure 8's data give female
  victims 81% in or around a house and **male victims 46% in or around a house**, 32% street,
  footpath or alleyway, 5% open outdoor, 5% licensed premises. "Men mostly in public" is ONS's chart
  title and holds only if the public categories are added up; the commonest single place for men is
  also a house.
- Alcohol and drugs: 35% of victims were thought to be under the influence.
- Method: sharp instrument 39%, the most common single weapon a kitchen knife.
- A caution ONS itself makes, and the course should copy: [V] "Demographic factors discussed in
  this section are not necessarily independently related to homicide." It points to socioeconomic
  factors at individual and neighbourhood level.

#### UK-4. ONS, *The nature of violent crime in England and Wales: year ending March 2018*
Same URL pattern, `.../yearendingmarch2018`. **Sections on alcohol, timing and location read.** The
most recent ONS narrative on *when and where* (later editions moved this into data tables, "Nature of
crime: violence"). Old, so cite with the year. [V] "Victims believed the perpetrator(s) to be under
the influence of alcohol in 39% (561,000) of violent incidents". [V] "more than half of violent
incidents (54%) occurred in the evening or during the night". [V] "the large majority of incidents
of domestic violence occurred around the home (78%), whereas incidents of stranger violence were
most likely to occur either around work (30%) or in the street (21%). Incidents of acquaintance
violence were most likely to take place at work". (Footnote markers removed; the source
continues "(40%, Figure 13)".) Stage 4 correction, lesson 03: "around work" does **not**
include pubs and clubs. Figure 13's notes define it as [V] "Includes work premises, whether inside or
outside or work garage or car parks." and pub or club separately as [V] "Includes pub or club
premises, whether inside or nearby street or car parks."; stranger violence in or near a pub or
club was 18.9% (Figure 13 data). See "Gate closures, lesson 03". A drafter wanting current figures should open the "Nature of crime: violence"
dataset (xlsx) on ONS.

#### UK-5. HMIC, *Crime-recording: making the victim count* (November 2014)
https://assets-hmicfrs.justiceinspectorates.gov.uk/uploads/crime-recording-making-the-victim-count.pdf.
**Summary and conclusions read** (126 pp; I read pp. 18 to 21 and the contents). The inspection that
confirmed the problem (Stage 4 correction, lesson 02: accreditation was removed in January 2014,
before this November 2014 report, and ONS attributes the removal to an OSR assessment). [V] "Over 800,000 crimes reported to the police have
gone unrecorded each year. This represents an under-recording of 19 percent. The problem is greatest
for victims of violence against the person and sexual offences, where the under-recording rates are
33 percent and 26 percent respectively." (Footnote marker removed. The extracted text reads
"underrecording" with the line-break hyphen lost; check the hyphen against the PDF.) This is why a rise in recorded violence after 2014 is not evidence
that violence rose: recording improved. Teach it as the textbook case.

#### UK-6. OSR, *Statement on statistics from the Crime Survey for England and Wales* (2 April 2026)
https://osr.statisticsauthority.gov.uk/guidance/osr-statement-on-statistics-from-the-crime-survey-for-england-and-wales/.
**Read in full.** The regulator answering three "misunderstandings", the third being that [V]
"Police recorded crime figures are inherently more accurate than CSEW estimates". It confirms: [V]
"Our 2024 review of the CSEW was undertaken following the temporary suspension of the accreditation
in July 2022 due to quality concerns, including a drop in the survey response rate." and [V] "In
2024, we reinstated the accredited official statistics status of the CSEW estimates." A drafter can
use this to show that the survey's own weaknesses are public and checked.

---

### 2. Repeat victimisation and concentration

#### RV-1. Pease, *Repeat Victimisation: Taking Stock* (Home Office Crime Detection and Prevention Series Paper 90, 1998)
https://popcenter.asu.edu/sites/g/files/litvpz3631/files/problems/domestic_violence/PDFs/Pease_1998.pdf.
**Summary and section 2 read** (pdftotext). The founding synthesis, still assigned (the UT Dallas
syllabus below assigns Farrell, Phillips and Pease 1995). Its conclusions: [V] "victimisation is the
best single predictor of victimisation; that when victimisation recurs it tends to do so quickly;
that high crime rates and hot spots are as they are substantially because of rates of repeat
victimisation; that a major reason for repetition is that offenders take later advantage of
opportunities which the first offence throws up". On the famous number, Pease himself warns: [V]
"The conclusion that 4% of people suffer 44% of crime is the sound-bite from Farrell and Pease
(1993) which seems to stick in people’s memory, but this kind of calculation is dependent upon
various counting conventions, and varies by place and time." For the course this is the most
practical finding in the whole literature: **if something has happened to you once, that is the
moment to change the circumstance that allowed it** (lock, route, the account that was breached),
because the risk of a repeat is highest soon after. It connects Part A's data to the planning
lessons.

---

### 3. Comparing crime with ordinary risks

All figures are from the official sources; a lesson must give the year and the geography, because
they differ.

#### R-1. CDC/NCHS FastStats pages (NVSS mortality data via CDC WONDER)
- "Accidents or Unintentional Injuries", Wayback 20260902230847 of
  https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/fastats/accidental-injury.htm. **Read in full.** [V] "Unintentional fall
  deaths Number of deaths: 48,308 Deaths per 100,000 population: 14.2"; motor vehicle traffic deaths
  41,241 (12.1 per 100,000); unintentional poisoning 75,761 (22.3); all unintentional injury 197,449
  (58.1). Source line: [V] "National Vital Statistics System – Mortality data (2024) via CDC WONDER".
- "Homicide", Wayback 20260101235813 of https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/fastats/homicide.htm. **Read in
  full.** [V] "All homicides Number of deaths: 22,830 Deaths per 100,000 population: 6.8", source
  year given as 2023. Firearm homicides 17,927. **Stage 4 note, lesson 01 (2026-09-25): superseded.**
  The live page (last reviewed 20 February 2026, read in a browser) now gives 2024 data: [V] "All
  homicides Number of deaths: 20,162 Deaths per 100,000 population: 5.9". Use **5.9 (2024)**; see
  "Gate closures, lesson 01".
- "Injuries" (same capture period): all firearm deaths 44,447, 13.1 per 100,000 (2024 label). Most
  US firearm deaths are suicides; a lesson comparing "gun deaths" with crime must not blur them.

**Stage 4 note, lesson 01 (2026-09-25):** with all three figures from 2024 (14.2, 12.1, 5.9), the
fall death rate is about 2.4 times the homicide rate, not "roughly twice", and the years no longer
mix. CDC's older-adult falls page gives the 65-and-over age-adjusted rate (78.4, 2024); see "Gate
closures, lesson 01". The paragraph below is kept as first written.

**The teachable comparison**: a US adult is roughly twice as likely to die in a fall as by homicide,
and about as likely to die on the road (14.2 and 12.1 against 6.8 per 100,000, mixing 2024 and 2023
data; recheck years). Falls are overwhelmingly deaths of older people (not established from this
page; check CDC's older-adult falls page before saying it), which matters because older people are
also the group most afraid of crime and least likely to suffer it.

#### R-2. NHTSA, 2025 early estimates and 2024 FARS (press release, 2026)
Wayback 20260906184145 of
https://www.nhtsa.gov/press-releases/traffic-deaths-2025-early-estimates-2024-annual. **Read in
full.** [V] "With an estimated 36,640 traffic fatalities in 2025—a 6.7% decrease from 2024—the
nation saw its second-lowest traffic fatality rate in recorded history at 1.10 fatalities per 100
million vehicle miles traveled." and [V] "NHTSA also released its annual traffic fatality data for
2024, reporting that 39,254 people died in traffic crashes in 2024." Note: the headline is framed
around the administration ("Trump's Transportation Department Announces Record-Low Traffic
Deaths"); quote the body, not the headline. NHTSA's FARS count (39,254) and CDC's NVSS count (41,241)
differ because they define a traffic death differently; that is itself a good example of "which
source?".

#### R-3. DfT, *Reported road casualties Great Britain, annual report: 2024* (25 September 2025)
https://www.gov.uk/government/statistics/reported-road-casualties-great-britain-annual-report-2024/reported-road-casualties-great-britain-annual-report-2024.
**Main points and chart text read.** [V] "1,602 fatalities, a decline of 1% compared to 2023";
[V] "in 2024, 76% of fatalities and 61% of casualties of all severities were male". **Geography
trap**: road statistics cover Great Britain (with Scotland); ONS crime and homicide cover England and
Wales only. Even allowing for that, roughly three people die on British roads for every one killed
by homicide in England and Wales (1,602 against 522). Say that with the caveat or not at all.

#### R-4. UK falls deaths: snippet and secondary only
The Centre for Ageing Better blog (https://ageing-better.org.uk/blogs/falls-continue-be-leading-cause-accidental-deaths,
read in full) reports RoSPA's figures: [V] "More than 23,000 people lost their lives because of
accidents in 2023" and [V] "Almost half (48%) of accidental deaths in 2023 resulted from falls."
That is a charity's summary of another charity's report. **A lesson needs the ONS mortality data
(ICD-10 W00–W19) or the NHS Digital "Mortality from accidental falls" compendium before stating a UK
falls figure.** RoSPA is on the unread line.

#### R-5. Fraud as the commonest crime
- **US**: FTC prepared statement to the Joint Economic Committee, 25 March 2026,
  https://www.ftc.gov/system/files/ftc_gov/pdf/ftc-testimony-jec-hearing-on-the-rising-scam-economy.pdf.
  **Pages 1 to 3 read.** [V] "In 2025, consumers submitted 3 million fraud reports to CSN and
  reported $15.9 billion in losses." And the reporting gap: [V] "When taking underreporting into
  account, the FTC has estimated that the overall cost of fraud to consumers for 2024 could be as
  high as $195.9 billion." Footnote 6 cites an FTC study that [V] "only 4.8 percent of victims of
  mass-market consumer fraud complained to a Better Business Bureau or a government entity".
- **UK**: CSEW fraud (UK-1): 4.5 million incidents, 7.8% of adults a victim in a year, nearly half
  of all crime the survey counts.
- **What people worry about agrees**: Gallup (F-5 below) found identity theft and scams are the only
  crimes most Americans say they worry about. Fear and risk line up here, which is worth pointing out
  in a course that otherwise spends time on where they diverge.

#### R-6. Warr on how to compare risks (see F-1)
Warr passes on the risk-communication advice of Covello, von Winterfeldt and Slovic (1987): [V]
"Avoid comparisons of risk that may appear to the audience to be noncomparable because of different
qualitative characteristics—for example, the risk of smoking compared to that of living near a
nuclear power plant." A lesson that says "you are more likely to die falling down the stairs than
being murdered, so stop worrying" commits exactly this error: violence is chosen by another person,
carries dread and moral injury, and is partly controllable by behaviour. Compare, but say why the
comparison is incomplete. This is also a point on which a learner who has been a victim will
rightly feel dismissed if the lesson is careless.

---

### 4. Fear of crime: the literature

#### F-1. Warr, "Fear of Crime in the United States: Avenues for Research and Policy" (*Criminal Justice 2000*, vol. 4, NIJ, pp. 451–489)
https://www.ojp.gov/criminal_justice2000/vol_4/04i.pdf. **Read in full** (public domain, a US
government publication). The single most useful source for the course's first lesson. Positions and
quotable lines:
- Fear is functional: [V] "Fear, then, is not intrinsically bad. It is when fear is out of proportion
  to objective risk that it becomes dysfunctional."
- Fear is risk times seriousness (Warr and Stafford): [V] "the degree of fear attached to particular
  crimes is a multiplicative function of the perceived seriousness and perceived risk of the
  offenses. To generate strong fear, an offense must be perceived as both serious and likely to
  occur." Hence burglary, not murder, topped the US fear rankings.
- People overestimate rare crimes and underestimate common ones: [V] "people tended to
  systematically overestimate the frequency of rare offenses while underestimating the frequency of
  common ones." They get the *order* right.
- The media mirror: [V] "the media are most likely to report precisely those crimes that are least
  likely to occur".
- The "magic dial" argument that reducing fear is not a pure good: [V] "Reducing fear, in other
  words, is not necessarily an unqualified good or cost-free; by relieving fear, we stand the chance
  of increasing public injury." (Extracted text reads "costfree"; the hyphen was lost at a line
  break, check it against the PDF.) And the other end: [V] "At the extreme is a “fortress society” in
  which citizens withdraw from public life altogether".
- Against calling fear "irrational": [V] "When applied to fear of crime, however, the concept of
  rationality is an ill-advised notion." (PDF hyphenates "ill-advised" across a line.) Because
  fear depends on what the person values: [V] "They are in no position, however, to tell people the
  value they should place on the elements of their lives and how much they should fear their loss or
  destruction."
- Perceptually contemporaneous offences: [V] "What for men is the perceived risk of robbery would for
  many women be the perceived risk of robbery, plus rape, plus additional injury". And a
  correction the course can offer as reassurance: [V] "burglaries normally occur when no one is
  home".
- The commonest precaution is avoidance: [V] "the single most common reaction to fear of crime in
  the United States is spatial avoidance; that is, staying away from places that are perceived to
  be dangerous". And the fear industry: [V] "There are entire industries in the United States that
  rely on fear of crime to sell products and services." Pair that with the gadget and app lessons.

#### F-2. Gray, Jackson and Farrall, "Reassessing the Fear of Crime" (*European Journal of Criminology* 5(3), 2008, 363–380)
LSE accepted manuscript, https://eprints.lse.ac.uk/20944/1/Reassessing_the_fear_of_crime_(LSERO).pdf.
**Read in full.** The British measurement critique. Standard survey questions ("How worried are you
about...?") inflate fear; asking how *often* shrinks it. From the abstract: [V] "The findings show
that few people experience specific events of worry on a frequent basis, and that ‘old’ style
questions magnify the everyday experience of fear. We propose that ‘worry about crime’ is often best
seen as a diffuse anxiety about risk, rather than any pattern of everyday concerns over personal
safety." The robbery numbers from the 2003/04 British Crime Survey: about a third said they were
very or fairly worried about being robbed, yet [V] "85% of respondents said that they had not
worried about being robbed in the past year, and only 5% of all respondents had worried once a
month or more." It summarises the Farrall and Gadd (2004) pilot (not read directly): 37% had felt
fearful in the past year, 13% more than five times. **Accepted manuscript**: check quoted wording
against the SAGE version before a lesson quotes it.

#### F-3. Lee, Jackson and Ellis, "Functional and Dysfunctional Fear of Crime in Inner Sydney" (*ANZ Journal of Criminology* 53(3), 2020)
LSE accepted manuscript, https://eprints.lse.ac.uk/103456/1/Final.Functional_DisfunctionalFearofCrime_resubmission.pdf.
**Abstract and conceptual section read.** States the "functional fear" position, which is the
course's philosophy in one idea: worry that [V] "motivates socially beneficial behaviour" and allows
[V] "‘adaptive vigilance and routine precaution’" (quoting Gray et al. 2011), against worry that is
frequent, hard to stop and impairs daily life. Its finding that [V] "gender and age are largely
unrelated to worry about crime, controlling for perceptions of community disorder, perceptions of
collective efficacy, direct victimisation experience and indirect victimisation experience" is one
Sydney sample (n=409); use it as an illustration, not a law.

#### F-4. Scott, "Stranger Danger: Explaining Women's Fear of Crime" (*Western Criminology Review* 4(3), 2003, 203–214)
https://www.westerncriminology.org/documents/WCR/v04n3/article_pdfs/scott.pdf. **Abstract and
literature review read** (open access). Useful mainly as a fair summary of the paradox debate and of
Stanko's feminist critique, which I could not read directly. Scott reports Stanko's argument that
[V] "conventional criminology tends to look at street crime and not crimes happening behind closed
doors between non-strangers and thereby undermines the detection of crimes of violence against
women." Her own finding, from the 1993 Statistics Canada Violence Against Women Survey: fear is
best explained by [V] "women having had negative experiences with strangers. Negative experiences
include being followed, receiving unwanted attention, and having received obscene phone calls."

#### F-5. Gallup, "Crime in U.S. Seen as Less Serious for Second Straight Year" (October 2025 poll)
https://news.gallup.com/poll/697124/crime-seen-less-serious-second-straight-year.aspx. **Read in
full.** 49% said crime had increased in the past year, the lowest since at least 2018; 31% would be
afraid to walk alone at night within a mile of home (40% in 2023, highest since 1993). On worries:
[V] "Overall, Americans worry most about being the victim of identity theft (69%) and being tricked
into providing financial information to scammers (53%). These are the only crimes included in the
survey about which majorities say they worry frequently or occasionally." Partisanship: when a
respondent's party holds the White House they are [V] "generally less likely to report an increase in
crime". Margin of error ±4 points (n=1,000). Neutrality: report the symmetry, which Gallup describes
as occurring for both parties over time, and do not single out one party.

#### F-6. Gallup, "Exploring the Local Positivity Bias in Crime Perceptions" (2025)
https://news.gallup.com/opinion/polling-matters/698624/exploring-local-positivity-bias-crime-perceptions.aspx.
**Read in full.** [V] "The local-national gap has been evident in every poll since Gallup’s crime
series began in 2000. Americans’ views of the seriousness of crime nationwide over this 25-year
period have averaged 43 percentage points higher than their views of local crime." Mechanism
offered: local views rest on direct experience, national ones on [V] "dramatic events and broader
narratives." This is an opinion piece, not a study; cite as such. **Stage 4 note, lesson 01
(2026-09-25):** the author is not "a Gallup editor". The byline is Frank Newport, and the page's
author note says [V] "Frank Newport, Ph.D., is a Gallup Senior Scientist."

#### F-7. ONS, *Public perceptions of crime in England and Wales: year ending March 2016*
https://www.ons.gov.uk/peoplepopulationandcommunity/crimeandjustice/articles/publicperceptionsofcrimeinenglandandwales/yearendingmarch2016.
**Read in full.** The UK version of the same gap: 60% thought crime had gone up nationally (84% in
2008/09) while the survey showed falls; [V] "In general, the survey shows that individuals’
perceptions of crime on a national level do not typically match well to reality." And [V] "When
individuals are asked questions about crime in their immediate local area, perceptions seem to match
reality more closely." ONS adds a fair caveat that the survey does not cover every crime people may
be thinking of. Old (2016); ONS has not repeated this analysis in narrative form that I found.

#### F-8. The textbook positions on the "fear–victimisation paradox" (not read directly)
Named here so the drafter knows the terrain; all on the unread line. **Ferraro, *Fear of Crime:
Interpreting Victimization Risk* (SUNY, 1995)** and **Ferraro, "Women's Fear of Victimization:
Shadow of Sexual Assault?" (*Social Forces* 75, 1996)**: the "shadow" hypothesis that fear of rape
raises women's fear of every other crime (Warr's F-1 lines are the citable statement of it).
**Hale, "Fear of Crime: A Review of the Literature" (*International Review of Victimology* 4, 1996)**:
the standard review. **Stanko** (1990s): women's fear is a reasonable response to hidden and
everyday victimisation. **Pain (1995, *BJC*)** on older women. **Ferraro and LaGrange (1987)**:
the "walk alone at night" question measures perceived risk in a place many older people never go,
so it manufactures part of the paradox. **Collins (2016, *Journal of Criminal Justice*)**: a
meta-analysis of fear-of-crime correlates. A drafter wanting to quote any of these must read them.

---

### 5. What the field considers essential (for Part A's lessons)

In the order they build:

1. **Two ways to count crime**, police records and victim surveys, and why they differ: not every
   crime is reported (US about half of violence; England and Wales about a third of survey crime);
   not every report is recorded (HMIC 19%); recording rules change (hierarchy rule, 2014 recording
   reforms, 2025 robbery clarification); surveys miss homicide, businesses, children and
   institutions; surveys have sampling error and response problems.
2. **Rates, counts and prevalence.** Per 1,000 persons 12+ (NCVS), per 100,000 inhabitants (FBI),
   per million (ONS homicide), percentage of adults (CSEW prevalence). Incidents against victims.
   Always ask: per how many, of whom, over what period.
3. **Who is actually at risk of violence**: the young (16 to 24 in the UK, 18 to 24 in the US), men
   for stranger and public violence, women for partner violence, sexual violence and stalking, the
   poor, the unmarried and separated, urban residents, people with disabilities, repeat victims.
   Older people have the lowest rates of violent victimisation and the highest rates of fall deaths.
4. **From whom**: it depends on the crime and on the victim's sex. Women who are killed are most
   often killed by a partner or ex-partner; men who are killed are most often killed by a friend,
   acquaintance or stranger; robbery is the stranger crime; nonfatal assault is a large stranger
   share in surveys and a small one in police data.
5. **Where and when**: violence clusters in evenings, nights and weekends, around alcohol and the
   night-time economy, and in public for men; domestic violence is at home; half of homicides
   start as quarrels.
6. **The commonest crime is fraud**, then theft; violence is a small share, and most violence
   causes minor or no injury.
7. **Fear is not the same as risk**, is shaped by seriousness and by media, is often less frequent
   than surveys suggest, and can be functional. The goal is calibrated fear, not zero fear.
8. **Ordinary risks** (falls, roads, poisoning) kill more people than crime does, with Warr's
   caution on how to make that comparison honestly.
9. **Repeat victimisation**: past victimisation is the best predictor, and the risk is highest soon
   after.

### 6. Common misconceptions (as the sources name them)

- **"Crime is rising"** as a default belief. Gallup: majorities said so in most years since 2000;
  ONS: 84% believed national crime rose in 2008/09. Long-run data in both countries show large
  falls since the mid-1990s (CSEW violence down 78% from its 1995 peak). OSR and ONS say national
  perceptions "do not typically match well to reality".
- **"Most violence is committed by strangers"** and its mirror, **"most violence is committed by
  people you know."** Each is true for some crimes and victims and false for others. Say which.
- **"Police figures are the real numbers"** (OSR Misunderstanding 3) and **"survey figures are just
  opinion"** (OSR Misunderstanding 1).
- **"Recorded crime went up, so crime went up."** HMIC 2014 and the ONS bulletins: recording changes
  drove much of the rise in recorded violence after 2014.
- **"Burglars attack people at home"**: Warr, citing Miethe and McCorkle, that burglaries normally
  happen when nobody is home.
- **"Women and older people are irrationally afraid."** Warr rejects "rationality" as the frame;
  Stanko and Scott argue hidden and everyday victimisation explains much of the gap. Contested; see
  section 7.
- **"A single year's figure is the trend."** NCVS rape reporting 46% then 24%; CSEW anti-social
  behaviour inflated by a question change; homicide fluctuations ONS warns about.
- **"A lifetime figure and an annual figure are the same kind of number"** (NISVS lifetime IPV
  against NCVS annual rates).

### 7. Contested questions and the positions on each

**7.1 Is the fear–victimisation paradox real?** (Contested empirical question, 3.1 kind 2.)
- *Paradox as found*: groups least victimised (women, older people) report most fear. Warr and
  Ferraro explain the gender part by perceived seriousness and the "shadow of sexual assault"; this
  does not call women's fear wrong, it explains its structure.
- *Paradox as artifact*: Ferraro and LaGrange (via F-8, unread) and Gray, Jackson and Farrall (F-2)
  argue the standard questions measure diffuse anxiety or perceived risk, not fear events; better
  questions shrink the gap. Lee, Jackson and Ellis (F-3) find age and sex largely unrelated to worry
  after controls.
- *Paradox as undercount*: Stanko and others (via Scott, F-4) argue women's victimisation is hidden
  from crime surveys and street-crime framing, and everyday harassment is real risk information.
  The CSEW's own admission that interviewer questions underestimate domestic abuse, and the NRC's
  conclusion that the NCVS likely underestimates rape, give this position official support.
- *Precaution explanation*: lower victimisation may partly be the product of the fear itself
  (avoidance works). Warr raises the general point that those most affected by a fear may rarely
  feel it because they avoid its source. I found no direct test of this for crime; say it is a
  hypothesis.
- What would settle it: repeated, event-based measures of fear alongside self-completion
  victimisation measures in the same sample. Status: genuinely open; the course should present all
  four and not pick.

**7.2 What share of violence is by strangers?** (Contested only in the sense that answers depend on
source; the facts are established within each source.) NCVS: about 46% of nonfatal violence (2024).
NIBRS: 13.5% of assaults, 16.5% of homicides, 42.5% of robberies. CSEW: stranger violence 0.7% of
adults, acquaintance 0.4%, domestic separately measured and higher. ONS Homicide Index: 18% of adult
male victims, far fewer women. Teach the method, not a single number.

**7.3 Is US crime rising or falling since 2020?** (Politically contested; the data questions are
empirical.) CCJ documents the NCVS–UCR divergence and the political fight over it. The course should
teach how to read both and let the learner check the latest releases, and should never state a
partisan conclusion. BJS and FBI agree on long-term declines.

**7.4 Should safety teaching reduce fear or tolerate it?** (Partly a value question.) Warr's dial:
less fear can mean less precaution; more fear means a smaller life. The functional-fear school
(F-3) says aim for fear that prompts proportionate precaution without harming quality of life.
Critics of "safety advice" argue it can shade into blaming victims for others' choices; Karmen's
textbook has a chapter titled "Victims' Contributions to the Crime Problem", which signals how
long-standing and sensitive the victim-precipitation debate is (chapter unread). The course should
state plainly that responsibility for a crime lies with the offender, and that precautions change
odds, not blame. That sentence is a value commitment the institute can make openly; it is consistent
with every position above.

### 8. Canonical textbooks (tables of contents only; all on the unread line)

- **Karmen, *Crime Victims: An Introduction to Victimology*, 10th ed. (Cengage, 2020).** The most
  widely assigned US victimology text. TOC [T] (Cengage Asia page via WebFetch): 1 What Is
  Victimology?; 2 The Rediscovery of Crime Victims; 3 Victimization in the United States: An
  Overview; 4 Violent Crimes: Murders and Robberies; 5 Victims' Contributions to the Crime Problem;
  6–7 Victims and the Criminal Justice System (police; prosecutors, courts, corrections); 8
  Victimized Children; 9 Victims of Violence by Lovers and Family Members; 10 Victims of Rapes and
  Other Sexual Assaults; 11 Additional Groups of Victims with Special Problems; 12 Repaying Victims;
  13 Victims in the Twenty-First Century. Chapters 3 and 5 bear on this part.
- **Doerner and Lab, *Victimology*, 10th ed. (Routledge, 2025).** TOC read from routledge.com
  (curl): Part I Definition and Scope (1 The Scope of Victimology; 2 Measuring Criminal
  Victimization); Part II Addressing the Impact (3 Costs; 4 Remedying the Impact; 5 Restorative
  Justice; 6 Victim Rights); Part III Types (7 Traditional Crimes; 8 Sexual Battery; 9 Intimate
  Partner Violence; 10 Child Maltreatment; 11 Crime and the Elderly; 12 Hate Crime; 13 Victimization
  at School; 14 Victimization at Work). Chapter 2 is the measurement chapter.
- **Lab, *Crime Prevention: Approaches, Practices, and Evaluations*, 11th ed. (Routledge, 2023).**
  TOC read from routledge.com (curl). Chapter 1 is "Crime and the Fear of Crime", then Crime
  Prevention, Evaluation; Part I Primary Prevention (Physical Environment, Community, Mass Media,
  Developmental, General Deterrence); Part II Secondary (Prediction, **Situational Crime
  Prevention**, **Displacement and Diffusion**, Partnerships, Substance Use, Schools); Part III
  Tertiary (Specific Deterrence and Incapacitation, Rehabilitation). The standard crime-prevention
  text; its situational chapters feed Part B's avoidance lessons.
- **Felson and Eckert, *Crime and Everyday Life: A Brief Introduction*, 6th ed. (Sage, 2018).**
  TOC [T] (Sage page via WebFetch): 1 Eight Fallacies About Crime; 2 The Chemistry for Crime; 3
  Offenders Make Decisions; 4 Bringing Crime to You; 5 Teenage Crime; 6 Big Gang Theory; 7 How
  Crime Multiplies; 8 Situational Crime Prevention; 9 Local Design Against Crime; 10 The Age of
  Exposure. Routine activity theory for general readers: a likely offender, a suitable target, and
  no capable guardian. The foundational paper is **Cohen and Felson (1979), *American Sociological
  Review* 44** (unread), with **Hindelang, Gottfredson and Garofalo (1978)** for lifestyle-exposure
  theory (unread). These two theories are what a victimology course teaches as "why some people
  are victimised more", and the course should name them.
- **Walklate (ed.), *Handbook of Victims and Victimology*** (Willan 2007; 2nd ed. Routledge 2018):
  the UK reference, on reading lists at Staffordshire (module descriptor, snippet only). Not read.

### 9. University syllabi consulted

- **UT Dallas, CRIM 6308 Victimology (Dr Michelle N. Harris, Spring 2024)**,
  https://dox.utdallas.edu/syl142097. **Read in full** (8 pp PDF). Sequence: introduction;
  **measurement** (NCVS, UCR, NIBRS *and the Crime Survey for England and Wales*, with an assignment
  to tabulate how they differ); theories (Hindelang et al. 1978; Cohen and Felson 1979; Miethe and
  Meier 1990; Pratt and Turanovic 2016); risk factors (self-control, alcohol: Felson and Burchfield
  2004); **revictimisation** (Farrell, Phillips and Pease 1995); trauma; victim services; sexual
  victimisation; **fear of crime** (Ferraro and LaGrange 1987; LaGrange, Ferraro and Supancic 1992;
  Ferraro 1996; Lane, Gover and Dahod 2009); intimate partner violence; special populations;
  **prevention** (Clarke 1997 on situational prevention; Felson; Coker et al. 2011 on Green Dot
  bystander intervention; Piza et al. 2019 CCTV meta-analysis). Notable choices: it opens with
  measurement before theory, and it puts US and UK surveys side by side, exactly this course's
  convention. It is a graduate seminar with no textbook.
- **UConn Police, "Women's Self Defense Classes: Surviving Violent Encounters"**,
  https://police.universitysafety.uconn.edu/education-and-programs/self-defense-classes/. **Read in
  full.** Not an academic course, but the shape of the typical campus programme: [V] "This
  comprehensive course progresses from awareness, prevention, risk reduction, and avoidance to basic
  self-defense participation." Twelve hours minimum, and the department [V] "cannot offer
  self-defense classes of fewer hours than listed." Useful to Part C on what a class covers.
- College "self-defense and personal safety" kinesiology courses (Citrus College KIN 104, West
  Valley KINS 6.03, Skyline COMB 401.1) appeared in search with catalog descriptions only (snippet
  only). A fuller Austin Community College syllabus link returned 404. I found no university course
  that teaches *personal safety* from the crime data, which suggests this course's Part A framing is
  unusual; that is a strength, but it means no syllabus to borrow the sequence from beyond the
  victimology ones.

### 10. Best free resources to link

- BJS **NCVS Dashboard (N-DASH)**, https://ncvs.bjs.ojp.gov, for learners to query rates themselves.
- FBI **Crime Data Explorer**, https://cde.ucr.cjis.gov.
- BJS, *The Nation's Two Crime Measures* (US-3), a four-page explainer.
- Council on Criminal Justice, *When Crime Statistics Diverge* (US-4).
- ONS **Crime in England and Wales** bulletins (quarterly) and the *User guide to crime statistics
  for England and Wales* (named in UK-1; not read).
- ONS *Homicide in England and Wales* (annual).
- police.uk crime maps (not reviewed here; Part B may).
- Warr (F-1), free on ojp.gov, readable by a motivated beginner.
- Open University OpenLearn, "The meaning of crime" (free course; description seen in search only,
  not reviewed).

### 11. Safety-critical notes for drafters

- **Never tell a learner their fear is irrational.** Warr, the functional-fear school and the
  feminist critique all agree on this, from different directions. Offer the numbers as
  information, not as a verdict on the reader.
- **Never compare a victim's risk to falling downstairs as a way of dismissing it** (R-6).
- **Domestic abuse and stalking are the risks the data say are most underestimated and most often
  come from someone known.** Part A should hand off to the planning and help lessons and to First
  Aid lesson 16 ("Where to get support") rather than teach support services itself.
- **Race and ethnicity**: ONS and BJS publish victim and offender figures by ethnicity. The course
  does not need them to teach personal safety, and ONS's own caveat about confounding makes a bare
  number misleading. Leave them out; if a reviewer insists, include ONS's caveat verbatim.
- First Aid lesson 01 already teaches the bystander literature (Genovese and after) and lesson 16
  teaches support after an emergency. Part A should point to both, not repeat them.

### 12. Open concerns about scope

1. **The data change every year and some series are mid-transition** (NCVS redesign; CSEW new
   sexual-victimisation questions; UK fraud recording moved to Report Fraud with police data
   suspended). A lesson should teach where to look and how to read, and give dated examples,
   rather than rest its argument on one year's numbers. Put the date next to every figure.
2. **Two countries, three geographies.** England and Wales for crime, Great Britain for roads,
   Scotland and Northern Ireland separate for both. The course says "UK" in its outcomes; Part A
   can only support "England and Wales" for crime. Either say so in the lessons or add Police
   Scotland and PSNI sources later.
3. **Fraud is the commonest crime and a physical-safety course may underweight it.** The course
   description is about awareness, de-escalation and escape. The data argue for at least a section
   on scams and account security; check the outline gives it a home.
4. **The known-versus-stranger message must be split by sex and crime type**, or it will mislead
   half the audience. A lesson that tells men "most violence is by people you know" is wrong for
   them; one that tells women "stranger attacks are rare" is right on the numbers but must be
   written so it does not dismiss street harassment, which the CSEW shows is common.
5. **Victim-blaming risk.** Every lesson built on this data (who is at risk, what raises risk) must
   say, once and plainly, that responsibility lies with the offender.

---

## Part B: Awareness and avoidance

*Stage 1 research, 2026-09-25, one of four parts. Scope: what situational awareness is and what the
evidence says about it (including the victim-selection and gait research); routine activity theory
and situational crime prevention as the frame for avoidance; the home security evidence; street and
travel guidance from official bodies and what stands behind it; the online and fraud basics that
belong in a personal safety course; domestic abuse and stalking safety planning resources; and the
common myths. All dates read are 2026-09-25.*

**How the quotation marks work in this part.** **[V]** means copied from text I extracted myself:
curl with an HTML stripper, pdftotext on a PDF, the PubMed E-utilities API, or an Internet Archive
capture (snapshot ID given). There are no **[T]** quotations in this part: I did not use the
summarising fetch tool for any quoted wording. Where a fact came only from a search result snippet,
it is labelled **snippet only** and nothing from it is in quotation marks. Two items were read from
two-column PDFs through pdftotext, where the column interleaving had to be undone by eye; those are
marked **[V, two-column]** and the sentence should be re-read against the PDF before a lesson
quotes it.

**Overlap with the other parts, and with First Aid.** Part A presumably owns "reading a crime
statistic" and the who-is-at-risk data. I have used some of the same ONS and BJS tables here only
where they bear directly on an avoidance claim or a myth (who strangers attack, where rape happens,
how burglars get in), and a drafter should merge these with Part A rather than teach them twice.
First Aid lesson 16 already teaches the handover to emergency services, "someone in crisis" (988,
Samaritans, the RCUK and ERC suicide steps) and where to get support after an emergency; Personal
Safety should link to it for anything medical or about the aftermath, and not repeat it. First Aid
lesson 01 already teaches the bystander research (Kitty Genovese, Darley and Latané, the Philpot
2019 CCTV study) and Good Samaritan law; this course should point there when it talks about asking
passers-by for help.

---

### 1. Frames: routine activity theory and situational crime prevention

These two ideas are the spine of the whole avoidance half of the course. They are established
criminology (3.1 kind 1 as descriptions of how opportunity works; the size of the effect in any
given setting is an empirical question).

#### B1. Cohen, L. E. and Felson, M. (1979). "Social change and crime rate trends: a routine activity approach." *American Sociological Review* 44(4): 588-608.
- **Read:** full text, from a JSTOR-watermarked copy on a University of Washington course page
  (faculty.washington.edu/matsueda/courses/587/readings/). The abstract and the theoretical
  sections read closely; the data tables skimmed.
- **What it establishes:** the triangle every personal safety course leans on. The abstract:
  "Most criminal acts require convergence in space and time of likely offenders, suitable targets
  and the absence of capable guardians against crime." [V] And the move away from the offender:
  "Rather than emphasizing the characteristics of offenders, with this approach we concentrate upon
  the circumstances in which they carry out predatory criminal acts." [V] Their central hypothesis
  was that "the dispersion of activities away from households and families increases the
  opportunity for crime and thus generates higher crime rates." [V]
- **Use in the course:** the three elements map onto the three things a reader can change: be a
  less suitable target (fewer valuables on show, locked door), add guardianship (company, a friend
  who knows your route, neighbours, lighting), and change the convergence (routes, times, routines).
  It also explains why this is not about predicting who is a criminal, which keeps the course away
  from profiling strangers by appearance.

#### B2. Clarke, R. V. (ed.) (1997). *Situational Crime Prevention: Successful Case Studies*, 2nd ed., Introduction.
- **Read:** the Introduction, from the Center for Problem-Oriented Policing's PDF
  (popcenter.asu.edu/sites/g/files/litvpz3631/files/scp2_intro_0_0.pdf): definition, displacement,
  and "Philosophical and Ethical Issues" read closely; the case studies not read.
- **Definition:** "Situational prevention comprises opportunity-reducing measures that (1) are
  directed at highly specific forms of crime, (2) involve the management, design or manipulation of
  the immediate environment in as systematic and permanent way as possible, (3) make crime more
  difficult and risky, or less rewarding and excusable as judged by a wide range of offenders." [V]
- **Displacement:** Clarke says the assumption that offenders simply go elsewhere "is not
  supported by empirical research which has generally found rather little displacement." [V]
- **The objections, from three directions, in his own summary.** This is useful for the neutrality
  audit because it shows the critique is not from one side. From the Right, situational prevention
  is an "irrelevant response to crime because it neglects issues of moral culpability and
  punishment" [V]; from the Left, it is "politically and socially naive in its neglect of the role
  of social and economic inequities in causation" [V]; liberals say that "by "tinkering" with
  symptoms it diverts attention from the need to tackle the "root causes" of crime" [V]. He also
  names the "blaming the victim" charge (citing Karmen, 1984) and the fear of a "fortress society".
  A lesson that uses the situational frame should say what it does not claim: it is about lowering
  your own risk, not a theory of why people offend or a judgement on anyone who was harmed.

#### B3. Center for Problem-Oriented Policing, "Twenty Five Techniques of Situational Prevention" (grid, Cornish and Clarke 2003).
- **Read:** full (one page PDF,
  popcenter.asu.edu/sites/g/files/litvpz3631/files/twenty_five_techniques_of_situational_prevention.pdf).
- **What it gives:** five columns (Increase the Effort, Increase the Risks, Reduce the Rewards,
  Reduce Provocations, Remove Excuses), 25 techniques. The personal-scale examples are the ones a
  lesson can borrow: under "6. Extend guardianship" the grid lists "Take routine precautions: go out
  in group at night, leave signs of occupancy, carry phone" [V]; "11. Conceal targets" and
  "12. Remove targets" for valuables; "Reduce Provocations" is the bridge to the de-escalation part
  of the course (Part C, presumably).

#### B4. Guerette, R. T. and Bowers, K. J. (2009). "Assessing the extent of crime displacement and diffusion of benefits: a review of situational crime prevention evaluations." *Criminology* 47(4).
- **Read:** snippet only (the paper is paywalled at Wiley; academia.edu copy did not download).
  On the unread line.
- **What the snippets say (do not quote):** 102 evaluations, 574 observations; displacement in
  about a quarter of observations and diffusion of benefits in about the same share; net reductions
  overall. If a lesson wants the number, someone must read the paper first. Clarke's Introduction
  (B2) is the citable statement that displacement is usually small.

#### B5. Endsley, M. R. (1995). "Toward a theory of situation awareness in dynamic systems." *Human Factors* 37(1): 32-64.
- **Read:** snippet only. On the unread line.
- **Why it matters:** "situational awareness" in self-defence writing is borrowed from aviation and
  human factors, where Endsley's three levels (perceive the elements, understand what they mean,
  project what happens next) are the standard definition. A lesson may use the three levels as a
  teaching frame if it cites Endsley after someone has read at least the article's opening
  definition. The point worth making is that in its home field, situational awareness is about
  noticing and anticipating, not about fear or scanning for villains, and that it degrades under
  load (which is where B11 comes in).

---

### 2. Situational awareness and victim selection: what the evidence says

This is the most over-sold area in popular self-defence writing, so it needs the most care. The
claim usually made is "predators pick victims by how they walk, so walk confidently and you will not
be picked." The evidence supports a narrower claim.

#### B6. Grayson, B. and Stein, M. I. (1981). "Attracting assault: victims' nonverbal cues." *Journal of Communication* 31(1): 68-75.
- **Read:** the ERIC abstract (eric.ed.gov, EJ238367) and extended secondary accounts in B7 and B8.
  **The full text was not read** (Wiley/OUP paywall; a Scribd copy exists but was not used). On the
  unread line as "Attracting Assault full text".
- **ERIC abstract:** "Describes a study in which prison inmates convicted of assault identified
  potential victims from videotapes. A lab analysis code was used to determine which nonverbal body
  movement categories differentiated victims and nonvictims." [V]
- **The paper's own line, as quoted in Gunns et al. 2002 (B7), with page:** "potential victims may
  be signaling their vulnerability to would-be assailants through gestures, posture and exaggerated
  movement" (Grayson and Stein 1981, p. 68). [V, via B7]
- **The cues, as summarised in Wheeler's thesis (B8):** those judged most vulnerable "displayed
  longer or shorter strides, and weight that shifted up/down, forward/back, or side-to-side. They
  also tended to use only one side or part of their body when moving, and tended to lift their
  feet." [V, via B8; the thesis OCR reads "forwardiback", corrected here]
- **Details commonly repeated but not verified here:** that about 60 pedestrians were filmed in a
  high-assault area of New York and that a small group of inmates rated them. These come from search
  snippets. A lesson must not give the sample sizes until someone reads the paper.

#### B7. Gunns, R. E., Johnston, L. and Hudson, S. M. (2002). "Victim selection and kinematics: a point-light investigation of vulnerability to attack." *Journal of Nonverbal Behavior* 26(3): 129-158.
- **Read:** full text (a PDF hosted on a University of Rijeka course page, ffri.hr). Abstract,
  introduction and general discussion read closely.
- **What it establishes:** with only moving points of light at the joints (no clothes, faces or
  bodies visible), raters could sort walkers by "ease of attack", and specific walking features
  predicted the ratings; clothing and footwear changed the ratings by changing the walk. Abstract:
  "Both female (Experiment 1) and male (Experiment 2) walkers could be differentiated according to
  ease-of-attack based solely on the kinematic information provided whilst walking." [V]
- **The limit, in the authors' words, which a lesson must carry:** "It is worth noting that in the
  reported research, participants were making ratings of ease-of-attack rather than actually
  attempting such assaults." [V] And: "In order to conclude that perceivers were accurate in their
  perceptions of vulnerability it would be necessary to demonstrate that those individuals rated as
  easiest to attack were actually easiest to attack." [V] They also note raters were told to judge
  ease of attack, and "Whether they would spontaneously attend to information specifying
  ease-of-attack is unknown." [V]

#### B8. Wheeler, S. (2010). *The Convergence of Psychopathy, Self-Rated Vulnerability, and Other-Rated Vulnerability.* MA thesis, Brock University.
- **Read:** literature review (pp. 2-9) and the discussion of body language (pp. 36-38) in full,
  from Brock's repository (brocku.scholaris.ca). The results chapters skimmed.
- **Why it is useful:** it is the clearest short review of the whole line (Grayson and Stein 1981;
  Montepare and Zebrowitz-McArthur 1988; Murzynski and Degelman 1996; Gunns et al. 2002; Johnston et
  al. 2004; Wheeler, Book and Costello 2009) with a table of which cue each study found. And it states
  the central weakness of the early work plainly: Grayson and Stein "had recorded actual gaits of real
  targets, [but] their study design did not include a comparison of ratings with target demographics
  either" [V, bracketed word added for grammar], and the same design "was employed by Gunns et al.
  (2002) and Johnston et al. (2004)". [V] That is: most of this literature measures whether people
  *look* vulnerable to raters, not whether the people who look vulnerable are actually victimised more.
- It reports Johnston et al. (2004) as finding "a marked decrease in vulnerable movement cues when
  participants merely imagined walking in an unsafe environment." [V, secondary] Johnston et al.
  itself is on the unread line; snippets say its training effect faded over time, which must be
  checked before a lesson says gait can be trained.

#### B9. Book, A., Costello, K. and Camilleri, J. A. (2013). "Psychopathy and victim selection: the use of gait as a cue to vulnerability." *Journal of Interpersonal Violence* 28(11): 2368-2383. PMID 23422847.
- **Read:** abstract (PubMed API). Wheeler, Book and Costello (2009, *Criminal Justice and
  Behavior*), the student-sample precursor, read only through the thesis (B8) and this abstract.
- **What it adds:** the offender side. Forty-seven maximum-security inmates in Ontario rated
  walkers. "Higher Factor 1 psychopathy scores (as measured by the PCL-R; Hare 2003) were positively
  related to accuracy in judging victim vulnerability." [V] **Stage 4 note, lesson 04
  (2026-09-25):** the abstract does not say what "accuracy" was scored against; an earlier gloss
  here ("agreement with the walkers' own reports of past victimisation") is not in it and is struck.
  Wheeler's thesis (p. 20, outside what B8 read) says her 2009 student study scored accuracy that
  way; do not transfer that to this study. Also in the abstract: "Forty-seven inmates viewed short
  video clips of targets walking and judged how vulnerable each target was to victimization." [V]

#### B10. Fulham, L., Book, A. S., Blais, J., Ritchie, M. B., Gauthier, N. Y. and Costello, K. (2020). "The effect of hypervigilance on the relationship between sexual victimization and gait." *Journal of Interpersonal Violence* 35(19-20): 4061-4082. PMID 29294783.
- **Read:** abstract (PubMed API).
- **What it adds:** gait was associated with victimisation history "but only in the Unaware
  condition" [V], i.e. when people did not know they were being filmed; and "hypervigilance may be an
  adaptive response that reduces perceived vulnerability in sexually victimized women." [V]
- **Why it matters for the course:** it suggests that paying attention changes how you look to an
  observer. It does not show that paying attention lowers your risk of being attacked; no study in
  this line measures later victimisation after a change in gait.

**Where this leaves the gait claim (for the drafter).** Well supported: raters asked to judge ease
of attack sort walkers from movement alone (B7), and inmates convicted of assault picked out
potential victims from videotapes (B6, abstract). (**Stage 4 note, lesson 04 (2026-09-25):** an
earlier "Established: raters, including convicted offenders, agree with one another ... from
movement alone" joined two studies; no source read reports inmates agreeing with each other from
movement alone.) Reasonably supported: walking style went with past victimisation in one study's
samples (B10); B9's "accuracy" has no stated criterion in the abstract.
Not shown: that changing your walk lowers your future risk. Also not shown: that street offenders
choose victims this way in real life, as opposed to in a rating task. Any lesson that says "walk
confidently and you will not be chosen" overstates it. The honest version is closer to: people who
look alert and purposeful are rated as harder targets, attention is cheap, and it also helps you
notice trouble sooner (B11), but the research has not tested whether it prevents attack.
**Victim-blaming note:** this literature is easily turned into "you were attacked because of how you
walk". The lesson must say that responsibility for an attack lies with the attacker, and that most
harm to women (B20) comes from people they know, where gait is irrelevant.

#### B11. Hyman, I. E., Boss, S. M., Wise, B. M., McKenzie, K. E. and Caggiano, J. M. (2010). "Did you see the unicycling clown? Inattentional blindness while walking and talking on a cell phone." *Applied Cognitive Psychology* 24: 597-607.
- **Read:** full text (PDF hosted at spryfeet.com; a copy of the published article).
- **What it establishes:** the cleanest evidence in this area for what awareness is and what
  degrades it. Summary: "cell phone users walked more slowly, changed directions more frequently,
  and were less likely to acknowledge other people than individuals in the other conditions" and
  "were less likely to notice an unusual activity along their walking route (a unicycling clown)."
  [V] The numbers: "Only 25% of the cell phone users had noticed the clown", against "51% of single
  individuals, 61% of music player users, and 71% of people in pairs." [V]
- **Use:** this is the evidence for the practical advice "phone away when you are walking somewhere
  unfamiliar or at night". Note it is about talking on a phone, on one campus, published 2010; music players
  did not reduce noticing in this study, which cuts against the common "never wear earphones" rule
  (the Suzy Lamplugh "one earphone" advice, B17, is a reasonable middle). Pairs noticed most, which
  is a neat illustration of guardianship (B1).
- **Stage 4 note, lesson 04 (2026-09-25):** the paper gives no year of data collection, only "a
  pleasant afternoon in the spring" and "Published online 19 October 2009" [V], so write "published
  2010", not "in 2009"; 151 walkers, 24 of them on a phone, in "a single 1-hour session" [V]; the
  walkers were observed, not assigned, and the authors say "Something about people who choose to have
  a cell phone conversation while walking may be the causal factor rather than the divided attention
  requirements of a phone call." [V]; only a four-group chi-square is reported, so music users
  "noticed more" than lone walkers is untested; say "no less".

#### B12. Fear, and why too much of it is also a cost.
- **Jackson, J. and Gray, E. (2010). "Functional fear and public insecurities about crime."**
  *British Journal of Criminology* 50(1): 1-22. **Read:** abstract and introduction (accepted
  manuscript, LSE Research Online, eprints.lse.ac.uk/25537). The abstract "differentiates between
  a dysfunctional worry that erodes quality of life and a functional worry that motivates vigilance
  and routine precaution." [V] In a London survey, "one-quarter of those individuals who said they
  were worried about crime also viewed their worry as something akin to a problem-solving activity:
  they took precautions; these precautions that made them feel safer; and neither the precautions
  nor the worries reduced the quality of their lives." [V] This is the best academic statement of
  what the course is aiming for: awareness that turns into a few cheap habits and then stops.
- **Jackson, J. and Stafford, M. (2009). "Public health and fear of crime: a prospective cohort
  study."** *British Journal of Criminology* 49(6): 832-847. **Read:** abstract (accepted
  manuscript, LSE Research Online, 25538). From Whitehall II: "we suggest a feedback model where
  worry about crime harms health, which in turn serves to heighten worry about crime." [V] Use: fear
  is not free. A course that frightens people into staying home has done harm.
- **ONS, *Perceptions of personal safety and experiences of harassment, Great Britain: 16 February
  to 13 March 2022*** (released 25 May 2022). **Read:** main points. "people felt less safe walking
  alone in all settings after dark than during the day; with women feeling less safe than men in all
  settings after dark" [V]; more people "had stopped walking in quiet places such as "parks or open
  spaces" after dark in the last month because of feeling unsafe; an increase in both men (from 18%
  to 24%) and women (from 32% to 37%)". [V] **Stage 4 note, lesson 01 (2026-09-25):** section 4
  shows these are shares of people who already felt very or fairly unsafe in at least one setting,
  not of all men or all women: [V] "The Opinions and Lifestyle Survey (OPN) asked people who said they
  felt very or fairly unsafe in any setting, if they had stopped doing certain activities in the last
  month." The same section reports stopping going to busy public spaces falling from 38% to 23%. Give
  the population with the figures. Use: the gap between how unsafe people feel in public
  and where harm actually happens (B20). Snippets give "one in two women" feeling unsafe on a quiet
  street after dark; I did not extract that figure from the chart, so it is not quoted.

#### B13. Practitioner frameworks not read (on the unread line)
- **Gavin de Becker, *The Gift of Fear* (1997).** The most widely recommended practitioner book on
  intuition and "pre-incident indicators". Not read. Snippets show reviewers raising a real problem
  with "trust your gut": intuition can carry prejudice as well as pattern recognition. If a lesson
  teaches "trust your instincts" (as the Suzy Lamplugh Trust does, B18; **Stage 4 note, lesson 04
  (2026-09-25):** the State Department's "Women Travelers" page, B18, never mentions instinct; its
  nearest line is "Being safe is more important than being polite"), it
  should pair it with that caveat, and frame the cue as behaviour (someone persisting after "no",
  following, cutting off exits) rather than appearance.
- **Jeff Cooper's colour code** (white, yellow, orange, red), taught in many firearms and
  self-defence classes. Not read; no primary source found. If used, cite a readable source and call
  it a teaching device with no outcome evidence.

---

### 3. Home security: what actually deters burglary

This is the strongest evidence base in the part, and the most useful to a reader, because the UK
crime survey asked victims and non-victims which devices they had.

#### B14. Tseloni, A., Farrell, G., Thompson, R., Evans, E. and Tilley, N. (2017). "Domestic burglary drop and the security hypothesis." *Crime Science* 6:3. PMC7089640. Open access (CC BY 4.0).
- **Read:** full text (PMC).
- **What it establishes:** that better household security caused most of the fall in burglary in
  England and Wales, and which devices mattered. "The study concludes that there is strong evidence
  that security caused the decline in burglary in England and Wales in the 1990s." [V] The
  mechanism: the fall was "solely in forced rather than unforced entries to households" [V], and
  the main drop was when the house was empty (when locks and lights are in use).
- **The WIDE finding, which a lesson should teach:** "properties fitted with the four 'WIDE'
  devices: window locks (W), indoor lights on a timer (I), door double or deadlocks (D) and
  external lights on a sensor (E) have an SPF of 49." [V] In the discussion: "WIDE security devices
  ... produce a very substantial (almost 50 times) lower risk of burglary with entry than 'no
  security'". [V] SPF is the security protection factor: the ratio of burglary-with-entry risk
  without security to the risk with it. A drafter must say "compared with no security at all", not
  "cuts your risk by 98%", and must say the comparison is between households, not a trial.
- **Alarms:** "somewhat counterintuitively, alarms were not identified as contributory." [V] And:
  "It would be prudent to think twice before recommending an alarm as part of a standard package of
  security devices to reduce the risk of domestic burglary." [V]
- **Limits, in its own words:** "the CSEW has asked only about the presence or absence of security
  devices. It has not been designed to capture their quality, nor whether the devices were in use
  at the time of the victimisation." [V] It also could not test area-level measures (Secured by
  Design, alley-gating, street lighting), and suggests security's role "is understated".
- **Who is most at risk, same paper:** "previous burglary victimisation is the single highest
  predictive factor of current risk". [V] (Stage 4 correction, lesson 03: this is in the paper's
  discussion and is **cited, not found**: the sentence ends "(Farrell and Pease 1993 ; Osborn and
  Tseloni 1998 )". Attribute it as "the paper repeats, citing earlier research", never "found".) So the week after a burglary is when to fix the lock.
- Companion papers: Tseloni et al. (2014), "The effectiveness of burglary security devices",
  *Security Journal* (Springer page did not download; snippet only); Farrell, Tseloni, Mailley and
  Tilley (2011), "The crime drop and the security hypothesis", *JRCD* 48(2) (not read; the parent
  hypothesis, mostly about cars).

#### B15. Tilley, N., Thompson, R., Farrell, G., Grove, L. and Tseloni, A. (2015). "Do burglar alarms increase burglary risk? A counter-intuitive finding and possible explanations." *Crime Prevention and Community Safety* 17(1): 1-19.
- **Read:** abstract and the hypotheses section in full (open access PDF, UCL Discovery).
- **Abstract:** "It finds that alarms have become associated with increased rather than decreased
  risk of burglary with entry. This counter-intuitive finding needs to be treated cautiously." [V]
- **Explanations it offers**, none tested: households fit alarms *after* an earlier burglary, so
  the alarm marks a repeat-victim household ("Latent repeat victim", which they call plausible);
  offenders and police have adapted (police no longer always attend alarms); alarms flag that there
  is something worth stealing; some alarms are poor-quality or do-it-yourself systems (Tilley's wording, hypothesis 7; corrected at lesson 5's second pass). (Stage 4 note, lesson 05: the
  authors call **two** of these plausible, the latent repeat victim and alarms as flags of target
  suitability, "quite plausible although we have no data with which to test it" [V]; the original
  adaptive-offender version they call "highly unlikely". Their Conclusion: "it would be premature to
  conclude that domestic burglar alarms are (or have become) counter-productive" [V]. Funded by the
  ESRC. Never teach the latent repeat victim as their one or leading explanation. See "Gate
  closures, lesson 05".) This matters for neutrality:
  the finding is a correlation from survey data, and the authors say so.

#### B16. The other side on alarms: US offender surveys and a Newark study (both funded by the alarm industry).
- **Blevins, K. R., Kuhns, J. B. and Lee, S. (2012). *Understanding Decisions to Burglarize from
  the Offender's Perspective.*** UNC Charlotte, for the Alarm Industry Research and Educational
  Foundation. **Read:** the university's press release only (inside.charlotte.edu, 15 May 2013);
  the full report at airef.org did not download. On the unread line. From the release: "Approximately
  83 percent said they would try to determine if an alarm was present before attempting a burglary,
  and 60 percent said they would seek an alternative target if there was an alarm on-site." [V]
  "About 12 percent indicated that they typically planned the burglary in advance, 41 percent
  suggested it was most often a "spur of the moment" event". [V] Motive: drugs 51 percent, money 37
  percent. [V] Funding: "funded by the Alarm Industry Research and Educational Foundation (AIREF),
  under the auspices of the Electronic Security Association (ESA), the largest trade association for
  the electronic life safety and security industry." [V]
- **Lee, S. (2008), Rutgers doctoral dissertation on burglar alarms in Newark.** **Read:** the
  Rutgers press release only. It reports that "Neighborhoods in which burglar alarms were densely
  installed have fewer incidents of residential burglaries than the neighborhoods with fewer
  burglar alarms," [V] funded by AIREF, with Clarke and Felson on the advisory committee. The
  release also quotes Wright and Decker's interview study *Burglars on the Job* (1994): "Most
  offenders, though, wanted to avoid alarms altogether and, upon encountering such devices abandoned
  all thought of attacking the dwelling." [V, secondhand; the book is on the unread line]
- **How to present the disagreement (3.1 kind 2, contested empirical):** what burglars *say* in
  interviews (and industry-funded US studies) points one way; what the England and Wales survey
  *shows* about alarmed households' risk points the other. Both could be true: an alarm may deter
  some burglars while alarmed homes are, on average, homes that were already targets. Evidence that
  would settle it: a longitudinal design that records when the alarm was fitted relative to prior
  victimisation, or a randomised installation study. The consensus on locks and lights (B14) is much
  firmer than on alarms. Say who funded what, without implying that funding decides the result.

#### B17. How burglars get in: the survey data.
- **ONS, *Overview of burglary and other household theft: England and Wales*** (released 20 July
  2017; data to year ending March 2017). **Read:** sections 3 to 5 in full. Findings a lesson can
  use, all [V]: offenders who got in did so "through a door around 70% of the time and through a
  window around 30% of the time"; "approximately two-fifths of domestic burglaries in dwellings take
  place during the morning or afternoon (6am to 6pm) and three-fifths take place during the evening
  or night (6pm to 6am)"; "in over half of incidents of domestic burglary in a dwelling where an
  offender gained entry into the dwelling, someone was at home at the time"; "it was almost equally
  likely in incidents of domestic burglary in a dwelling that the offender(s) was a stranger or
  known by the victim" (Stage 4 note, lesson 05: the bullet opens "in the majority of years", a
  hedge this entry had dropped; keep it). Higher-risk groups: households headed by 16 to 24 year olds, single-parent
  households, urban households. On the decline: "households are currently four times less likely to
  be a victim of burglary than in 1995." [V] **This is 2017 data**; the drafter should check the
  current ONS "Nature of crime: burglary" tables (spreadsheet, not read) before giving figures as
  current.
- **Catalano, S. (2010). *Victimization During Household Burglary.*** BJS Special Report, NCJ
  227379, NCVS 2003-2007. **Read:** full text-only version (bjs.ojp.gov/content/pub/ascii/vdhb.txt).
  "In about 28% of these burglaries, a household member was present during the burglary. In 7% of
  all household burglaries, a household member experienced some form of violent victimization." [V]
  "In 40% of unlawful entries to unoccupied residences, offenders gained access through an unlocked
  door or window". [V] When someone was home, offenders got in through an open (27%) or unlocked
  (28%) door or window, and in 18% "someone inside the home let the offender in". [V] (Stage 4
  note, lesson 05: the 18% and the 12% pushed-in are shares of **unlawful (unforced) entries** to
  occupied homes, per the PDF's Table 7, not of all burglaries with someone home, where they are
  about 10.7% and 7.2%. The 28% includes attempted forcible entries. See "Gate closures, lesson
  05".) "Offenders
  were known to their victims in 65% of violent burglaries; offenders were strangers in 28%." [V]
- **The US and UK differ on "someone was at home"** (over half of UK burglaries with entry, about
  28% in the US). Different definitions and periods; do not reconcile, report both and say so.
- **A figure to avoid:** the Suzy Lamplugh home page says "half of all burglaries happen on the spur
  of the moment through unlocked doors or windows" [V] with no source. The BJS figure (40% of
  entries to *unoccupied* homes) and the UNCC spur-of-the-moment figure (41%) are different
  measures. Do not repeat the Lamplugh line.
- **Practical guidance that matches the evidence:** Suzy Lamplugh's home page: "If you are in your
  home and think there is an intruder, call the police – don't go and investigate by yourself"
  (Stage 4 note, lesson 05: the spaced dash fails the build, so a lesson quotes the two halves
  separately, never the dash) and
  "If you come home and suspect an intruder has been inside, don't enter the property." [V] Bogus
  callers: "Use the telephone number given in the phone book or on your utility bill, rather than
  the one on the identity card" [V], and lock other entrances before answering, because "a bogus
  caller will try and distract you whilst an accomplice robs you, often by entering through a back
  door." [V] (suzylamplugh.org/personal-safety-at-home). NCPC's home page gives the US equivalents:
  spare keys with a neighbour "not under a doormat or planter", timers on lights, and "Illuminate or
  eliminate places an intruder might hide". [V] (ncpc.org/resources/home-neighborhood-safety/).

---

### 4. Street and travel guidance, and what stands behind it

#### B18. Official and charity guidance

- **Suzy Lamplugh Trust, "Personal safety out and about"** (suzylamplugh.org/personal-safety-out-and-about).
  **Read:** full page. The UK's personal safety charity since 1986, and it runs the National
  Stalking Helpline. Its disclaimer is the most honest sentence on the subject and a lesson could
  quote it: its advice "is in no way intended to suggest that taking such actions will eliminate
  incidents of aggression and violence. The guidance merely helps readers better understand how to
  assess and mitigate risks to their personal safety." [V] Core advice [V]: "Before you go out,
  consider how you are going to get home"; "Try to use well-lit, busy streets and use the route you
  know best"; if followed, "cross the road, turning to see who is behind you. If you are still being
  followed, keep moving. Head to a busy area and tell people what is happening"; running: "Consider
  just having one earphone in"; shouting "a specific instruction – such as "Call the police!""; and
  on personal alarms, "Don't wait to check that your alarm has had the desired effect; just go." It
  recommends alarms over 130 decibels. **Caution:** its alarm advice ("holding it as close to the
  attackers face as possible") shades into physical technique; keep it at "set it off and leave".
- **UK FCDO, "Solo and independent travel"** (gov.uk/guidance/solo-and-independent-travel, published
  22 December 2023). **Read:** full. [V]: "Leave an itinerary with someone at home and agree how
  regularly you will be in contact"; "if walking alone, avoiding poorly lit areas, particularly at
  night"; "booking accommodation before you travel, in particular your first night's accommodation.
  You are at your most vulnerable when you first arrive in a foreign country as you are likely to be
  tired and unsure of your surroundings"; "never leaving your key where someone can note your room
  number". It also covers carbon monoxide and fire exits in accommodation, a reminder that the
  biggest travel risks are often not crime.
- **US Department of State, "International Traveler's Checklist"** (travel.state.gov). Live site
  returned a Cloudflare block; **read from Internet Archive capture 20260103122443.** Five steps:
  check the Travel Advisory, enrol in STEP ("It also helps the U.S. embassy or consulate reach you or
  your emergency contact in an emergency" [V]), copy documents ("Give a set of copies to a trusted
  friend or family member" [V]), check medicines, and driving permits.
- **US Department of State, "Women Travelers"** (capture 20251226183516). **Read:** full. [V]:
  "Consider using app-based transportation companies, which offer a record of your ride"; "Think
  ahead and come up with a safety plan to deal with unsafe situations, in the event you end up in
  one"; "Consider bringing personal safety whistles or alarms. However, confirm legality before
  traveling with items like pepper spray"; "In an unsafe situation, it can help to speak loudly ...
  Being safe is more important than being polite." Also that some destinations "may punish women
  who get pregnant but are not married, including victims of sexual assault", which is exactly the
  kind of legal difference a traveller must check. The pepper spray point is a useful US/UK
  contrast for the law part of the course (it is a prohibited weapon in the UK; Part D presumably
  owns this).
- **UK police StreetSafe** (police.uk/streetsafe): an anonymous way to report places that feel
  unsafe (poor lighting, being followed) that are not crimes. The page renders by script and was not
  read; on the unread line. Snippets only.
- **NCPC (US).** Beyond the home page above, the "Street Smarts" material was not found in readable
  form; on the unread line.

#### B19. What the evidence behind "well-lit, busy streets" and "use CCTV areas" actually is.
- **Welsh, B. C. and Farrington, D. P. (2008). "Effects of improved street lighting on crime."**
  Campbell Systematic Review 2008:13. **Read:** the Campbell Collaboration's plain-language summary
  only (full review on the unread line). [V]: "This review of 13 studies of street lighting
  interventions in the United Kingdom and United States, spanning four decades, finds that crime
  decreased by 21% in areas that experienced street lighting improvements compared to similar areas
  that did not"; UK 38% against US 7%; and "when street lighting was improved, nighttime crimes did
  not decrease more than daytime crimes, suggesting that community pride may play a crucial role."
  So lighting helps, but not only by letting people see; this is area-level, not a measure of the
  risk to one walker on one street.
- **Piza, E. L., Welsh, B. C., Farrington, D. P. and Thomas, A. L. (2019). "CCTV surveillance for
  crime prevention: a 40-year systematic review with meta-analysis."** *Criminology & Public Policy*
  18(1): 135-159. **Read:** research summary and methods (PDF hosted at prohic.nl). "The findings
  show that CCTV is associated with a significant and modest decrease in crime. The largest and most
  consistent effects of CCTV were observed in car parks." [V, two-column] Use: cameras help modestly,
  mostly against property crime in car parks; a camera is not a guardian who will step in.
- **Where the rest of the street advice stands.** Most of it ("plan your route", "tell someone
  where you are going", "stay with friends") is reasonable inference from routine activity theory
  (B1) and the guardianship evidence (B11's pairs), not tested in trials. The course should present
  it that way: low cost, sensible, unproven, and say which is which (the house "sort").

---

### 5. Who actually harms whom: the facts the myths get wrong

These overlap with Part A; included because each one corrects a specific piece of avoidance advice.

#### B20. Sexual violence: ONS, *Nature of sexual assault by rape or penetration, England and Wales: year ending March 2025* (released 4 November 2025).
- **Read:** main points and the perpetrator and location sections.
- Stage 4 correction, lesson 03: the perpetrator and location figures below are for **the survey
  years ending March 2020 and March 2025 combined**, not one year, and the measure is **rape or
  assault by penetration experienced at any time since age 16**. [V] "For YE March 2020 and YE March
