  2025 combined, for victims who experienced sexual assault by rape or penetration since the age of
  16 years, the most common perpetrator was a partner or ex-partner (43.2%)." Label every figure so.
- [V]: of victims since age 16, "around 4 in 10 (43.2%) were victimised by their partner or
  ex-partner (44.5% of females and 20.2% of males) and 16.3% were victimised by a stranger (15.0% of
  females and 37.5% of males)"; "over one-third (38.2%) were victimised in their own home and 8.0%
  were victimised on the street, in a car park, park, or another open public space". The next most
  common location was the perpetrator's home (28.5%). [V]
- **Use:** the core correction to "don't walk alone at night" as the main safety message for women.
  The street is a real but small share. It does not make the street advice wrong; it makes it the
  smaller part of the picture, and it means the course must spend real time on known-person
  violence (B24 onward), which is where the risk is.
- Of stranger rapes, "63.4% said that they themselves were under the influence of alcohol at the
  time" and "11.9% said that they thought they had been drugged". [V] Handle with great care: a
  lesson may report this only alongside the statement that responsibility lies with the offender.

#### B21. Violence generally: stranger violence falls more on men, and most on the young.

(Stage 4 correction, lesson 03: the heading used to say "falls mostly on young men". No source
here says that. ONS gives stranger violence by sex (1% of men, 0.4% of women, all ages) and by age
(2.2% at 16 to 24, both sexes) separately: rates, not shares of victims, and no joint figure for
young men. Say "more often men than women, and most often 16 to 24 year olds".)
- **ONS, *The nature of violent crime in England and Wales: year ending March 2024*** (released 26
  September 2024). **Read:** main points. [V]: "men accounted for a higher proportion of victims of
  violence with and without injury where the perpetrator was a stranger (1% of men, compared with
  0.4% of women)"; while women were more likely to experience "domestic abuse (6.6%), stalking
  (4.0%), sexual assault (3.4%), and harassment (11.2%)". Police-recorded homicide: "72% of homicide
  victims were male". [V]
- **BJS, Tapp, S. N. and Coen, E. J. (2025). *Criminal Victimization, 2024.*** NCJ 310547.
  **Read:** Table 1 and highlights (pdftotext). Stranger violence 3,091,500 of 6,671,640 violent
  victimisations in 2024 [V, table], about 46%; rate 10.8 per 1,000 against 23.3 for all violent
  crime. [V, table] So in the US a large share of (non-fatal) violence is by strangers, and the
  "most violence is by people you know" line is true for women and for sexual violence but too broad
  as a general claim. A drafter must not flatten this.
- **Myth this corrects:** "the person most at risk on the street at night is a woman alone."
  Measured by stranger violence, men are more at risk than women, and the young more than older
  people (see the correction under the heading). Women's fear of the street is not irrational
  (harassment is common: 27% of women against 16% of men in the 2022 ONS survey [V]), but the
  greatest measured risk of serious violence to women is at home.

#### B22. Fraud is now the commonest crime a household meets.
- **ONS, *Crime in England and Wales: year ending March 2026*** (released 23 July 2026). **Read:**
  main points. [V]: "9.6 million incidents of CSEW headline crime", of which "4.5 million fraud
  incidents", and "a 10% increase in the number of fraud victims (to 3.8 million victims)". Homicides
  fell "to 499 offences ... the lowest figure since current police recording practices began". [V]
- **Use:** a personal safety course that ignored fraud would be teaching the rare risk and skipping
  the common one.

#### B23. Children: "stranger danger".
- **Wolak, J., Finkelhor, D. and Sedlak, A. J. (2016). *Child Victims of Stereotypical Kidnappings
  Known to Law Enforcement in 2011.*** OJJDP Bulletin (NISMART-3). **Read:** highlights and
  definition (pdftotext). [V]: "An estimated 105 children were victims of stereotypical kidnappings
  in 2011, virtually the same as the 1997 estimate." Definition: "abductions in which a slight
  acquaintance or stranger moves a child at least 20 feet or holds the child at least 1 hour, and in
  which the child is detained overnight, transported at least 50 miles, held for ransom, abducted
  with the intent to keep permanently, or killed". Also: kidnappers "lured almost 70 percent of
  victims through deception or nonthreatening pretexts". [V]
- **NCMEC, "Straying Away from 'Stranger Danger'"** (missingkids.org blog, 2023). **Read:** full.
  [V]: "only one percent of missing child cases reported to NCMEC are the classic nonfamily
  abduction, or stereotypical kidnapping, that many parents fear." Its replacement scripts, which a
  lesson could quote: "It's important for you to get my permission before going anywhere with
  anyone." and "Let me know right away if anyone asks you to keep a secret, makes you feel
  uncomfortable or tries to get you to go somewhere with them." And: "If you need help, look for a
  uniformed police officer, store clerk with a nametag or a parent with children." [V]
  **Stage 4 note, lesson 06 (2026-09-25):** that third script is trimmed. In full it is "You should
  not approach just anyone. If you need help, look for a uniformed police officer, store clerk with
  a nametag or a parent with children." [V], and the page pairs each script with the rule it
  replaces: the third replaces "Never talk to strangers"; the permission script replaces warning a
  child "to stay away from people they don't know"; the "Let me know right away" script replaces
  warnings about "bad guys" and "you can tell someone is bad just by looking at them". Verbatim
  lines in "Gate closures, lesson 06".
- **Scope note:** this is a course for adults. A short household section on talking to children is
  in scope; child protection as a subject is not.

---

### 6. Online and fraud safety: what belongs here, and where to point instead

**Where the line falls.** Digital Literacy (published, Term 1) already teaches passwords and
multi-factor authentication (lesson 10, "Passwords and the ladder above them", including why the
password-expiry advice was withdrawn) and phishing and cons (lesson 11, "Recognising a con").
Cybersecurity for Everyone (planned, Term 6) will go further. Personal Safety should **not** reteach
either. It should link to Digital Literacy 10 and 11 and keep to the places where the online world
turns into physical or financial risk to the person:

1. **Pressure scams by phone, text and doorstep**, taught as one pattern with the in-person
   distraction burglary and bogus caller (B17), because they are the same con.
2. **Meeting someone from online in person.**
3. **Location sharing, trackers and stalkerware**, especially in abusive relationships (B26).
4. **Reporting and getting money back** (bank first, then police reporting lines).

#### B24. The pressure-scam pattern: two official statements that agree.
- **FTC, "How To Avoid a Scam"** (consumer.ftc.gov/articles/how-avoid-scam). **Read:** full. Four
  signs [V]: "Scammers PRETEND to be from an organization you know"; "Scammers say there's a PROBLEM
  or a PRIZE"; "Scammers PRESSURE you to act immediately"; "Scammers tell you to PAY in a specific
  way". Advice [V]: "Resist the pressure to act immediately. Honest businesses will give you time to
  make a decision." "Stop and talk to someone you trust." And: "The FTC will never threaten you, say
  you must transfer your money to "protect it," or tell you to withdraw cash or buy gold and give it
  to someone." Report at ReportFraud.ftc.gov.
- **Take Five to Stop Fraud** (UK Finance-led campaign, takefive-stopfraud.org.uk). Live site
  returned an Akamai block; **read from Internet Archive capture 20260902060025.** Three steps: Stop,
  Challenge, Protect. [V]: "It's ok to reject, refuse or ignore any requests. Only criminals will
  try to rush or panic you." "Contact your bank immediately if you think you've been scammed and
  tell the Police at reportfraud.police.uk or on 0300 123 2040." (Note: the UK reporting service
  formerly called Action Fraud now appears as Report Fraud at reportfraud.police.uk; check the
  current name before a lesson prints it.)
- **The teaching point, in both countries:** urgency is the tell. The same rule ("hang up and call
  the number you already have") defeats the phone scam, the text scam and the man at the door with
  an ID card (B17's "use the telephone number ... on your utility bill").

#### B25. Meeting people from online.
- **Suzy Lamplugh Trust, "Safety Online"** (suzylamplugh.org/safety-online). **Read:** full. [V]:
  "Tell someone where you're going, who you're meeting (name, website arranged through etc) and
  check in with them before, during and after the date." "Agree that if you don't check in as
  agreed or respond then your friend would call the place where you've met or even go there to
  check on you." "Meet in a public space and plan an exit strategy if needed." "When chatting with
  someone online consider talking about where you've been rather than where you're going." This is
  guardianship (B1) applied to dating, and a lesson can build a worked example on it.
- **Caution:** the same page tells readers to change passwords "regularly" [V]. That contradicts
  current UK guidance: the NCSC "explicitly advised against" regular password expiry in its 2015
  guidance [V] (ncsc.gov.uk blog, "The problems with forcing regular password expiry"). Do not
  repeat the Lamplugh password advice; link to Digital Literacy lesson 10.

---

### 7. Domestic abuse and stalking: safety planning and where to get help

**Handling note.** This is where the course is most likely to be read by someone in danger. Three
rules for the drafter, drawn from how the services themselves write: (1) the reader is the expert
on their own situation and decides; the services say so ("We will never tell you what to do", Refuge
[V]; "you are the expert in your situation", the Hotline [V]); (2) leaving can be the most dangerous
time (B27), so a lesson must never say "just leave"; (3) browsing can be monitored, so the lesson
should say so near the top and give the numbers early. Every domestic abuse lesson should give the
numbers in the first screen, not the last.

#### B26. The services, with numbers read from their own pages
- **US: National Domestic Violence Hotline** (thehotline.org). **Read:** home page and "What is a
  Safety Plan?". "Call 1.800.799.SAFE (7233)", "Text "START" to 88788", and live chat. [V] Also a
  line for young people, 866.331.9474, and a video phone line for deaf and hard-of-hearing callers.
  [V] Safety plan definition [V]: "a personalized, practical plan to improve your safety while
  experiencing abuse, preparing to leave an abusive situation, or after you leave." Why plan in
  advance: "it can be hard to think clearly or make logical decisions during moments of crisis." [V]
  The site's own warning: "Internet usage can be monitored and is impossible to erase completely."
  [V] The interactive planner (safety-plan.thehotline.org) was not opened; the old
  "create-a-safety-plan" URL now returns "Page not found". On the unread line.
- **UK: National Domestic Abuse Helpline, run by Refuge** (refuge.org.uk). **Read:** helpline page.
  "Call for free and in confidence, 24 hours a day: 0808 2000 247." [V] Live chat on weekdays and
  weekends (hours on the page). The page says the Helpline "is for women experiencing any kind of
  domestic abuse and concerned friends, family and colleagues". [V] Language Line interpreters and a
  BSL video service. [V]
- **UK, for men: Men's Advice Line (Respect)** (mensadviceline.org.uk). **Read:** home page header.
  "Freephone 0808 8010327 (Mon–Fri 10am–5pm)". [V] A lesson must include this; the ONS data (B20,
  B21) show men are victims too, and a course that gives only a women's line fails them.
- **UK: National Stalking Helpline, run by the Suzy Lamplugh Trust.** "0808 802 0300" [V] (page
  header). Opening hours not captured; check before printing.
- **US: stalking.** SPARC (stalkingawareness.org), a DOJ Office on Violence Against Women-funded
  project, is the reference body but says plainly: "SPARC does not provide services or guidance
  directly to victims/survivors of stalking" and points to "VictimConnect, DV Hotline, or RAINN". [V]
  So the US lesson should list those (VictimConnect and RAINN numbers not yet read; check).
- **UK: the Silent Solution.** Independent Office for Police Conduct
  (policeconduct.gov.uk/our-work/key-areas-of-work/silent-solution). **Read:** full. "A recorded
  message will instruct callers who can't talk to press 55 to be put through to police. Staying
  silent and not pressing 55 will lead to the call being terminated." [V] The campaign followed the
  murder of a woman who "had wrongly thought that police had her number and if she made a silent
  999 call, they would know to come." [V] This is exactly the kind of practical fact a lesson should
  teach, with the US note that there is no single equivalent (text-to-911 availability varies by
  area; check the FCC page before stating it).
- **Tech abuse: Refuge Tech Safety** (refugetechsafety.org). **Read:** the AirTag page. Its framing
  line is the one to carry: "depending on whether or not you are living with the person who is
  harming you, you may choose to take different steps. Control and coercion make some of these
  steps impossible or not safe." [V] The practical how-to (finding an unknown tracker, securing an
  Apple ID) belongs as a link, not in the lesson text, because the steps change with each phone
  update.

#### B27. Campbell, J. C. et al. (2003). "Risk factors for femicide in abusive relationships: results from a multisite case control study." *American Journal of Public Health* 93(7): 1089-1097. PMC1447915.
- **Read:** full text (PMC).
- **What it establishes:** the risk factors behind the Danger Assessment and behind the rule that
  separation is dangerous. Abstract: "perpetrator's access to a gun and previous threat with a
  weapon, perpetrator's stepchild in the home, and estrangement, especially from a controlling
  partner" increased risk; "Never living together and prior domestic violence arrest were
  associated with lowered risks." [V] The separation effect: "The risk of intimate partner femicide
  was increased 9-fold by the combination of a highly controlling abuser and the couple's separation
  after living together (adjusted OR = 8.98; 95% CI = 3.25, 24.83)." [V] Abuser's access to guns:
  adjusted OR 5.44 in model 5. [V] **Stage 4 note, lesson 07 (2026-09-25), corrected:** the 8.98
  is model 4; with threats added (model 5) the combination fell to adjusted OR 4.07 (1.33 to 12.4),
  "although substantially reduced, remained strong" [V], and in model 7 access to a gun was no longer
  significant once the abuser's use of a gun in the worst incident entered. Stalking, forced sex and
  abuse in pregnancy were significant in the bivariate analysis; in the multivariate analysis forced
  sex remained close to significance (adjusted OR 1.87, 95% CI 0.97 to 3.63, P < .07) [V], and the
  authors explain stalking's drop by its link with earlier threats with a weapon and threats to kill,
  "factors that more closely predict intimate partner femicide risks" [V] (threats to kill adjusted
  OR 2.60, model 5). Leaving: having ever left or asked the partner to leave, adjusted OR 3.19 (model
  3); the worst incident triggered by the victim leaving for reasons other than another partner, 4.04
  (model 7). Lines in "Gate closures, lesson 07".
- **Limits, in its own words:** "Perhaps the most important limitation of the study is its necessary
  reliance on proxy respondents for data regarding hypothesized risk factors". [V] Urban sample only.
- **The victim's own gun (contested; handle neutrally).** Campbell: "A victim's access to a gun could
  plausibly reduce her risk of being killed, at least if she does not live with the abuser. A small
  percentage (5%) of both case and control women lived apart from the abuser and owned a gun,
  however, and there was no clear evidence of protective effects." [V] In the paper's model table,
  victim sole access to a gun is significant in one model and not significant once relationship
  variables are added. [V, from the table] **This is a live value-and-evidence dispute in the US.**
  The Hotline's position, from its firearms page: "we do not advocate for survivors of domestic
  violence to purchase a gun." [V] Advocates of armed self-defence argue the opposite from the right
  of self-defence and from defensive gun use surveys (not researched in this part; Part D, on the
  law and on weapons, should find the strongest statement of that position in its proponents' own
  words, and a neutral lesson must include it). A lesson here should report Campbell's finding
  exactly (no clear evidence either way for the victim's gun; strong evidence of danger from the
  abuser's gun), state the Hotline's position as the Hotline's, and not resolve the value question.

#### B28. A source to use with caution: the Hotline's "Safety Planning Around Guns and Firearms" page.
- **Read:** full (thehotline.org/resources/safety-planning-around-guns-and-firearms/).
- It says "the presence of a gun in the home increases the risk of homicide for women by 500
  percent." [V] That appears to paraphrase Campbell's abuser-access finding (OR about 5), but "a gun
  in the home" is broader than "the perpetrator's access to a gun". A fact-checker should hold any
  lesson to Campbell's wording, not the Hotline's.
- Some of its tips should **not** be reproduced: one suggests reporting an abusive partner's car to
  911 "for suspected drunk driving" as a way to get police to find a gun [V], which amounts to
  advising a false report; others ("hiding under the bed might elicit some sympathy", advice on what
  to say with a gun pointed at you) are not evidence-based and stray into tactics the course does not
  teach. The page's general safety-planning advice (keep a charged phone, protective orders can
  require surrender of guns in many states [V]) is fine to cite.

#### B29. The feminist critique of personal safety advice, and the strongest counter-evidence.
- **Vera-Gray, F. and Kelly, L. (2020). "Contested gendered space: public sexual harassment and
  women's safety work."** *International Journal of Comparative and Applied Criminal Justice* 44(4):
  265-275. **Read:** abstract only (London Met repository); full text and Vera-Gray's book *The
  Right Amount of Panic* (2018) on the unread line. Abstract [V]: "the "safety work" mandated for
  women and girls in public functions to limit women's space for action and responsiblise them for
  preventing violence." (Their spelling.) This is the best short statement of the view that
  personal safety advice, especially advice to women about strangers, is misdirected (the risk is
  mostly from known men), costly (it restricts freedom), and shifts responsibility onto victims. It
  should be stated in its own terms and not treated as fringe; it is mainstream in UK
  violence-against-women research and consistent with the ONS data (B20).
- **Senn, C. Y. et al. (2015). "Efficacy of a sexual assault resistance program for university
  women."** *New England Journal of Medicine* 372: 2326-2335. PMID 26061837. **Read:** abstract
  (PubMed API). A randomised trial of the Enhanced Assess, Acknowledge, Act programme, which teaches
  women to "assess risk from acquaintances, overcome emotional barriers in acknowledging danger, and
  engage in effective verbal and physical self-defense." [V] "The 1-year risk of completed rape was
  significantly lower in the resistance group than in the control group (5.2% vs. 9.8%; relative
  risk reduction, 46.3% [95% confidence interval, 6.8 to 69.1]; P=0.02)." [V] A letter in NEJM by
  Torgerson (2015) commented on it; not read, on the unread line.
- **How the two fit (for the contested-questions section):** the critique and the trial are less
  opposed than they look. EAAA is aimed at acquaintance risk, not strangers, and its authors
  describe it as feminist self-defence; Vera-Gray and Kelly end by proposing feminist self-defence
  too. The real disagreement is between advice that tells women to restrict where they go (the
  target of the critique) and training that helps people recognise and resist the risks they
  actually face (what the trial tested). Part C or D likely owns self-defence training; the overlap
  with this part is the "assess" and "acknowledge" steps, which are situational awareness applied to
  people you know. Coordinate so the trial is taught once.

---

### What the field considers essential (this part)

1. **Opportunity is the part of crime you can change.** Routine activity theory (offender, target,
   guardian) and situational prevention (effort, risk, reward, provocation, excuse). Taught as a
   tool for your own risk, not a theory of offenders and not a judgement on victims.
2. **Awareness is attention, and it has a cost.** Endsley's three levels; phones cut noticing
   (Hyman); pairs notice most. Worry that turns into a few habits is useful (Jackson and Gray);
   worry that shrinks a life harms health (Jackson and Stafford).
3. **What the victim-selection research does and does not show.** Raters agree on who looks easy to
   attack; the link to real victimisation is partial; no study shows that changing your walk
   prevents attack.
4. **Home: locks and lights, in combination.** WIDE, the strongest evidence in the part. Alarms are
   contested. Unlocked doors and windows remain a large share of entries. Don't go in if you think
   someone is inside. The weeks after a burglary are the riskiest.
5. **Street and travel: plan the way home, tell someone, stay where there are people and light.**
   Present as sensible and low-cost, with the evidence behind lighting and CCTV being area-level and
   modest.
6. **Match the precaution to the actual risk.** Stranger violence falls mostly on young men; most
   sexual violence against women is by partners and people they know, at home; fraud is now the
   commonest crime; stereotypical child abduction is very rare.
7. **Pressure is the tell in every con.** One rule for phone, text and doorstep: stop, hang up, call
   back on a number you already have. Link to Digital Literacy for the technical side.
8. **Domestic abuse and stalking: plan, don't prescribe.** The numbers early; leaving is the most
   dangerous time; the reader decides; tech can be used against you.

### Common misconceptions (named by the sources)

- **"Stranger danger."** NCMEC does not support the message; stereotypical kidnappings are about a
  hundred a year in the US (B23). For adults, the equivalent myth is that the main threat to women
  is a stranger outdoors (B20).
- **"Don't walk alone at night" is the core of women's safety.** The street is a small share of
  where rape happens (8.0%), and stranger violence falls more on men (B20, B21). The advice is not
  wrong, only misweighted.
- **"Walk confidently and you won't be picked."** Overstates rating studies (B6 to B10).
- **"An alarm is the best protection."** Not supported by the England and Wales data; locks plus
  lights are (B14, B15). Contested with US offender surveys (B16).
- **"Burglars strike when nobody is home."** Often, but in the UK over half of burglaries with
  entry happened with someone at home, and in the US about 28% (B17).
- **"Change your passwords regularly."** Withdrawn advice (NCSC); still on some safety charity pages
  (B25).
- **"Just leave."** Separation after living together, especially from a highly controlling partner,
  went with higher odds of femicide (B27; not "the highest-risk period": a case-control study gives
  no timing, and other factors had larger odds; Stage 4 note, lesson 07).
- **"If I call 999 and can't speak, they'll come."** Not unless you press 55 (B26).
- **"Earphones make you a target."** Music players did not reduce noticing in Hyman's study; phone
  conversations did (B11).

### Contested questions and the positions on each

1. **Do burglar alarms protect a home?** (3.1 kind 2.) *Against, or at least unproven:* Tilley et
   al. 2015 and Tseloni et al. 2017 find alarms associated with higher risk of burglary with entry in
   England and Wales since about 2000, and advise caution about recommending them (B14, B15). *For:*
   offender surveys in the US (UNC Charlotte 2012, Wright and Decker 1994) and a Newark area study
   (Lee 2008) find burglars say they avoid alarms and densely alarmed areas have fewer burglaries
   (B16); these were largely funded by the alarm industry. *What would settle it:* data on when alarms
   were fitted relative to earlier burglary, or a randomised installation trial. *Consensus:* none on
   alarms; strong on locks and lights.
2. **Does situational awareness (and gait) prevent victimisation?** (3.1 kind 2.) *For:* offenders
   and raters agree on cues; some cues track victimisation history; awareness improves noticing
   (B7 to B11). *Against, or not shown:* no study measures future victimisation after training;
   rating tasks are not attacks; most serious harm to women comes from known people, where gait is
   irrelevant (B20). *What would settle it:* a prospective study of training and later
   victimisation.
3. **Is personal safety advice for women protective or harmful?** (Partly empirical, partly value.)
   *Critique:* Vera-Gray and Kelly: "safety work" restricts women and makes them responsible (B29).
   *Defence:* guardianship and target-hardening lower risk (B1, B14); a randomised trial shows
   acquaintance-focused resistance training halves rape (B29, Senn). *How to present:* the evidence
   separates restriction-based advice from skill-based training; the value question (who should
   carry the burden of prevention) is described, not resolved.
4. **Should a domestic abuse victim own a gun?** (Value question with an empirical core; US-centred.)
   Campbell's data show clear danger from the abuser's access and no clear evidence of protection
   from the victim's (B27). The Hotline advises against; advocates of armed self-defence argue for
   it (not yet researched here; Part D should supply their best statement). Describe, do not
   prescribe.
5. **Does displacement undo situational prevention?** (3.1 kind 2, but with a strong lean.) Clarke
   and the Guerette and Bowers review (snippet only) say displacement is usually small and
   diffusion of benefits about as common (B2, B4).

### Best free resources to link

- Suzy Lamplugh Trust personal safety pages and the National Stalking Helpline (UK).
- National Domestic Violence Hotline, including its safety planning tool (US); National Domestic
  Abuse Helpline (Refuge) and Refuge Tech Safety (UK); Men's Advice Line (UK).
- IOPC Silent Solution page (UK).
- FTC "How To Avoid a Scam" and ReportFraud.ftc.gov (US); Take Five and Report Fraud (UK).
- FCDO travel advice and "Solo and independent travel" (UK); State Department Travel Advisories,
  STEP and the traveller's checklist (US).
- NCMEC KidSmartz (for the short household section on children).
- Tseloni et al. 2017 (open access) for anyone who wants the burglary evidence itself.
- The POP Center's 25 techniques grid.
- Digital Literacy lessons 10 and 11 (internal), First Aid lessons 01 and 16 (internal).

### Safety-critical guidance (this part)

- Never tell a reader in an abusive relationship to leave; give the numbers and the planning
  resources and say the reader decides.
- Put a "browsing can be monitored" line and the helpline numbers at the top of any domestic abuse
  or stalking lesson.
- Do not reproduce the Hotline firearms page's false-report tip or its tactics for when a gun is
  pointed at you.
- If you think someone is inside your home, do not go in or investigate; call the police.
- The personal alarm is for making noise and leaving, not for close-in use against an attacker.
- Every phone number and opening time was read on 2026-09-25 and must be rechecked at drafting.

### Open concerns about scope (this part)

- **Overlap with Part A** on victimisation statistics: merge, do not teach twice.
- **Overlap with Parts C and D** on self-defence training (Senn), weapons carried for defence
  (pepper spray, guns) and the law; this part touches them only where avoidance and planning do.
- **Domestic abuse and stalking** could be a lesson of their own, and probably should be: they are
  where most serious risk to women lies, and a course that spends three lessons on streets and one
  paragraph on the home would repeat the misweighting the evidence corrects.
- **Fraud** must stay small and point to Digital Literacy; the pull to reteach phishing will be
  strong.
- **Children:** one household section at most; child protection is not this course.
- **Source quality:** the gait literature's founding paper (Grayson and Stein) was not read in full,
  and its sample sizes circulate widely in unreliable forms. Read it before any lesson describes it.

---

## Part C: De-escalation, confrontation and resistance

*Stage 1 research for Personal Safety, Part C of four, 2026-09-25. Scope: de-escalation and its
evidence (health settings, police, workplaces); the verbal and non-verbal techniques practitioners
teach; robbery (comply or resist); sexual assault resistance and resistance education; bystander
intervention; the stress response; what self-defence classes can and cannot teach, and how to
choose one. Every date read is 2026-09-25 unless a capture date is given.*

**How the quotation marks work in this part.** **[V]** means copied from text I extracted myself:
curl plus an HTML stripper, pdftotext on a PDF, the PubMed E-utilities API (efetch abstracts), the
Crossref API (abstracts as deposited by the publisher), or an Internet Archive capture (ID given).
Nothing here came from the WebFetch summarising tool, and nothing from a search-result snippet is
in quotation marks. Where I say "snippet only", nothing from that item may be stated in a lesson as
a finding. **Abstract only** means exactly that: the lesson may report what the abstract says and
no more, and must not describe methods or caveats the abstract does not state.

**What this part does not duplicate.** First Aid and CPR lesson 1 already teaches the bystander
effect, the correction to the Kitty Genovese story, Fischer and colleagues' 2011 meta-analysis and
Philpot and colleagues' 2020 CCTV study (at least one bystander intervened in 90.9% of 219 public
conflicts). Personal Safety should **point to that lesson** and build on it, not reteach it. First
Aid lesson 16 already covers what a person may feel after an emergency and where to get support
(Samaritans 116 123, 988 in the US and Canada). Personal Safety's aftermath lesson should link it.

---

### 1. Access problems a drafter inherits

1. **Several key robbery papers could not be read.** Zimring and Zuehl (1986, Chicago robbery
   deaths) is on Berkeley's repository as an image-only scan that neither curl nor the in-app
   browser could turn into text; Cook's robbery papers and Kleck and DeLone (1993) are behind
   JSTOR, Springer and a Cloudflare wall at scholarlycommons.law.northwestern.edu; OJP abstract
   pages for them redirected. What is known about them here is **snippet only**. The compliance
   side of the robbery question therefore rests on the NCJRS abstract of Block and Skogan (1985),
   the NIOSH convenience-store cohort (Faulkner 2001), the Home Office study (Smith 2003) and
   police advice. A drafter who wants Zimring and Zuehl's actual findings must read the scan by
   eye.
2. **Tark and Kleck (2004 and 2014) were read at abstract level only** (Crossref API). Part D also
   lists "Tark and Kleck" as unread, meaning the full papers. A lesson may quote the abstracts and
   no more.
3. **The Metropolitan Police and Right To Be sites block curl** (Cloudflare). Both were read from
   Internet Archive captures, IDs given. **The CPS rape myths annex has moved:** its URL returns 404
   today and I read it from capture 20260421173121. The drafter should find the current CPS
   location before citing it.
4. **The CDC's STOP SV technical package has been withdrawn from cdc.gov** (the current PDF says
   the content has been removed and points to "Resources for Action", which I did not read). I read
   the 2016 package from an Internet Archive capture of 2021. A lesson must say it is the 2016 CDC
   document, not current CDC guidance.
5. **Engel, McManus and Herold (2020)** was read in full from a copy hosted by a Dutch
   organisation (prohic.nl), not the publisher. That is an openly posted third-party copy, not a
   workaround of a block, but the provenance is not Wiley's. Quote it with that caveat or check
   against the publisher's page first.
6. **Two recent items are preprints or very new.** The Liebst and colleagues "would I be hurt"
   bystander study was read as two PsyArXiv preprint abstracts (2020); I did not find its published
   version. Lindegaard and colleagues' commercial robbery study was published online on
   2026-09-15, ten days ago. Both need a check at drafting time.

---

### 2. De-escalation: what the guidance says

#### 2.1 NICE NG10, *Violence and aggression: short-term management in mental health, health and community settings* (published 28 May 2015)

URL: https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng10/chapter/recommendations. **Read: the de-escalation
section (1.3.12 to 1.3.20) and 1.4.5 in full; the rest of the recommendations skimmed.** It is
the UK's official guidance for health and social care staff, not advice to the public, and a
lesson must say so. What it establishes: de-escalation is the first-line response, restraint is a
last resort, and the named components.

- Training should enable staff to "recognise the early signs of agitation, irritation, anger and
  aggression", "use techniques for distraction and calming, and ways to encourage relaxation",
  "recognise the importance of personal space" and "respond to a service user's anger in an
  appropriate, measured and reasonable way and avoid provocation." (1.3.12) [V]
- "If a service user becomes agitated or angry, 1 staff member should take the primary role in
  communicating with them." (1.3.18) [V]
- "Use emotional regulation and self‑management techniques to control verbal and non‑verbal
  expressions of anxiety or frustration (for example, body posture and eye contact) when carrying
  out de‑escalation." (1.3.19) [V]
- "Communicate respect for and empathy with the service user at all stages of de‑escalation."
  (1.3.17) [V]
- Restrictive intervention "only if de-escalation and other preventive strategies ... have failed",
  and "Continue to attempt de‑escalation throughout a restrictive intervention." (1.4.5) [V]

Teaching use: the one-voice rule, self-regulation first, space, and "flashpoints" (1.3.15 names
refusing a request, asking someone to stop, asking someone to do something they don't want to) are
directly transferable to a shop counter, a car park or a family argument. The lesson must present
the transfer as the course's inference, not NICE's.

#### 2.2 Richmond and colleagues, "Verbal De-escalation of the Agitated Patient" (Project BETA consensus statement, *Western Journal of Emergency Medicine* 2012;13(1):17-25)

URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3298202/. **Read: in full (PMC).** The American
Association for Emergency Psychiatry's workgroup. **This is the most usable practical text on
verbal de-escalation, and it says openly that it is expert consensus, not trial evidence.**

- On the evidence: "a review of the literature indicates that scientific studies and medical
  writings on verbal de-escalation are few and lack descriptions of specific techniques and
  efficacy." The guidelines "were therefore developed by the consensus of the authors and a review
  of the limited available literature". [V]
- The ten domains: respect personal space; do not be provocative; establish verbal contact; be
  concise; identify wants and feelings; listen closely; agree or agree to disagree; lay down the
  law and set clear limits; offer choices and optimism; debrief. [V, headings]
- Space: "maintain at least 2 arm's lengths of distance ... if a patient tells you to get out of
  the way, do so immediately. Both the patient and the clinician should be able to exit the room
  without feeling that the other is blocking his way." [V]
- Body: "Hands should be visible and not clenched." "The clinician should avoid directly facing
  the agitated patient and should stand at an angle". "Excessive, direct eye contact, especially
  staring, can be interpreted as an aggressive act." [V]
- One voice: "Multiple people verbally interacting can confuse the patient and result in further
  escalation." [V]
- Short sentences: "use short sentences and a simple vocabulary" and "Repetition is essential".
  [V]
- Agreeing: three ways to agree ("agreeing with the truth", "agreeing in principle", "agree with
  the odds"), and "if there is no way to honestly agree with the patient, agree to disagree." [V]
- Limits: tell the person what is unacceptable "in a matter-of-fact way and not as a threat." [V]
  **(Stage 4 note, lesson 08: the clause is about naming a consequence. The full passage: "If
  necessary, tell the patient that he may be arrested and prosecuted if he assaults anyone. This
  should be communicated in a matter-of-fact way and not as a threat." The distinction is of
  manner, not whether a consequence is named; see "Gate closures, lesson 08".)**
- Humiliation: "do not challenge the patient, insult him, or do anything else that can be
  perceived as humiliating." [V]

#### 2.3 Workplace guidance: OSHA (US) and HSE (UK)

- **OSHA, Workplace Violence topic page** (https://www.osha.gov/workplace-violence, read in full).
  Defines workplace violence as "any act or threat of physical violence, harassment, intimidation,
  or other threatening behavior that occurs at the work site." Gives the CFOI figures: of 5,283
  fatal workplace injuries in 2023, "740 fatalities were due to violent acts. Homicides (458)
  accounted for 61.9 percent of violent acts and 8.7 percent of all work-related fatalities." Risk
  factors include "exchanging money with the public", working "alone or in isolated areas", and
  "working where alcohol is served". And: "There are currently no specific OSHA standards for
  workplace violence." [V]
- **OSHA 3148, *Guidelines for Preventing Workplace Violence for Healthcare and Social Service
  Workers*** (2016 revision, PDF, searched and sections read). "training should cover the policies
  and procedures for a facility as well as de-escalation and self-defense techniques. Both
  de-escalation and self-defense training should include a hands-on component." [V]
- **OSHA 3153, *Recommendations for Workplace Violence Prevention Programs in Late-Night Retail
  Establishments*** (2009, PDF, searched). Among the topics training "may include": "Specific
  instructions on how to respond to a robbery such as turning over money or valuables without
  resistance". [V] (Stage 4 note, lesson 09: the list is headed "Topics may include the following:",
  not "should include"; the "should" sentence just before it is "guidance to limit workers from
  intervening in workplace altercations". See "Gate closures, lesson 09".) This is the official US
  employer position on robbery, and belongs in §3.
- **HSE, "Violence and aggression at work"** (https://www.hse.gov.uk/violence/, employer guide
  page 1 and worker page read in full). HSE's definition: "'Any incident in which a person is
  abused, threatened or assaulted in circumstances relating to their work.'" It includes "verbal
  abuse or threats, including face to face, online and via telephone". Workers should "use the
  information and training your employer has provided to help you prevent incidents or know how to
  calm things down to avoid incidents escalating" and report incidents; lone workers and "New
  starters, trainees and other vulnerable workers" may be at greater risk. [V]

#### 2.4 Police de-escalation: ICAT and its evaluations

- **Engel, McManus and Herold, "Does de-escalation training work? A systematic review and call for
  evidence in police use-of-force reform"**, *Criminology & Public Policy* 2020;19:721-759. **Read:
  abstract and first pages in full, the rest searched (third-party PDF, see §1).** "We identified
  64 de-escalation training evaluations conducted over a 40-year period, primarily in the fields of
  nursing and psychiatry." Trainings appear to "lead to slight-to-moderate individual and
  organizational improvements", but "conclusions concerning the effectiveness of de-escalation
  training are limited by the questionable quality of almost all evaluation research designs." [V]
- **Engel and colleagues, Louisville ICAT evaluation**, *Criminology & Public Policy* 2022;21:199-233.
  **Read: abstract (Crossref) and the CrimeSolutions profile in full.** ICAT (Integrating
  Communications, Assessment, and Tactics) was developed by the Police Executive Research Forum.
  "Using a stepped‐wedge randomized controlled trial research design, the panel regression results
  demonstrated statistically significant reductions in use of force incidents (−28.1%), citizen
  injuries (−26.3%), and officer injuries (−36.0%) in the post‐training period." Before it, "no
  research has empirically demonstrated that these trainings reduce use of force in the field". [V]
  The NIJ's CrimeSolutions rates it "Effective" on one study, and describes the modules, including
  "taking a 'tactical pause'", "using time and repositioning to establish and maintain a
  'reactionary gap'", and active listening defined as "listening to understand and not just
  respond". [V]
- **Engel, Corsaro, Motz and Cherkauskas, *Evaluation of ICAT Training with the Indianapolis
  Metropolitan Police Department*** (NIJ-funded final report, 30 June 2025, PDF, executive summary
  read in full). A replication with "a condensed 8-hour version of ICAT without scenario-based
  practice". Findings: "19.6% initial reduction in subjects who had force used against them
  (12-month follow-up), and 18.3% reduction sustained over an 18-month period"; "25.2% initial
  reduction in the number of subjects injured"; but officer injuries showed no statistically
  significant change. **(Stage 4 note, lesson 08: the condensed course "was supplemented, however,
  with additional departmentwide emphasis on the use of de-escalation tactics and skills through
  other trainings, policies, and data collection"; the report does not attribute the officer-injury
  result to the shorter format. See "Gate closures, lesson 08".)** Arrests also fell 15.3%, and the authors bound the training's independent
  effect on force at "-5.0% to -19.6%". Attitudes improved after training but "Many of these gains
  diminished by the follow-up period, with attitudes often returning to baseline". [V]

**What this establishes for a civilian course**: that de-escalation can be trained and, in one
profession with good evaluations, reduces force and injury; that attitude gains fade without
reinforcement; that the evidence is best for police and weakest for everyone else. It does not
establish that a member of the public who reads a page will de-escalate better. That is the
course's inference, and a lesson must label it.

#### 2.5 The evidence gap in health settings

- **Spencer, Johnson and Smith, Cochrane review CD012034.pub2 (2018), "De-escalation techniques for
  managing non-psychosis induced aggression in adults"** (PMC6513023, read: abstract and results
  summary in full). Found one eligible study (306 nursing-home residents with dementia in France);
  "The limited evidence means that uncertainty remains around the effectiveness of de‐escalation
  and the relative efficacy of different techniques." [V] A companion Cochrane review on
  psychosis-induced aggression (Du and colleagues, 2017) reportedly included no studies (snippet
  only; the Cochrane page returned 55 words).
- **Heckemann and colleagues, *Nurse Education Today* 2015;35:212-9** (abstract, PubMed). Nine
  before-and-after studies of aggression-management training for hospital nurses: all "reported
  increased confidence, improved attitude, skills, and knowledge about risk factors post
  training", but "There was no significant change in incidence of patient aggression." [V]
  **(Stage 4 note, lesson 08: quotation completed; the abstract also concludes "Training does not
  reduce the incidence of aggressive acts.")**
- **Price and Baker, *Int J Ment Health Nurs* 2012** (abstract): de-escalation "has been
  overlooked by rigorous research, and it is often assumed that staff are able to perform these
  techniques in clinical practice." [V] **Hallett and Dickens, *Int J Nurs Stud* 2017** (abstract):
  five theories of de-escalation proposed, all "lacked empirical support". [V]

#### 2.6 Practitioner teaching outside the professions (expert opinion, labelled as such)

- **Peyton Quinn's "Five Rules"**, quoted and discussed by self-defence author Marc MacYoung on his
  blog ("Addition to the Five Rules of Violence", 2015,
  https://macyoungsmusings.blogspot.com/2015/01/addition-to-five-rules-of-violence.html, read in
  full): "1) Do not insult him 2) Do not challenge him 3) Do not threaten him 4) Do not deny it's
  happening 5) Give him a face saving exit." [V] MacYoung adds a sixth, do not command, and
  attributes to Rory Miller the line "You cannot shame a predator". [V, but the Miller attribution
  is MacYoung's; I did not read Miller.] **This is practitioner opinion with no study behind it**,
  and a thoughtful one; it agrees closely with Richmond's domains II and IX. The blog uses coarse
  language; paraphrase rather than quote at length. MacYoung's framing of "social" versus
  "asocial" violence (credited to Miller) is widely taught: the idea that de-escalation works on
  status contests and not on someone who has already decided to rob or hurt you. **(Stage 4 note,
  lesson 08: "status contests" and that summary are this file's gloss, not the post's. The post
  says only that there is violence "for social reasons and for asocial reasons", that the five
  rules "will (most likely) prevent social violence, they won't stop asocial violence", and gives a
  robber as its asocial example; "status" and "means to an end" are not in it. See "Gate closures,
  lesson 08".)** That distinction is reasonable and **untested**; a lesson can teach it as a
  practitioner model.

**What a lesson can say, sorted (standards 3.1).** Established: official bodies in both countries
recommend de-escalation first, and ICAT reduced police force in two stepped-wedge trials.
Contested or unknown: whether specific verbal techniques work, and whether training members of
the public reduces violence. Expert consensus without trial evidence: the specific techniques
(space, angle, hands visible, one voice, short sentences, agree where you honestly can, limits
stated without threat, a face-saving exit). Be exact about which is which.

---

### 3. Robbery: comply or resist

This is a **contested empirical question** (3.1), and a lesson must present both sides at their
strongest and say what would settle it. The course's framing should be that the choice is
situational and the victim's to make, and that nobody who complied or resisted chose wrongly.

#### 3.1 The case for handing it over (official advice, and data behind it)

- **Metropolitan Police, "How to protect yourself from street robbery"** (Internet Archive capture
  20250812180728, read in full): "if you're threatened with violence, don't risk your personal
  safety. Property can be replaced, you can't." [V]
- **Metropolitan Police Department, Washington DC, "Guarding Against Robbery and Assault"**
  (brochure, January 2011, PDF, read in full): "Don't resist. Give up your property— don't give up
  your life." On carjacking: "If the carjacker threatens you with a gun or other weapon, GIVE UP
  YOUR CAR!" [V] (Stage 4 note, lesson 10: under the same heading it lists running away, hiding,
  screaming and raising an alarm as preventive "Self defense measures"; see "Gate closures, lesson
  10". Do not reproduce the unsourced line that follows them.)
- **OSHA 3153** (above) lists, among the topics late-night retail training may include, "turning
  over money or valuables without resistance". [V] (Stage 4 note, lesson 09: a recommendation and a
  "Topics may include" item; never write that OSHA "tells" employers to train it.)
- **Faulkner, Landsittel and Hendricks, "Robbery characteristics and employee injuries in
  convenience stores"**, *Am J Ind Med* 2001;40:703-9 (abstract, PubMed). A prospective cohort of
  460 convenience store robberies: "Injury risk was strongly associated with ... employee
  resistance", among other factors, with "Numerous intercorrelations between these
  characteristics". [V] The abstract does not say whether injury came before or after resistance.
- **Smith, *The nature of personal robbery*, Home Office Research Study 254** (January 2003, PDF
  from the College of Policing library, sections on injury and resistance read in full, method
  read). Nearly 2,000 police crime reports and witness statements from seven force areas. "One in
  four robberies involved resistance by the victim in the form of a physical struggle". "Female
  victims were more likely to resist their attacker and, probably as a consequence, more likely to
  sustain some physical injury". "Two out of every five (43%) personal robberies resulted in injury
  of some sort". In snatch robberies, "Violence may follow, should the victim resist." [V] Note the
  words "probably as a consequence": the causal link is the author's inference from police records,
  not a measured sequence. Also: "robberies involving the use of weapons were less likely to result
  in physical injury to the victim" [V], which fits the argument that a visible weapon produces
  compliance, and compliance lowers injury.
- **Block and Skogan, *Dynamics of Violence Between Strangers: Victim Resistance and Outcomes in
  Rape, Robbery and Assault*** (NIJ, 1985; NCJRS abstract read in full, report not read). US
  National Crime Survey, 1973 to 1979. "Generally, nonforceful resistance was related to more
  favorable victim outcomes. Victims who resisted by screaming, running away, reasoning with their
  attackers, or other nonforceful means were less likely to be injured and more likely to avoid
  completion of the crime." Limitations stated: the survey "excludes crimes in which the victim dies,
  and it does not obtain data on the sequencing of victim and offender actions". [V] (Stage 4 note,
  lesson 10: the quotation previously stopped at "injured", cutting the half that favours
  resistance. The abstract also says the effects "varied with victim and offender characteristics,
  the crime setting, and whether or not the offender used a weapon" [V], without saying in which
  direction. Non-forceful resistance going with better outcomes than none is evidence against
  "Don't resist" as written, so this study is not support for the compliance side; it is filed here
  only because it is usually cited in this argument. See "Gate closures, lesson 10".)
- **Unread, snippet only:** Zimring and Zuehl (1986) studied Chicago robbery killings and are
  usually cited for the finding that resisting victims were over-represented among those killed;
  Kleck and DeLone (1993) found, per a search snippet, that unarmed physical force against a robber
  appeared to raise injury risk. Neither may be stated as a finding until read.

**Best case in its proponents' terms**: a robber wants property and speed; a compliant victim
gives both and ends the encounter; the people most at risk of being killed in a robbery are those
who resist, and that is the one outcome surveys of survivors can never capture. Property is
replaceable. London's Metropolitan Police and the Washington, DC police advise compliance, and the
US workplace regulator lists it as a training topic. (Stage 4 note, lesson 10: previously "Police in
both countries", which is two forces; none of the three official pages cites Faulkner, Smith or
Block and Skogan, so no study may be said to "stand behind" the advice.)

#### 3.2 The case that resistance often helps

- **Tark and Kleck, "Resisting crime: the effects of victim action on the outcomes of crimes"**,
  *Criminology* 2004;42:861-910 (Stage 4 note, lesson 10: Crossref gives 861-910). **Abstract only
  (Crossref API).** 27,595 personal contact crime
  incidents from the NCVS, 1992 to 2001, sixteen self-protection actions. "self‐protection in
  general, both forceful and nonforceful, reduced the likelihood of property loss and injury,
  compared to nonresistance." "The appearance, in past research, of resistance contributing to
  injury was found to be largely attributable to confusion concerning the sequence of SP actions and
  injury. In crimes where both occurred, injury followed SP in only 10 percent of the incidents."
  The sentence in full: "Combined with the fact that injuries following resistance are almost
  always relatively minor, victim resistance appears to be generally a wise course of action." [V]
  And: "A variety of mostly forceful tactics, including resistance with a gun, appeared to have the
  strongest effects in reducing the risk of injury, though some of the findings were unstable due to
  the small numbers of sample cases." [V] (Stage 4 note, lesson 10: both sentences were previously
  given without their first halves.) Part D handles guns; this part should not.
- **Guerette and Santana, "Explaining Victim Self-Protective Behavior Effects on Crime Incident
  Outcomes"**, *Crime & Delinquency* 56(2), 2010, pp. 198-226 (online 27 June 2008, per Crossref).
  **Abstract only (Crossref).** NCVS 1992 to 2004,
  robbery and rape. "Greater levels of victim resistance increase the effort needed by offenders,
  resulting in some cases in a 93% and 92% decrease in the odds of a robbery and rape being
  completed, respectively, compared to when no resistance is used." [V] Note that this outcome is
  completion, not injury.
- **Monk, Heinonen and Eck, *Street Robbery*, Problem-Oriented Guides for Police No. 59** (ASU
  Center for Problem-Oriented Policing, 2010; read: the offender-targeting section). "using
  protective measures to resist robbery helped over half of U.S. victims in 2006, while aggravating
  the incident in less than 8 percent of cases." [V] Source is its footnote 27, a BJS figure I did
  not check. Victim self-report of "helped" is a weak measure.

**Best case in its proponents' terms**: once you account for whether injury came before or after
resistance, resistance rarely provokes injury; many attackers use force first, so non-resistance
does not buy safety; resistance of both kinds cut property loss and injury (Tark and Kleck), and
mostly forceful tactics appeared to cut injury most; greater resistance cut the odds of a robbery
being completed (Guerette and Santana). (Stage 4 note, lesson 10: previously "especially
non-forceful (running, shouting)", which is Block and Skogan's finding, not any resistance-side
source's.)

#### 3.3 What would settle it, and how a lesson should frame it

Both sides share the same weakness. **Victims choose whether to resist, and they choose in the
situations where resisting looks survivable** (unarmed robber, open space, a way out). Surveys of
survivors cannot count the dead; police records rarely record the sequence. No trial can randomise
resistance. So the classification is **contested empirical, with a working consensus on the
narrow case**: when a weapon is shown and all that is wanted is property, every official body
read says hand it over, and the resisters' own data do not contradict that. Where the attacker
wants more than property (to move you somewhere, to hurt you, to sexually assault you), the
calculation changes, and the sexual assault evidence (§4) points toward resistance. A good lesson
teaches the distinction between **a demand for property** and **an attack on the person**, and says
plainly that the law in neither country requires a victim to resist or to comply. (Stage 4 note,
lesson 10: no statute, guidance or case on a robbery victim's duties was read, so a lesson may say
only that no source this course read suggests the law anywhere the course covers requires a victim
to hand property over or to resist, with the general-information label.)

---

### 4. Sexual assault: resistance, resistance education, and the freeze

**Handling note.** This is the section most likely to hurt a reader, and the one most likely to be
read as blaming victims. Every source in favour of resistance education read here says the
opposite of blame, and the lesson must too, in the first paragraph: the only cause of a sexual
assault is the person who commits it; freezing is involuntary and common; a survivor who did not
fight did nothing wrong; the law in England and Wales requires no resistance. Put the freeze
material (§4.4) **before** the resistance evidence, not after it.

#### 4.1 Does resisting change the outcome?

- **Ullman, "Review and Critique of Empirical Studies of Rape Avoidance"**, *Criminal Justice and
  Behavior* 1997;24(2). **Abstract only (Crossref).** "Consistent evidence suggests the
  effectiveness of forceful resistance strategies for avoiding rape; however, few studies have
  analyzed resistance within the social and situational contexts of rape". It asks for data "on
  the sequential ordering of offender attack and victim resistance". [V]
- **Ullman, "A 10-Year Update of 'Review and Critique of Empirical Studies of Rape Avoidance'"**,
  *CJB* 2007. **Abstract only**; the abstract describes scope, not findings, so nothing beyond the
  scope may be cited from it.
- **Tark and Kleck, "Resisting Rape"**, *Violence Against Women* 2014. **Abstract only (Crossref).**
  NCVS 1992 to 2002, "taking into account whether harm to the victim followed or preceded
  self-protection (SP) actions." "Additional injuries besides rape, particularly serious injuries,
  following victim resistance are rare. Results indicate that most SP actions, both forceful and
  nonforceful, reduce the risk of rape completion, and do not significantly affect the risk of
  additional injury." [V]
- **Brecklin and Ullman, "Self-defense or assertiveness training and women's responses to sexual
  attacks"**, *J Interpers Violence* 2005 (abstract). N = 1,623; victims with prior training "were
  more likely to say that their resistance stopped the offender or made him less aggressive" and
  "were angrier and less scared during the incident". [V] Self-report, cross-sectional.

#### 4.2 Resistance education: the EAAA trial and its follow-ups

- **Senn and colleagues, "Efficacy of a Sexual Assault Resistance Program for University Women"**,
  *NEJM* 2015;372:2326-35. **Abstract read in full (PubMed).** First-year women at three Canadian
  universities, randomised to the Enhanced Assess, Acknowledge, Act (EAAA) programme ("four 3-hour
  units ... with the goal of being able to assess risk from acquaintances, overcome emotional
  barriers in acknowledging danger, and engage in effective verbal and physical self-defense") or
  to brochures. 451 versus 442 women. "The 1-year risk of completed rape was significantly lower in
  the resistance group than in the control group (5.2% vs. 9.8%; relative risk reduction, 46.3% [95%
  confidence interval, 6.8 to 69.1]; P=0.02). The 1-year risk of attempted rape was also
  significantly lower in the resistance group (3.4% vs. 9.3%, P<0.001)." [V] Note the wide
  confidence interval; a lesson should give both absolute risks, not just the 46%.
- **Senn and colleagues, "Secondary and 2-Year Outcomes"**, *Psychol Women Q* 2017 (PMC5821219,
  **read in full**). Reductions "between 30% and 64% at 2 years". [V] On blame: "The program makes
  clear that perpetrators are entirely responsible for the crimes they commit." Alcohol is taught
  as a risk factor, "however, it is made clear that only the presence of a man willing to commit
  sexual assault generates any 'risk' of sexual assault." [V] "sexual assault interventions for
  women must not inadvertently suggest that women are responsible for sexual assault". [V] The
  programme also reduced "woman blaming" attitudes. On the critics: "Some feminist scholars ...
  have suggested that resistance education for women is misguided, in that perpetrators may simply
  move on to another woman"; the authors respond that perpetrators "may learn important lessons",
  and concede "which explanation is correct is an empirical question". [V] Effects on completed
  rape diminished over the second year; the authors suggest boosters.
- **Senn and colleagues, "Testing the effectiveness of a sexual assault resistance programme in
  'real-world' implementation"**, *Eur J Psychotraumatol* 2023 (abstract). Five universities,
  quasi-experimental (comparison: women who signed up but did not attend). "Completed rape was
  significantly reduced by 57.3% at 6-months." [V] Weaker design than the RCT.
- **Barata and colleagues**, *J Interpers Violence* 2025 (abstract). Substudy of 153 women from the
  trial: EAAA "significantly reduced the 1-year risk of IPV by 54.4% (p = .037, 95% CI [2.9%,
  79.8%])". [V] Small and wide.
- An online version (IDEA3) is in a multisite RCT (protocol, *JMIR Res Protoc* 2025; title only).
- **CDC, *STOP SV: A Technical Package to Prevent Sexual Violence*** (Basile and colleagues, 2016,
  read from an Internet Archive capture; see §1). Lists "Empowerment-based training for women to
  reduce risk for victimization" as an approach, with EAAA as its example, and says
  "Empowerment-based approaches that focus on increasing participants' self-efficacy to identify and
  reduce exposure to risky situations and people through intensive skills training have greater
  research and theoretical support than approaches focused primarily on physical self-defense
  training." [V]

#### 4.3 Beyond the campus, and the dissent

- **Nairobi, Kenya (IMPower)**: Sarnquist and colleagues, *Pediatrics* 2014 (cohort, abstract):
  sexual assault rates fell from 17.9 to 11.1 per 100 person-years in the intervention group. [V]
  Baiocchi and colleagues, *Prev Sci* 2017 (cluster-randomised, abstract): "a 3.7 % decrease, p =
  0.03 ... (initially 7.3 % at baseline)". [V] Its conflict statement notes the instructors "and
  thus the survey interviewers, were employees of Ujamaa-Africa", the programme provider. [V]
- **The later, independent Nairobi trial found no effect.** Sarnquist and colleagues, *Prev Sci*
  2024 (abstract): 3,263 girls aged 10 to 14, 24-month follow-up, "Odds of reporting rape were not
  significantly different in the intervention versus SOC group (OR: 1.21; 95% CI (0.40, 5.21), p =
  0.63)." It "improved on previous ESD studies in this setting with longitudinal follow-up of
  individuals and independent data collection." [V] **Its protocol carries an Expression of Concern**
  (BMC Public Health 2021, read in full): survey group sizes were larger than the protocol stated,
  which "may have caused underreporting of sexual assault incidents"; the authors are split on
  it. [V] So the one trial with independent data collection is itself disputed.
- Malawi (Decker and colleagues, *BMC Public Health* 2018, abstract): past-year sexual assault
  "RR 0.68, 95% CI 0.56, 0.82". [V] South Sudan and Kakuma (Wheeler and colleagues, *J Adolesc
  Health* 2024, abstract): rape fell "from 10.7% to 5.5%" in programme schools, "no change in the
  control schools". [V] Both are implementation trials run with the programme provider. (Stage 4
  note, lesson 12: Wheeler's words are "there was no change in the control schools (10.0%-9.0%)"
  [V]; "run with the provider" rests on the author affiliations, which list Ujamaa-Pamodzi and
  Ujamaa-Africa for Decker and Ujamaa Africa for Wheeler [V]. Both Nairobi trials also ran a
  boys' programme alongside the girls'; see "Gate closures, lesson 12".)
- **Kettrey and colleagues, *J Adolesc Health* 2023;72:831-44** (meta-analysis, abstract): 80
  studies of campus programmes; "Effects on sexual assault victimization were significant but small
  (g = 0.15)", and "programs that use a risk reduction framework are associated with less favorable
  outcomes than programs that do not". [V] **Hollander, Edwards, Ullman, Senn and others replied**
  ("Empowerment Self-Defense Prevents Rape: A Response to Kettrey et al.'s Meta-Analysis", 2024),
  and Kettrey's team replied that their meta-analysis "Is Not About Empowerment Self-Defense
  Programs" (titles only, PubMed; neither letter read). The dispute is about which programmes count
  as ESD. (Stage 4 note, lesson 12: that sentence is the research file's reading of the two titles,
  not of either letter, and both titles are on the unread line: never teach it as what either side
  argues. A third comment exists, Coker and Ray (CDC), *J Adolesc Health* 74(1):210, 2024, PubMed
  38103920, metadata only, unread. Kettrey's abstract also says "effects on sexual assault
  perpetration were nonsignificant" [V], and its pool is "American college students".)
- **Hollander**, *Violence Against Women* 2014 (abstract): a 10-week university feminist
  self-defence class; participants "are less likely to experience sexual assault" over a year than
  similar women who did not take it. [V] Non-randomised. Her 2016 and 2018 reviews (abstracts)
  argue ESD is "so far the *only* approach that has produced substantively significant decreases in
  victimization rates" and that the critiques (ineffective, victim-blaming, neglects acquaintance
  assault) are "misguided". [V] (Stage 4 note, lesson 12: the 2016 abstract's list has four items,
  not three: "concerns that it is ineffective, encourages victim blaming, neglects acquaintance
  assault, and does not target the underlying factors that facilitate sexual violence" [V]. The
  fourth is the critics' value case; quote the list whole. The same abstract says ESD "helps to
  change the root conditions that allow violence against women to flourish". [V] See "Gate
  closures, lesson 12".)

**The value question underneath (3.1, category 3).** Whether prevention effort should go to
teaching potential victims to resist, or to changing perpetrators and communities, is partly a
value question. Proponents (Senn, Hollander, the CDC 2016 package) say resistance education is
the best-evidenced tool available now, alongside bystander and perpetrator work. Critics (the
position Senn reports; Kettrey and colleagues' "ecological perspective"; Basile's 2015 NEJM
editorial, unread) say it places the burden on women and may displace rather than prevent
assaults. A lesson should give both in their own terms and reach no verdict on the value part.

#### 4.4 The freeze

- **Möller, Söndergaard and Helström**, *Acta Obstet Gynecol Scand* 2017 (abstract). 298 women
  seen at a Stockholm emergency clinic within a month of rape: "70% reported significant tonic
  immobility and 48% reported extreme tonic immobility during the assault." Tonic immobility is "a
  state of involuntary, temporary motor inhibition", and was associated with later PTSD (OR 2.75)
  and severe depression. [V]
- **CPS, *Rape and Sexual Offences, Annex A: Tackling Rape Myths and Stereotypes*** (capture
  20260421173121). Lists as false: "'A real rape victim would never freeze when attacked, they would
  fight back'" and "'If the victim didn't scream, fight, or get injured then it wasn't rape'". "When
  under threat, the brain will implement instinctual survival responses that the victim will not
  necessarily have any control over ... not just fight or flight, but flop, freeze or befriend."
  "Consent and submission are different." It cites *Malone* [1998]: "No requirement that absence of
  consent has to be demonstrated or communicated to the accused." [V] This is the England and Wales
  prosecutor's position; Part D should carry the US equivalents. (Stage 4 note, lesson 11: this is the
  2021 capture; the live annex's wording and citation are in "Gate closures, lesson 11".)

---

### 5. The stress response

- **Kozlowska and colleagues, "Fear and the Defense Cascade"**, *Harv Rev Psychiatry* 2015
  (abstract). "Arousal is the first step ... flight or fight is an active defense response ...
  freezing is a flight-or-fight response put on hold; tonic immobility and collapsed immobility are
  responses of last resort to inescapable threat". [V] The best single framing for a lesson.
- **Roelofs, "Freeze for action"**, *Phil Trans R Soc B* 2017 (abstract, Crossref). "Freezing is not
  a passive state but rather a parasympathetic brake on the motor system, relevant to perception and
  action preparation." It also says human freezing's "phenomenology and neurobiological
  underpinnings remain largely unexplored". [V] Useful against the idea that freezing is failure.
- **Bracha, "Freeze, flight, fight, fright, faint"**, *CNS Spectr* 2004 (abstract): proposes that
  sequence as "a more complete description of the human acute stress response sequence". [V] A
  proposal, not a finding.
- **Taylor and colleagues, "tend-and-befriend"**, *Psychol Rev* 2000 (abstract): "Although
  fight-or-flight may characterize the primary physiological responses to stress for both males and
  females, we propose that, behaviorally, females' responses are more marked by a pattern of
  'tend-and-befriend.'" [V] A theory; teach as a proposal. (Stage 4 note, lesson 10: PubMed lists
  "Comment in Psychol Rev. 2002 Oct;109(4):745-50; discussion 751-3." The comment was not read, so
  it may not be called a critique.)
- **Artwohl, "Perceptual and Memory Distortions During Officer Involved Shootings"** (2008 update of
  her 2002 FBI LEB article, AELE workshop PDF, read in full). Survey of 157 officers after shootings:
  "diminished sound 84%", "tunnel vision 79%", "memory loss for part of the event 52%". [V]
  Voluntary written survey, collected after group debriefings, by a police psychologist; the author
  herself says "the exact percentages don't matter". Good for "your senses and memory will not
  behave normally", with those caveats. (Stage 4 note, lesson 10: the figures are officers'. Artwohl
  writes that "The same concept applies to other eyewitnesses and the suspects as well" as her view,
  without data, so carrying the figures to anyone else is labelled; her 21% for something recalled
  that had not happened is "not huge" but "certainly not trivial", never "common". The debriefing
  instruction is a design choice she defends, not a caveat she offers. See "Gate closures, lesson
  10".)
- **Training under stress**: Nieuwenhuys and Oudejans, *Cogn Process* 2011 (abstract): 27 officers;
  after training with an opponent who shot back with soap cartridges, "shot accuracy of the
  experimental group no longer deteriorated under anxiety", still present at 4 months. [V] (Stage 4
  note, lesson 12: the abstract does give a possible mechanism: "Additional analyses showed that
  these effects are potentially related to changes in visual attention on task-relevant
  information." [V] Never write that neither abstract gives one; the 2015 abstract gives none.) But
  Nieuwenhuys and colleagues, *Appl Ergon* 2015 (abstract, 57 officers): threat-induced errors in
  shoot/don't-shoot decisions "persisted" despite three weeks' practice and "may be hard to prevent
  within the limits of available practice". [V] Together: practice with an opponent who shot back
  helped accuracy hold up; three weeks of practice did not remove threat's effect on shoot
  decisions. (Stage 4 note, lesson 10: previously "stress-exposed practice helps skills more than it
  helps judgement". The 2015 abstract says only that officers "were divided over four different
  practice groups and practiced their shooting decisions for three consecutive weeks", and that the
  effects "remained equally strong for all practice groups" [V]; it does not say any group practised
  under threat, and nothing compares the size of threat's effects on skill and on judgement.)
- **Practitioner claims to handle carefully.** Grossman's *On Combat* and Siddle's heart-rate
  "conditions" (e.g., fine motor skill failing above a stated heart rate) are widely taught in
  self-defence and police training. I found no peer-reviewed validation of the specific thresholds
  and did not read either book. A lesson should not state the numbers. "Fawn", often added to
  fight-flight-freeze, comes from therapist Pete Walker's clinical writing, not from experimental
  research; "befriend" in the CPS text is the institutional version.

**Classification.** Established: acute threat produces involuntary freezing in many people,
distorts perception and memory, and degrades fine skill. Contested or proposed: the exact sequence
and names of the stages, and sex differences. Practitioner lore: heart-rate thresholds.

---

### 6. Bystander intervention

(Build on First Aid lesson 1; do not reteach Genovese, Fischer or Philpot 2020.)

- **Right To Be (formerly Hollaback!), "The 5Ds of Bystander Intervention"** (capture
  20260827212950, read in full). "Distract, Delegate, Document, Delay, and Direct". "four of them
  are indirect methods of intervention." History: "In 2012, we partnered with the bystander program
  Green Dot, which pioneered the Three Ds ... In 2015, we expanded those to include Delay, and in
  2017, we expanded them again to include Document." [V] On Document: "ALWAYS ask the person who
  was harassed what they want to do with your recording and/or notes. NEVER post it online or use it
  without their permission." On Direct: "Use this one with caution", check whether you and the
  person are physically safe, "keep it short and succinct". [V] **Neutrality flag**: on delegating to
  police the page says "you should not contact the police unless you've checked with the person
  being harassed and they've explicitly asked you to call the police", giving reasons about
  communities that "may rightfully feel unsafe in the hands of police". [V] That is an
  organisation's stated position on a contested question. A lesson should attribute it, and set it
  beside the police position (call 999 or 911 when someone is in danger) and the plain point that
  a violent assault in progress is an emergency. It must not adopt either stance in its own voice.
- **Evidence for the 5Ds**: a Right To Be press release (capture 20251205135030, read in full)
  reports a study by Germain Impact Solutions, funded by RALIANCE, in which "67% of attendees
  reporting applying the bystander intervention skills". [V] Self-reported, commissioned, no
  comparison group, report itself not read. **There is no controlled evaluation of the 5Ds that I
  found.** Teach it as a widely used practitioner framework.
- **Green Dot**: Coker and colleagues, *Am J Prev Med* 2017 (cluster RCT, 26 Kentucky high schools,
  abstract): sexual violence perpetration prevalence rate ratios "0.83 (95% CI=0.70, 0.99) in Year 3
  and 0.79 (95% CI=0.67, 0.94) in Year 4". [V] Coker and colleagues, *AJPM* 2016 (three campuses,
  non-randomised, abstract): victimisation "17% lower" on the Green Dot campus. [V] Kettrey and
  Marx, *J Youth Adolesc* 2019 (meta-analysis of 15 studies, abstract): bystander programmes have
  "a significant, desirable effect" on bystander efficacy, intentions and intervention. [V]
- **What bystanders actually do, on camera and by observation.** Levine and colleagues, *Psychological Science* 2011
  (abstract, Crossref): 42 CCTV incidents; "third parties were more likely to take conciliatory
  actions than to escalate violence", and "conciliatory behaviors are more successful when carried
  out by multiple third parties than when carried out by one person." [V] Parks and colleagues,
  *Aggressive Behavior* 2013 (abstract): 860 incidents in Toronto bars, from 503 nights of in-person
  observation, not camera footage (Stage 4 note, lesson 09); authors read it as third parties
  "responding to the perceived danger of serious harm" [V]; third parties were more
  likely to step in when aggression was more severe, and those factors predicted non-aggressive
  involvement more strongly than aggressive involvement. [V]
- **Is it dangerous to intervene?** Liebst and colleagues (PsyArXiv preprint, 2020, abstract): 93
  CCTV recordings, 417 interveners; "interveners were rarely physically harmed—at a rate of 3.6%".
  [V] Preprint; check for a published version.
- **Not every emergency is a street fight.** Lindegaard and colleagues, *J Soc Psychol*, online
  2026-09-15 (abstract): in 43 CCTV-recorded commercial robberies, bystanders helped "in 35% of the
  events", and more bystanders did not mean more help. "researchers should be wary of generalizing
  bystander intervention rates across emergency types." [V] A lesson that teaches Philpot's 90.9%
  should add this.

---

### 7. What self-defence classes can and cannot teach

**What the evidence supports.** Structured empowerment self-defence programmes (EAAA and its
relatives) reduced sexual assault in one well-run RCT with two-year follow-up and in several
provider-run trials; one independent trial found no effect. The evidence is almost entirely about
women and girls and sexual assault. **I found no controlled evidence on self-defence classes for
men, or for general assault or robbery**, which is where most men's risk lies (Part A's numbers).
Police research adds that skills learned under realistic stress hold up better under stress
(Nieuwenhuys 2011), that judgement under threat is hard to train (2015), and that gains fade
without refreshers (IMPD ICAT attitudes; EAAA's diminishing second-year effect). OSHA says
de-escalation and self-defence training "should include a hands-on component". [V]

**What a page can and cannot do.** It can teach recognition, the decision between leaving,
talking, complying and resisting, what the stress response will do to you, and how to pick a class.
It cannot teach a strike or an escape, and the course must never ask a reader to practise one on a
person.

**Choosing a class: sources.**
- **National Women's Martial Arts Federation, "How to choose a self-defense class"** (Amy Eckert,
  2025, https://nwmaf.org/nwmaf-blog/how-to-choose-a-self-defense-class/, read in full). "In a good
  empowerment self-defense program, students learn awareness, assertiveness, and verbal and physical
  skills." Look for an instructor who "Knows the facts", including that "a good class will address
  situations involving acquaintances and romantic partners as well as attacks by strangers";
  "Respects people's decisions ... and does not blame or judge survivors"; offers strategies "to
  help students make their own decisions ... instead of telling students what they should or should
  not do"; and lets students "determine their own levels of participation". Credited in part to
  the National Coalition Against Sexual Assault. [V]
- The CDC 2016 package's line that skills-based empowerment training has more support than
  "approaches focused primarily on physical self-defense training" [V] is the strongest official
  steer on class type.
- Commercial "how to choose" pages (instructor lineage, certificates, years teaching) are
  marketing by schools; they are not evidence and should not be cited.

**Course-built checklist (the course's inference from the sources above, labelled so):** covers
